Coinbase Wins Full Federal Trust Charter From OCC

📅 2026-11-09  |  Read Source →

Coinbase has won a full national trust bank charter from the Office of the Comptroller of the Currency, upgrading the conditional approval it received in April and putting its institutional custody business under direct federal supervision, the exchange said Monday.

The OCC approved the application for Coinbase National Trust Company last Friday, the company said in a blog post. OCC licensing records, as reported, show the chartering process was completed Nov. 6.

Coinbase applied in October 2025 and received preliminary approval on April 2. In the intervening months it had to finish its organizational and operational preparations before the OCC would let the trust bank open.

“This charter allows us to bring clear and consistent federal uniformity to our industry-leading custody capabilities,” Coinbase wrote, adding that it strengthens the regulatory framework under which it serves customers. The company said it has served as a custodian for more than 14 years.

A national trust bank can hold assets for clients as a fiduciary, and Coinbase’s focus is custody for institutional customers. It is not a commercial bank. It cannot take ordinary retail deposits or make loans, and the charter does not authorize it to issue stablecoins.

Staking, prime trading and financing will keep running through Coinbase affiliates, not the trust itself. Under the OCC’s April decision, the trust bank acts as an intermediary for those services and does not perform them directly.

The decision also sets conditions. The trust bank must hold at least $60 million in Tier 1 capital and enough liquid assets to cover 180 days of operating expenses during its first three years.

The license does not automatically connect Coinbase to Federal Reserve payment systems either. That requires a separate application under the Fed’s account access guidelines.

Coinbase already runs custody through Coinbase Custody Trust Company, a limited-purpose trust regulated by the New York State Department of Financial Services. The company plans to move that business to the national entity over three years.

Coinbase is the latest crypto firm to complete a federal trust charter. Ripple received final approval on Oct. 26, and Circle won final OCC approval in July, a decision that put USDC reserves on a path toward federal supervision.

The OCC has been courting the sector. In August, Comptroller Jonathan V. Gould said “America and the OCC are once again open for business,” as the regulator touted its charter push. Ripple, Circle, Crypto.com and Paxos had all received conditional approval by then. Block applied for its own banking charter in September.

The approval did not come without resistance. The Independent Community Bankers of America urged the OCC in December to reject Coinbase’s application, arguing the exchange has “demonstrably flawed risk and control functions.” The American Bankers Association asked the regulator in February to slow its review of crypto charter requests.

Underneath those objections is a turf war over stablecoins. Coinbase wants to pay customers rewards for holding the tokens, and banks say that could erode their deposit base.

Coinbase (COIN) did not disclose any financial impact from the charter in its announcement, which framed the approval as a regulatory milestone.

The timing is notable. The approval arrives days after Democrats won both chambers of Congress, and a charter issued by an executive-branch regulator sits outside any legislative fight, at least for now. Vice Chairman Ryan VanGrack said the company has spent years “asking for clear rules and rigorous oversight.” The company also said the approval is “another major step” in integrating digital assets into the American financial system.

The next test is whether Coinbase moves its existing custody clients over on schedule, and whether the community bankers who opposed the charter take their objections to court or to Congress.

51 Years Later, Jaws' Most Famous Line Is Still The Greatest Quote In Movie History

📅 2026-11-09  |  Read Source →

How many movies can you name that inspire such a massive, visceral reaction with such a minimalist approach? "Jaws" stands on an island unto itself in this regard, practically since the moment the blockbuster-defining hit first made landfall in 1975. The one-word title alone is a stroke of genius, standing in stark contrast to clunky word salads that studios tend to go with these days (usually followed by an obnoxious subtitle of some kind, naturally). John Williams' iconic score and that famous two-note theme quite literally speak for themselves, evoking a sense of dread and menace that would've been lost amid a more overbearing arrangement.

But most of all, despite including a barnburner monologue or two, let's just say there's a reason why one of the simplest and most straightforward lines of all has gone on to become arguably the most famous quote in cinema history. Of course, we're talking about actor Roy Scheider's pitch-perfect portrayal of police chief Martin Brody's moment of realization out at sea: "You're gonna need a bigger boat." Celebrated and parodied and recreated countless times since in pop culture, this one piece of dialogue towers above all the rest.

It's fun to imagine those in the theater on opening day actually feeling their moviegoing reality shift around them during this scene, just as the action in "Jaws" itself shifted into another gear entirely. The truth is, however, this was one of those singular moments in cinema that needed time and perspective to fully appreciate. It's not hyperbolic in the least to state that everything changed after this, in ways that are still reverberating today. Over 50 years later, it's as good a time as any to look back and appreciate the greatest quote of them all.

"You're gonna need a bigger boat." — Martin Brody

Have six total words ever encapsulated so many primal emotions? We're not exactly reinventing the wheel to propose that "Jaws" taps into an instinctive fear of the unknown and the horrors of nature, lurking just outside our comfortable existence. Despite how high we rise and how far our greedy ambitions take us, we're still fragile beings at the mercy of forces beyond our control — whether it's hapless swimmers trying to enjoy summer vacation, or, say, a certain mayor of Amity Island concerned solely about tourism profits over public safety. And when a killer apex predator is on the prowl, well, not even a shark hunter's boat is enough to keep us safe in unfamiliar waters.

That's the psychoanalyzing version of events, at least. While sitting down to experience "Jaws" for the umpteenth re-watch (or even for the very first time), what sticks out the most in this classic scene is just how terrified everyone is once they finally catch a glimpse of their quarry. Even Robert Shaw's cold-blooded Quint, a man who's seen and survived the worst, is visibly taken aback by the sight of a gigantic dorsal fin breaching the surface. In the moment, this isn't an existential concern about humanity's place in the food chain. This is pure survival, plain and simple.

Amusingly enough, there's another alternate explanation behind this legendary line. In a now-famous production story, it's been revealed that this was actually an improvised insult aimed squarely at penny-pinching producers. As co-writer Carl Gottlieb explained, "['You're gonna need a bigger boat'] became a catchphrase for anytime anything went wrong." With that added layer of irony, it's no wonder this quote took on a life of its own.

The Queens Public Library system is being held for ransom

📅 2026-11-09  |  Read Source →

In a bizarre turn of events, on Wednesday November 4th, New York City’s Queens Public Library (QPL)  system (which operates 66 branches), experienced an attack on its online networks by a hacker. The entire system is offline, and is being held for ransom.

According to the QPL, all branches remain open, but online networks are down, resulting in disruptions to the library website, on-site computers, check-out and return machines, wi-fi, the library’s mobile app, and all digital media services.

It has not been disclosed what or how much the hackers are asking in return for releasing the system. It is also unknown who is behind the ransomware attack.

QPL CEO Dennis Walcott told Gothamist, “Unfortunately, it’s part of the world that we live in right now. So the Queens Public Library is a victim of the type of world that we’re living in, where people are launching cyber attacks against various types of entities, large corporations, and now Queens Public Library.”

His remark highlights the absolute strangeness of the events… the QPL is hardly a “large corporation.” In fact, it’s a very small nonprofit. It’s not even New York’s largest library system. The NYPL, which services Manhattan, the Bronx, and Staten Island, operates 92 locations. (For those wondering, the Brooklyn Public Library system operates 62 branches.)

But also… it’s a library system! It’s whole business is to provide widespread access to books, additional literary and media resources, internet access, printing services, education opportunities, and community and children’s programming to people. FOR FREE. What the hell type of jerk goes after a library system, especially a library system in one of the most ethnically and linguistically diverse areas in the world? (As a Queens native and huge QPL fan, but also just a person who thinks books are good, I’m furious.)

Walcott also noted that it is currently unknown if user information has been compromised.

However, QPL users can still check books in and out manually (it is a library, after all). So, analog tech has saved the day.

Stay tuned for more updates, and long live libraries!

Art, syrup, a legendary stone: Maxie Dara’s 3 favorite heists of the last century

📅 2026-11-09  |  Read Source →

Crime is a subject often synonymous with tragedy, with strife, with human suffering. But sometimes it’s also a little goofy.

I spent my first two books, A Grim Reaper’s Guide to Catching a Killer and A Grim Reaper’s Guide to Cheating Death, focused on the murder of it all. My search history probably has me on at least one watch list and I can give you a handful of creative ways to kill somebody with a glitter pen if you ask nicely.

But for my third dive back into my world of death with A Grim Reaper’s Guide to Living Twice, I decided to play against type and go for a silly little heist instead. And it turns out I’m not alone. It fact, it turns out recent history is full of silly little heists that take the concept of a crime and turn it into a certified shenanigan. From student hijinks to condiment capers, here are my top three favorite heists of the last century.

Come with me to my home nation of Canada, to my birth province of Quebec, where poutine is plentiful and everybody’s at least a little disappointed in your lack of French fluency. It also happens to be a hub of maple syrup production, though that feels like something of an understatement. According to The Canadian Encyclopedia, “In 2020, Quebec contributed 96.4 per cent of Canadian maple product exports.”

With all that sweet tree nectar flowing, it may come as no surprise to you (though it genuinely did to me) to learn that the province keeps a syrup stockpile in the form of the Strategic Maple Syrup Reserve. Think of it as a sort of Fort Knox, but instead of gold it’s filled with pancake toppings, which is way more appealing in my opinion. And I’m not the only one who thinks so.

Between 2011 and 2012, a group of sticky-fingered (sorry) Reserve employees decided to help themselves to the stock in order to sell it to other provinces and states. This slow-motion heist involved months of siphoning maple syrup out of heavy barrels. The thieves would then either replace their loot with water or leave the barrels empty. Unfortunately for the robbers, there’s a bit of a difference between an empty barrel and one filled with syrup—a six-hundred-pound difference, in fact.

When an inspector for the organization in charge of the Reserve tried to climb up a stack of these supposedly heavy barrels only for one to nearly topple under his weight, he became just a smidge suspicious. Sure enough, a subsequent investigation found that around three thousand tonnes of maple syrup had been stolen, its value a staggering $18 million.

The investigation also uncovered a crime ring comprised of syrup industry insiders, from truck drivers to sugar shack workers, who had rented a separate space in the same warehouse as the Reserve so they could perpetrate their crimes unnoticed. Though my favourite fact about this entire case is that a maple syrup black market exists, and the ringleader of the operation, Richard Vallières, had already been a part of it long before this heist. Some criminal underworlds just sound more delicious than others, you know?

Kids these days, with their music and their haircuts and their schemes to steal back ancient symbols of Scottish heritage.

It’s Christmas in 1950 and four Glasgow University students have decided to crank their youthful rebellion up a notch. While other twenty-somethings are sleeping off hangovers or opening presents with their families, Ian Hamilton, Gavin Vernon, Kay Matheson and Alan Stuart are plotting to retrieve the Stone of Scone.

Also known as the Stone of Destiny, the roughly three-hundred-and-thirty-six-pound block of red sandstone had been an important part of Scottish coronations for hundred of years before King Edward I stole it for England during the 13th century. During its long history the stone was imbued with rich legends, connecting its heritage all the way back to the first king of Scotland and making it an important national symbol. South of the border it continued to be used in coronations, this time siting beneath each English monarch as they were crowned, but its importance to Scotland remained.

Centuries after its initial theft by King Edward I, the four plucky students decided to reclaim the stone in an act of national pride. The group drove south and waited until midnight to steal the Stone of Scone from where it now lay in Westminster Abbey.

With Kay on guard duty, the three men tried to hoist the stone to freedom, but it broke in two during their attempt. Ian took the smaller piece while Gavin and Alan took the larger and fled in a separate car. Back with Kay, Ian soon found himself in the crosshairs of a passing officer, but in an Ocean’s Eleven-like twist, the students disguised their heist as a youthful makeout session and managed to get away.

The story only gets more wild from there, with the stone halves finding themselves hidden under floorboards and buried under a campsite before they were eventually reunited. And while it did end up returning to England after the students were caught, the Stone of Scone was given back to Scotland in 1996, and none of the young thieves faced legal consequences for their actions.

The Louvre is no stranger to an entertaining heist. In 1911, a man called Vincenzo Peruggia dressed as a museum worker and snatched the Mona Lisa in broad daylight. Unfortunately for the Louvre but very fortunately for schadenfreude fans everywhere, the museum art minister was on holiday at the time and before he left he had apparently insisted, “don’t call me unless the Louvre burns down or the Joconde [French for Mona Lisa] is stolen.”

Fast forward just shy of a hundred years and this same level of absolute tomfoolery was on display again. In 2025, two men dressed as construction workers, hoisted themselves up to a museum balcony using a furniture lift and stole €88 million worth of French Crown Jewels in under eight minutes. Though the culprits were eventually caught, the heist will live on in infamy for both its devious efficiency and the frankly top tier memes it inspired.

Maxie Dara is a writer and actor from Ontario, Canada. She has been a freelance journalist focusing on the local arts and culture scene for more than five years, with bylines in publications such as Hamilton Magazine and Beyond James, among others. She is also a two-time award-winning playwright, taking home the Best of Fringe award at the 2017 Hamilton Fringe Festival for the musical comedy This Is Not a Musical: The Musical! and the 2020 Torpedo Prize for her play Alone Together, a pandemic drama. Maxie knew she wanted to be a writer at the age of seven, when she first fell in love with the written word. She also wanted to be a mermaid but has mostly focused on the writing side of things.

5 mysteries that explore the darker sides of Dublin

📅 2026-11-09  |  Read Source →

Dublin is a city of many words. Of Nobel prizewinners and pub storytellers, never short of a thing or two to say. From the calligraphy of the Book of Kells to the modernism of James Joyce to the intricate interior worlds of Sally Rooney, Dublin’s literary heritage is long established.

But there’s a darker side to this city, a corrupt underbelly of money and power, a rich history of villains and anti-heroes. Here are some of my favorites.

From the queen of Dublin noir, this is a tense, atmospheric investigation into the murder of a family on one of Dublin’s ghost estates in the aftermath of the 2008 financial crash. Brianstown is built on the ruins of a faded seaside resort and it embodies all of the isolation and desperation so prevalent at the time.

It’s bleak and brutal and a searing indictment of a government that put the survival of its banks ahead of the welfare of its people. It’s also a grueling mystery that plays out to the very end.

This compelling exploration of the 1982 murders committed by Dublin socialite Malcolm Macarthur is both true crime and a fascinating dive into the mind of a convicted killer. After Macarthur was arrested at the then Attorney General’s home, an event that almost brought down the government, he spent forty years in prison.

On his release, O’Connell approached him in the street to talk about his life. The result is an insightful and unsettling study of a “highborn savage” so convinced of his own superiority that he will go to any lengths to blur the lines between truth and invention. As a bonus, The Book of Evidence by John Banville, shortlisted for the 1989 Booker Prize, is inspired by Macarthur’s story.

A whydunit it more than a whodunit, this is a masterful exploration of the motivation and morals of a group of private-school teenagers. When Conor Harris is kicked to death by three of his peers, the Dublin establishment is forced to look beyond its veneer of respectability for answers. A connected yet distant narrator deconstructs institutional entitlement, broken family dynamics and the moral decay that can hide behind money, old and new.

Inspired by real events, Bad Day in Blackrock is a scathing yet sometimes tender glimpse of a section of Dublin society still getting used to its newfound Celtic Tiger wealth.

Andrew Hughes, The Convictions of John Delahunt

This chilling historical thriller is based on the true story of a notorious Victorian murderer. When a lackluster Trinity student becomes a police informant, it’s not long before he’s looking for scapegoats for his own crimes. Delahunt starts to fabricate conspiracies and frame innocent people to claim government bounties until his greed and escalating moral decay culminates in a shocking, cold-blooded murder that scandalizes the entire nation.

Set among the taverns, tenements and alleyways of 1840s Dublin, and courtrooms that dole out their own twisted justice, this is an engrossing and vivid portrait of a city as sinister and corrupt as its amoral protagonist.

And if I may be so bold… In 1980s Dublin, outsider Lou Manson enrolls at the prestigious Highfield Manor to try and expose the school’s dark secret. But she soon discovers that the Highfield elite will go to any lengths to protect their own reputation—even when the consequences are fatal.

When We Were Silent is a propulsive and fiercely feminist exploration of power, corruption and retribution.

Fiona McPhillips is an Irish journalist, author, and screenwriter. In 2019, she started an MA in Creative Writing at Dublin City University, graduating with first class honours. Fiona's debut novel, When We Were Silent, was runner-up for the 2021 Crime Writers Association Debut Dagger. Fiona lives in Dublin with her three kids, two cats, and a dog.

PSV back on top of Eredivisie as Feyenoord slump in Heerenveen

📅 2026-11-09  |  Read Source →

See more DutchNews articles in your Google search results

See more DutchNews articles in your Google search results

PSV returned to the top of the Eredivisie with a thumping 5-2 win over Cambuur Leeuwarden as previous leaders Feyenoord lost a seven-goal thriller against the other Frisian club, Heerenveen.

It took just 46 seconds for PSV to get off the mark as Sven Mijnans headed in unmarked.

The midfielder added a second after 15 minutes and completed his hat-trick with a free-kick in the 80th minute to make the score 4-2, just as Cambuur were threatening a comeback.

Feyenoord’s dip in form continued as they let a two-goal lead slip at Heerenveen. The Rotterdammers looked to be in control going into the break with a trademark drilled shot by Anis Hadj Moussa and a penalty by Nacho Ferri.

But Maas Willemsen bundled in the ball from a corner on the stroke of half-time, before Ringo Meerveld came on for the second half and scored a hat-trick as Feyenoord’s defence crumbled under pressure.

“In the matches when we played well I saw a team that fought for each other,” Feyenoord coach Giovanni van Bronckhorst said after the game. “Mentally strong, kept running even when we didn’t have the ball. That’s our strength. When we don’t do that we’re a lesser team.”

Mexx Meerdink scored a hotly contested late penalty to settle a tempestuous game in Alkmaar as AZ came back from 3-1 down against Groningen to claim their first win in five matches.

Goalkeeper Etienne Vaessen was given a second yellow card for protesting too hard against the decision to penalise him for upending Ayoub Oufkir. Once the commotion died down Meerdink, who had missed from the spot against Vaessen earlier in the second half, made no mistake against substitute goalkeeper Lovro Stubljar.

Ajax missed the chance to take over top spot at Twente as their former striker Wout Weghorst scored a last-minute penalty to rescue a point for the home side.

Ajax had been 2-0 up with a bullet header by their giant striker Tolu Arokodare and a long-range strike by Rayane Bounida, before an own goal by Dies Janse brought the visitors back into the game.

Fortuna Sittard also had to settle for a point against Telstar after going 2-0 up thanks to a bizarre own goal by Sem Valk. The defender’s sliced clearance looped over the head of Ronald Koeman Junior as the goalkeeper tried to head it away rather than risk being penalised for handling a backpass.

Goals just before half-time by Abdelnour Soualhia and Patrick Brouwer brought Telstar back into the game, but neither side was able to find a winner after the break.

That result allowed ADO, under the guidance of interim coach Levi Schwiebbe, to close the gap on 16th-placed Telstar to one point with a 3-1 win against Sparta, while bottom Willem II collected their third point of the season at home to PEC Zwolle.

7 novels where Americans in Paris solve mysteries

📅 2026-11-09  |  Read Source →

“Paris is so very beautiful that it satisfies something in you that is always hungry in America,” wrote Ernest Hemingway in a letter to his mother. The pull to Paris from across the ocean has always been strong. Since the 1800s Americans have traveled to the City of Light in search of artistic, intellectual, and personal freedom. They found better (but not perfect) racial equality, the mental and physical space to write or paint, affordable rent (in the good old days), cheap red wine, ethereal croissants, and very often romance.

The experiences of real-life Americans like Hemingway, Josephine Baker, Julia Child, and James Baldwin inspired a trove of fictional stories. In books, movies, and TV series these characters’ storylines often followed a similar trajectory. Once in Paris the Americans became more sophisticated, more assured, more themselves. Think of Frank Mulligan, Gene Kelly’s character in An American in Paris, Audrey Hepburn’s Sabrina, or the titular Emily in the Netflix hit Emily in Paris.

In the popular imagination, Paris is a place of joie de vivre, of pleasure. But, the rose-colored glasses through which we Americans often view the city also makes Paris an ideal setting for mysteries and thrillers. Who doesn’t love experiencing the frisson of danger lurking behind a pretty façade?

The main character in my new mystery novel The Foreign Correspondent’s Wife finds that very darkness in the City of Light. It’s 1949, and Kat Kelly is a whip-smart reporter who’s run out of the profession when men return home from the war. She comes to Paris with Charlie, her foreign correspondent husband, and enrolls in culinary school, eager to make a fresh start. But when one of her classmates, a former GI, is brutally murdered, she becomes obsessed with solving the crime, soon suspecting that the killing has its roots in the Nazi Occupation of Paris during World War II.

Like Kat, most American characters in mysteries or thrillers set in Paris have much to learn from the city. But, often, they bring a uniquely American perspective to whatever shenanigans they find themselves embroiled in—a can-do attitude, a refusal to take no for an answer, a moxie that makes them the ideal person to ferret out the truth.

Kat and Charlie join a long line of Americans starring in mysteries and thrillers set in Paris. Here are a few of my favorites.

No, this 2026 novel isn’t about the 2025 Louvre heist. But what timing! Piazza’s most recent page-turner features dual timelines, both revolving around the art of Vincent Van Gogh.

In the first, set in the 1990s, Emma is an American art student forced to clean apartments when her École des Beaux-Arts scholarship is canceled. Soon she gets drawn into the mysterious world of her employer, the disinherited matriarch of a stupendously rich—and potentially criminal—family of art dealers.

In the second, we follow Jo Van Gogh, the painter’s real-life sister-in-law as she struggles to build a market for Vincent’s work after his death. The stories braid together beautifully, and with fraud, assault, a soupçon of romance, and of course a heist, this one’s a real work of art.

The Maggie Hope series is anchored in World War II-era Britain, but in the seventh installment, the Massachusetts native finds herself in occupied Paris on a dangerous mission. The mathematician-turned-spy must discover the fate of both a fellow agent and her half-sister.

The narrative is teeming with fascinating historical details and real-life characters, but it never feels heavy-handed.The Paris Spy is a smart, fast-paced story that’s full of both action and heart.

The second entry in Pavone’s Kate Moore series finds Kate living in Paris with her family. She’s overseeing a sleepy CIA substation, attempting to shore up her flailing marriage, and planning dinner parties in an effort to fit in better with the other international moms.

When seemingly unrelated incidents flare up across the city—a bomb threat at a train station, a suicide bomber near the Louvre, an American billionaire spirited out of his apartment—Kate realizes that everything is connected and she may be the only person who can unravel the plot before it’s too late.

Packed with cameos from the city’s landmarks and biting asides, The Paris Diversion is the rare espionage thriller that’s as witty as it is twisty.

Mark Pryor, The Most Mysterious Bookshop in Paris

When Pryor launched the Hugo Marston series in 2012 with The Bookseller, his main character and our de facto detective was head of security at the U.S. Embassy in Paris. When his friend, an antiquarian bookseller, is kidnapped, Marston can’t help but pursue the case.

Nine books later and Marston has retired from the embassy to open a bookshop of his own. But when blackmail and murder come for a high-end chocolate maker located in an ancient Parisian chateau with a troubled history, Marston is drawn back into the city’s dark web of secrets.

While Black’s popular Aimée Leduc series wouldn’t make this list—Aimée may live in Paris, but she’s not a Yank—this spy thriller does. Set during World War II, Three Hours in Paris features Kate Rees, an American markswoman who is dropped into the Nazi-occupied French capital by the Brits with an assignment to assassinate Adolf Hitler on his visit.

When the plan falls apart, Kate must evade capture in a city teeming with danger while trying to figure out who betrayed her.

Colleen Cambridge, In the Spirit of French Murder

The fourth book in this post-World War II cozy mystery series is a gem. It finds American Tabitha Knight once again investigating a murder alongside her larger-than-life, food-obsessed pal Julia Child. This time, the victim is an elderly man who’s friends with Tabitha’s grandfather and his partner.

Eerie encounters with a medium, forays into dangerous parts of the city, and mouthwatering culinary descriptions keep this clever confection of a book moving at a brisk, entertaining clip.

Okay, this is cheating because the characters in this page-turner are Canadian, so North-American, but it’s too much of a favorite to omit. Number 16 in the Chief Inspector Armand Gamache series, which is mostly set in Quebec, this installment finds Gamache and his family in Paris on vacation. When his billionaire godfather is struck and gravely injured in a hit and run, Gamache realizes it was no accident.

The act of violence sparks a ticking clock mystery that sets the chief inspector and his son-in-law (and former deputy) Jean-Guy Beauvoir criss-crossing the city, unsure whom to trust and desperate to uncover the truth before it’s too late for their family.

Jenna Helwig is the author of The Foreign Correspondent, the food director at Real Simple, a five-time cookbook author, a culinary school graduate, and the creator of the Cookbookery Collective Substack. When she isn’t cooking or writing, she’s likely reading mystery novels, watching baseball, or planning her next travels. Jenna lives in Brooklyn, New York, with her family and adorable dog Pepper.

The New Moon in Scorpio Has Arrived—Here’s What It Means for Every Star Sign

📅 2026-11-09  |  Read Source →

The New Moon in Scorpio occurs on November 9. The second New Moon of autumn marks the start of a fresh lunar cycle, calling on us to manifest with intention. It is a moment for deep contemplation and renewal, anchored in Scorpio’s penetrating, introspective terrain. Step by step, we are learning to step into our power by honoring our most authentic, self-sufficient selves—regardless of the roadblocks standing in our way.

“The New Moon in Scorpio represents an intimate kind of beginning,” Bri Luna, founder of The Hoodwitch and author of Sex Magic For Dummies, explains. “Scorpio brings us into contact with sex, death, sorrow, and the appetites we keep private. Under this Moon, we may recognize how much of ourselves we’ve concealed to preserve a relationship or hold on to a life we’ve outgrown. There can be grief in admitting that, but also a renewed thirst for living. We can still want something deeply, even after disappointment.”

Internally, we are called to reshape and revitalize our daily lives. The urge to evolve into a more grounded, radiant version of ourselves is palpable. The rebirth we are undergoing invites us to shed old layers: like the metamorphosis from chrysalis to butterfly, real transformation takes patience before we can truly take flight.

Luna adds: “Desire is an important part of my magical practice, and something I explore in my new book. Working with pleasure and sexual energy through ritual asks us to understand what we’re calling into our lives and why. For this New Moon, I would begin with the desires you keep editing before you’ve even allowed yourself to name them. Give yourself some privacy, tell yourself the truth, and consider what honoring that urge would ask of you.”

Action-oriented Mars, the traditional ruler of Scorpio, squares this New Moon. The bold fire of Mars in Leo pushes us to take decisive steps toward fulfilling our longings. The catch? Mars entered its pre-retrograde shadow on November 5 (gearing up to station retrograde on January 10), which means forward momentum may feel sluggish. We are also navigating five concurrent planetary retrogrades: Mercury in Scorpio clouding choices, Venus in Libra recalibrating relationships, Saturn in Aries testing commitments, Uranus in Gemini upending collective viewpoints, and Neptune in Aries reframing our ideals. Hesitation and fog are natural under this sky, but they provide fertile ground to pause and reassess. Resist the urge to rush. Meditate on what your spirit actually needs by tuning into gut-level intuition.

Read your New Moon in Scorpio horoscope below.

Living well remains the best revenge, especially when you have no interest in petty disputes. If you find yourself watching karmic loops play out for others, there’s no need to get your hands dirty. What goes around comes around—step back, protect your peace, and focus on your own lane.

People are naturally drawn to your warmth and charm right now. With the lunation stirring up connections from the past, present, and future, your instinct is to nurture genuine camaraderie. Just make sure you aren't spreading your emotional bandwidth too thin; be discerning about who gets a front-row seat to your life.

The energy you’ve been pouring into others needs to be directed back inward. Before comparing your progress to what everyone else is doing, reclaim your focus. Clarify your own immediate priorities and creative sparks—once you reconnect with what drives you, you’ll move forward without second-guessing yourself.

Vulnerability can feel daunting, especially when scars from past connections linger. While defining a partnership or opening up emotionally might bring up old anxieties, remember that every dynamic is unique. Don’t let former heartaches dictate current possibilities; take intimacy at a pace that feels genuinely safe.

Retreating to your sanctuary is the best way to ride out external noise until the dust settles. You’re in the mood to guard your energy and keep your inner circle close and calm. Filter out anything that drains your vitality, prioritizing quiet restorative rituals that reinforce your peace of mind.

Conversations may feel unusually fraught or prone to misinterpretation right now. If others are projecting their stress or deflecting accountability, verbal sparring won’t help. Opt for clarity, brevity, and space—sometimes putting your thoughts into a carefully considered note is far more effective than an off-the-cuff debate.

Reframing your financial habits is front and center under this lunation. Stepping away from a scarcity mindset is the first step toward unlocking sustainable earning power and security. Transforming how you manage and value your resources takes time; don’t hesitate to seek sound advice from trusted professionals to sharpen your strategy.

We are all creatures of habit—even you. Take an honest look at recurring patterns that have outlived their purpose. Breaking generational or personal cycles can feel intense, but it is deeply liberating. Use this New Moon in your sign to take that first decisive step toward personal reinvention; real momentum will follow.

A sudden moment of clarity may reveal someone’s true motives, dislodging assumptions you’ve held for a while. If you previously gave people the benefit of the doubt at the expense of your gut instincts, don’t dwell on regret. Take the revelation as valuable data to better curate who earned your trust moving forward.

Be selective about the alliances you form right now. Aligning with colleagues or partners who aren’t on your wavelength will only complicate your path, particularly if conflicting agendas create unnecessary friction. Do your due diligence and ensure that potential collaborators are as dependable, honest, and hardworking as you are.

Clarifying long-term career benchmarks may feel like a moving target amid so much retrograde motion. The secret is not to lose faith in your underlying vision just because the timeline shifts. Stay committed to your craft; opportunities often resurface in unexpected and far more fitting ways once the celestial fog clears.

Releasing rigid expectations opens the door to expansive intellectual and philosophical shifts. You might find yourself revisiting core beliefs from earlier in life with a fresh, more forgiving perspective. True growth requires staying adaptable rather than digging your heels in—there is power in doing a complete 180 when new insight demands it.

“I’m really upset” – One thing hugely annoyed Andoni Iraola despite Liverpool win on Sunday

📅 2026-11-09  |  Read Source →

Andoni Iraola was left ‘really upset’ after Liverpool’s win over Crystal Palace on Sunday despite the positive, morale-boosting result for his team.

Having begun November with defeats to Arsenal and Fenerbahce, the Reds went to Selhurst Park in search of a pick-me-up in their final game prior to the international window, and they duly got in the form of a 3-1 victory.

Goals from Alexis Mac Allister, Florian Wirtz and Alexander Isak had the points secured well before a stoppage-time penalty when, in a clash of heads between Milos Kerkez and Dario Osorio, the Hungarian was deemed to be the perpetrator and referee Tony Harrington pointed to the spot.

Eddie Nketiah converted from 12 yards to deny Liverpool a fifth Premier League clean sheet of the campaign, and speaking to Sky Sports shortly after the final whistle, Iraola’s frustration over the awarding of the penalty was clear.

The Basque said: “I’m very upset [with] what they are calling in our box penalties, and the ones they’re not calling in the other box. I know that we are 3-0, it’s the 95th minute, but I’m very upset.

“It’s a head-to-head but even Osorio’s head, after touching the ball, is the one going to Milos. It’s not Milos to Osorio. A clash of heads.

“OK, you can say 3-0, 95 minutes, nothing happens, but for me there is more than this. There is more because it’s not the first time this season and you compare and I’m really upset, yes.”

Want more Empire of the Kop coverage? Add us as a preferred source on Google to your favourites list for news you can trust

Thankfully the decision came at a point in the match where it had no bearing on the overall result, although we can still see why it put a dampener on the victory for Iraola.

To us, it seemed like a 50-50 contest between Kerkez and Osorio, with both players making a genuine attempt to win the ball and unfortunate to have clashed heads – most importantly, both were fine after the collision.

Harrington’s call didn’t have any major impact in the overall context of the day, but we take the Liverpool head coach’s point about the apparent inconsistency in terms of what referees deem to be a penalty and what they don’t.

There was certainly a far stronger case for a spot kick in our defeat to Arsenal last weekend when Jurrien Timber blatantly tugged Isak’s shirt inside the box, but somehow none of the officials on duty thought it was a foul.

It didn’t hurt the Reds yesterday, but hopefully on another occasion where there’s a stoppage-time penalty given in a game which is in the balance, we won’t end up having to talk about Iraola’s side being wronged by the referee and VAR.

10 must-try techniques for creating environment art that drives visual storytelling

📅 2026-11-09  |  Read Source →

Join The Club for quick access. Enter your email below and we'll send confirmation, and sign you up to our newsletter.

Sign up to Creative Bloq's daily newsletter, which brings you the latest news and inspiration from the worlds of art, design and technology.

Get full access to premium articles, exclusive features and a growing list of member rewards.

An effective environment is about much more than spectacular landscape.

When you purchase through links on our site, we may earn an affiliate commission. Here’s how it works.

I’ve learned that an impactful environment is about more than just a beautiful image or a spectacular landscape: it acts as a character in its own right, capable of conveying emotion all on its own. Having started my career as a concept artist, I realised that environments don’t only serve as background for characters – they're a powerful narrative tool.

All visual elements I design must serve the story being told by revealing the stakes, the history or the identity of the location. Before I even begin sketching, I always ask myself: “What happened here?” This helps me define my initial narrative intent. Every visual choice made is as much about building an atmosphere as it is about solving challenges related to composition, readability, or credibility.

I like to think that my images spark the viewer’s curiosity, inviting them to form their own interpretation of the scene. Through elements like composition, lighting or colour, a simple environment can tell its own story, inviting the viewer to imagine what took place there.

Below, I’ll share some of the key techniques I use daily to bring environments to life with a strong identity and visual storytelling. If you need to update your tools, see our guides to the best best drawing tablets and the best laptops for drawing.

Before I even begin drawing or creating my initial sketches, I always dedicate time to researching all the references needed for my project. I build a library of reference images based on real-world elements such as landscape photographs, architectural details, traditional western paintings, nature, historical documents, animals, objects, etc.

The world around us is an endless source of inspiration! That is also why I enjoy observational drawing and studying my subjects before creating a unique image from my imagination. I might do this using traditional media directly in my sketchbook or digitally; I like to vary my techniques

I always try to give each environment its own distinct personality and identity. It is this identity that guides my artistic choices and shapes every design decision I make after that. An environment that feels like part of a much larger universe is always more impactful and credible.

To achieve this, I always consider architectural elements, the location’s history, culture, climate and technology, along with anything else that helps build a world the viewer wants to explore. The real challenge is successfully turning this environment into a veritable character in its own right within the story.

During my creative process, I often explore silhouettes and shapes before diving into the details. This allows me to focus on the overall forms and define the primary forms. Finding strong, impactful silhouettes, even from a distance, ensures the design is instantly recognisable and readable.

This might take the form of quick sketches of various architectural or natural elements. My goal here is to explore multiple possibilities and generate as many iterations as possible to find an interesting design. The silhouettes need to be expressive and recognisable at a glance.

Light is a powerful compositional tool; it helps convey a clear intent and guides the viewer’s eye. I always strive to craft the lighting to create an interesting focal point within the image. Carefully designed lighting can transform an ordinary setting into a memorable scene that evokes a specific emotion.

I often use light as a narrative element to suggest the time of day, the atmosphere or a particular emotion; I view it as a true emotional language. Strong contrast around the main subject immediately draws the eye. Conversely, softer areas allow the viewer’s gaze to rest.

The design phase is probably the most important, which is why, when I have more complex designs to create, I often proceed with a more precise outline to establish a truly solid foundation. This allows me to resolve any challenges that arise when conceptualising a specific environment or architectural style.

I start by establishing the main shapes, then gradually work in the details. It is a decisive step for the rest of the project as during this stage I determine the function of my various designs. Using this process is common in video games.

To bring my environments to life, I enjoy playing with proportions and varying scales. Pairing small elements with gigantic structures instantly captures the viewer’s attention and lends the image an almost mythological aspect.

This dramatic contrast in scale also allows me to create compositions that reinforce this sense of exploration and travel. These contrasts further amplify the feeling of wonder. I love the idea of the viewer imagining themselves exploring these places, much like they would in a video game, for instance

How to draw convincing buildings using real-world references

After gathering photo references of architectural elements for this world, I begin working on the main masses. 3D can be a great tool for laying out initial, very simple shapes made up of primitives like cubes or cylinders. I model my 3D base using Blender.

Sign up to Creative Bloq's daily newsletter, which brings you the latest news and inspiration from the worlds of art, design and technology.

Once I’ve blocked in the main shapes, I start drawing over them with more precise line work. This allows me to refine the designs and add small details like decorations, props, vegetation, etc. This line work serves as a solid foundation for moving on to colour.

For the final stage, I focus on colours and textures. I aim to achieve a pleasing colour harmony and bring the buildings to life by adding details that suggest imperfection: signs of wear, dirt and stains, etc. I wanted to depict a peaceful and unique place.

Colours define the mood, but they also convey emotion. I love using vibrant shades and harmonies that evoke a sense of wonder. Nature is my primary source of inspiration, though I also draw from the worlds of Studio Ghibli and the work of great western painters.

I view colour as a tool that lends a unique feel to my images and serves as a key narrative element. Much like light, colour allows me to highlight and draw attention to a crucial element within the image.

When creating an illustration, I always strive to ensure the atmosphere aligns with the emotion or story I wish to convey. I place great importance on crafting a unique atmosphere that transforms the setting into a true protagonist of the story being told.

Mystery is also a powerful narrative device; it is an element I love to incorporate into my images because it allows the viewer’s imagination and interpretation to run free. Lighting, composition, the depth of the various planes and colour harmony all play a role in creating this feeling and bringing it to life.

Over the years, I’ve had the opportunity to experiment with various techniques and acquire a range of skills, allowing me to adapt to different media and graphic styles. In a constantly changing world, adaptability is a valuable asset. I strongly encourage exploring different mediums.

Learning tools like 3D software (specifically Blender) proved to be a real asset early in my career. It’s always a great way to satisfy one’s curiosity and challenge oneself. However, I believe the tool itself shouldn’t become the ultimate goal, as it’s easy to get lost in the process.

Joris is an environment concept artist and illustrator working mainly in video games and animation. He loves creating stories and building new worlds.

‘Not Your Daddy’s 22.’ This Is The Best 22 Creedmoor Ammo for Deer Hunting

📅 2026-11-09  |  Read Source →

Published Nov 9, 2026 9:00 AM EST

We may earn revenue from the products available on this page and participate in affiliate programs. Learn More ›

The latest for gun hunters and competitive shooters.

New cartridges like the 22 Creedmoor and 22 ARC have been developed alongside the modern practice of leveraging heavy-for-caliber bullets into better downrange performance and, as it turns out, the 22 Creedmoor is a stone-cold deer killer. There are still relatively limited factory ammo options compared to legacy cartridges, and if you’re looking for the best 22 Creedmoor ammo for deer specifically, there are a couple of important considerations. Here’s a look at five of the solid options we have tested for accuracy and velocity, using the bullets that have killed deer and other big game with. Being that Hornady brought the cartridge to SAAMI for standardization, they have the widest variety of factory ammo, but there are other good options too.

People get a lot of big feelings when you broach the subject of shooting big-game animals with .22-caliber rifles, so it’s important to talk about what we mean, and what we don’t. There have undoubtedly been thousands of deer killed with various varmint bullets and .22 rimfire cartridges with a wide variety of results.

That’s not what we’re talking about here.

As numerous insightful old timers discovered by loading cartridges like the .220 Swift, .22/250 Remington, and .223 Remington with more purposeful bullets — sometimes requiring tighter twist barrels — .22-caliber centerfires kill deer very effectively.

The 22 Creedmoor, with the same bullets and barrel length, will leave even the great Swift in the dust, and it’s specifically designed to work best with modern, long, high-BC bullets that maintain their speed and terminal effectiveness hundreds of yards beyond what traditional 40- to 60-grain .224 bullets could. These bullets are perfectly capable of delivering decisive penetration and trauma on any reasonable shot at a deer.

There are a couple important things to consider when picking the best 22 Creedmoor ammo for deer. First is bullet weight and construction. There isn’t currently a plethora of 22 Creed factory ammo on the market, but it’s wise to avoid the varmint-specific loads. Many varmint bullets are designed specifically to be fragile. At some of the velocities you’ll see from these 22 Creed varmint loads, the volatile bullets can sometimes not penetrate well.

An example of this is Hornady’s 62-grain ELD-VT, launched with the 22 ARC in mind. According to Hornady their shooters, after killing hundreds of coyotes, noticed that they saw a higher percentage of them running after being hit when the 62-grain ELD-VT was used in the 22 CM than when it was launched slightly slower out of the ARC. The problem was remedied with a slightly heavier 69-grain slug. Deer aren’t exceptionally big or dense animals, and there are other ammo options that will work well.

The other consideration isn’t as straightforward, and is one significant drawback of the 22 Creedmoor. Its standardized twist rate of 1:8 accommodates those more fragile bullets at high velocities while generally stabilizing the heavy-for-caliber 80-plus-grain bullets — but not always. At low elevations and in cold weather, your bullets can lose stability, and your accuracy can go to hell completely.

I first noticed this while shooting at about 28 degrees Fahrenheit and 1,000 feet of elevation. This barrel consistently printed 20-shot samples under an inch with the 80-grain ELD-X, but, beyond 600 yards, my bullets were dropping way more than they had been doing a few days prior when it was warmer. Later, at 0 degrees Fahrenheit, groups at 100 yards opened up to nearly 6 inches with the same bullets. I’ve documented this instability, which varies from barrel to barrel, in several guns now.

The takeaway is that if you are going to be hunting late season at low elevation in cold temperatures, either choose a bullet that will work reliably in a 1:8-twist barrel, or at least test your rifle and ammo in those conditions before hunting.

The 80-grain ELD-X is one of the best all-around .22-cal performers for larger game. This bullet follows the same general design as other ELD-X bullets, featuring a lead core and mechanical internal locking ring to control expansion. It has Hornady’s heat shield tip with DRVT for more consistent drag. This is one of the most efficient hunting-specific .22-cal bullets there is, with a BC that allows it to stay above 2,000 fps for over 600 yards in the 22 Creedmoor. The most unique characteristic to this bullet — compared to the ELD-X in other calibers — is that it has a proportionally thicker jacket. This was to ensure controlled expansion even at velocities over 3,000 fps.

This bullet has proven to penetrate well and do a lot of damage. I shot a whitetail buck with the 80-grain ELD-X loaded in a .223, which would have been the equivalent of about a 450-yard shot from the 22 Creed. It hit the buck, which was quartering to, through the front shoulder and exited halfway back the ribs on the opposite side. I’ve also executed a similar shot on a huge black bear from about 15 yards with a 22 ARC — leaving the bullet under the offside hide after about 18 inches of penetration.

This is an all-around performer in the 22 Creedmoor, but is more optimal in a 1:7-twist barrel. If you’re planning on using it in cold weather, you should test it beforehand.

Many hunters will intentionally steer clear of match ammunition and Hornady does not recommend this ammo for deer hunting. Sometimes folks try stuff without asking permission, however, and it ends up working pretty damned well. Now, seeing how well the 80-grain ELD-X works, I’m not compelled to choose the nearly identical ELD-M bullet over it. They have the same BC and often have nearly the same point of impact and velocity. But this load has crushed a lot of bucks and is a favorite of Derrick Ratliff of Horizon Firearms and one of the big players in bringing the 22 Creed to the commercial market.

The ELD-M bullet will generally be more fragile than the ELD-X, and it creates even more violent wound cavities. This doesn’t mean it explodes on shoulders and won’t penetrate — that’s not the case. But when seeing the holes it puts through deer, you may want to tone it down. There’s no harm if you choose a more rigidly constructed bullet, and you should always be thoughtful about the shots you do take by keeping your bullet construction in mind. Still, this ammo is out there killing bucks with authority.

If you need or want a monolithic copper bullet, this will likely be the only factory option you can find. It uses a 65-grain CX bullet, which is a comfortable 1:8-twist bullet. In testing, Hornady found the 70-grain CX — made for the 1:7-twist 22 ARC — doesn’t stabilize well. I was able to shoot a pronghorn with this bullet, handloaded for an 18-inch 22 Creedmoor, before factory ammo was available. The bullet passed through both shoulders and the buck was dead before he hit the ground.

If you’re a fan of shoulder-shooting deer, this is a bullet you will be very happy with. The CX is designed for high weight retention and won’t produce a wound as violent as the ELD-X or ELD-M, but it is a penetrating son-of-a-gun. The 65-grain CX has a lower BC than the 80-grain lead-core bullets, but has enough velocity to help it maintain a similar effective range. For most deer hunters choosing a 22 Creed, you’ll be hunting at distances where that lower BC won’t come into play.

We’ve been big fans of the Federal Fusion line for deer ammo because it’s affordable, shoots well, and delivers great terminal performance. Federal just announced this 22 Creedmoor deer ammo, but I saw the press release a few days after buying some in a local store, so it should be available for rifle seasons. This is probably the most affordable 22 Creed ammo that’s deer-appropriate, and it features the same plated jacket as other Fusion bullets.

I’ve gone through a couple boxes of this and the ammo shot pretty well. It averaged 3,100 fps from a 22-inch barrel, which would put it around 125 fps slower than box velocity of 3,300 fps in a 24-inch barrel. Velocities can vary depending on several factors, but it does track with a lot of factory 22 CM ammo in the 70-80-grain range which ranges from 3,050 to 3,200 fps from 20-inch barrels — the most common contemporary barrel length.

If maximizing efficiency is important to you, there are better bullets, but this should work comfortably at any temperature in a standard 1:8 twist barrel, and will deliver great performance for the needs of most deer hunters.

Another excellent option for deer would be Nosler’s 70-grain AccuBond offering. This is more of a legacy bullet — made for 1:9- or 1:8-twist barrels — that’s also loaded in .223, rather than one of the more optimized 22 Creedmoor options. I would love to see Nosler come out with a heavier, more efficient option that leverages the Creed’s case and chamber design and load this to a bit more competitive speeds. Still, this is a deadly and effective bullet at normal deer-hunting distances, and would be a good choice.

Modern bullets are what really make the 22 Creedmoor shine as a deer cartridge. We have options that are more efficient with more predictable performance than ever. If you want to hunt deer with a 22 Creedmoor, pick ammo that will fit how and where you hunt.

Read Next: Best Ammo for Deer Hunting, Tested for Accuracy

Most people won’t have to worry about bullet stability with factory ammo, but it’s something you need to pay attention to at low elevation in temperatures below freezing. As with any hunting rifle and ammo, the most important factor is you. Pick a rifle and ammo you like and get out there and practice.

Tyler Freel is a Staff Writer for Outdoor Life. He lives in Fairbanks, Alaska and has been covering a variety of topics for OL for more than a decade. From backpack sheep hunting adventure stories to DIY tips to gear and gun reviews, he covers it all with a perspective that’s based in experience.

The Best Roof Rakes of 2026, Expert-Tested and Reviewed

📅 2026-11-09  |  Read Source →

100+ Most Unique and Practical Gifts 🎁 for Homeowners and DIYers

By Zach Lazzari, Additional research provided by Glenda Taylor, Timothy Dale

Published Nov 9, 2026 9:00 AM EST

We may earn revenue from the products available on this page and participate in affiliate programs. Learn More ›

According to the Federal Emergency Management Agency (FEMA), one foot of wet snow can weigh as much as 21 pounds per square foot—and that weight is exactly why using a roof rake can be essential to prevent damage.

To help you find the right tool for the job, we tested a range of different rakes on a tall shed and a camper after heavy snowfalls in Montana, working on metal and shingle roofs with different heights and pitches. Our favorite was the combo pack from Avalanche! since it can handle large amounts of snow and includes built-in wheels to prevent damage. Plus, it comes with different heads for different needs.

Keep reading to learn more about that model and the rest of the best roof rake options available.

Bottom line: Choosing the right roof rake can prevent damage and ensure safety during snow removal. Explore top picks and expert advice for the best options.

Note that roof rakes are intended for snow removal on single-story homes only. They should only be used when standing on the ground, and never while standing on a ladder. Attempting to balance a long pole while navigating snow-slick ladder rungs is a surefire way to slip and fall. Even if you have great balance, the weight of the snow you’re pulling off the roof could easily knock you off a ladder.

Selected for quality, functionality, material construction, and design, all of these tested products are excellent options for keeping the roof snow-free during the winter.

Equipped with a retractable plastic slide for enhanced efficiency, this rake pulls dislodged snow down the slide and off the roof, saving users time and effort. It’s also equipped with small wheels to help it glide along the roof without damaging it.

The rake extends from 4 to 15.5 feet when each fiberglass extension bar is attached. This process is simple, but it takes a few minutes to add each extension. With telescoping models being the exception, most other roof rakes use fitted extension segments similarly.

When adding an extension pole, users might need to remove gloves to press the locking button. I really liked the fiberglass poles because they aren’t nearly as cold as metal options. It is important to ensure that the segments are seated well at each joint, with the locking button in place.

Super durable, the plastic head is up to heavy-duty challenges. Raking snow off a roof is usually awkward with any rake, and that was the case with this one. But I didn’t mind a small amount of extra weight while reaching for the high spots because the durable design also gave me the confidence to hit the heavy snow spots and pull hard. I felt like the fiberglass poles were strong as well.

One other reason this won our top spot is the fact that multiple heads are included for different needs. The head that’s equipped with wheels and a slide should be able to tackle most users’ needs. The slide allows snow to fall off the roof easily, creating a pathway over gutters and other obstacles. When users hit stubborn areas and need a head that can bust through ice and grab big chunks of snow over shingles, asphalt, and tricky surfaces, they’ll want to use the rake head with wheels.

What our tester says: “I think this combo package is a great investment for anyone requiring regular use of a snow roof rake. Shoppers can find lighter-duty options for regions with less snow accumulation, but for those folks living in the mountains and regions where the snow piles up, I think this high-quality system is worth the price.”—Zach Lazzari, Product Tester and Writer

Read our full review: Avalanche! Combo Package

Get the Avalanche! Combo roof rake at The Home Depot.

The push-button telescoping design on this True Temper roof rake can extend the pole up to 17 feet, and it quickly collapses to 7 feet for storage. It weighs just 4.88 pounds, so users can easily lift it over their heads and control the movement of the rake.

If their home maintenance regularly requires removing substantial snow deposits, users will likely appreciate the lightweight aluminum design, which is easy to lift and maneuver thanks to its nonslip handle. Plus, this rake effectively pulls down deep snow accumulations in a single draw thanks to its 24-inch-wide poly blade.

This isn’t the longest roof rake out there, but its lightweight build is a trade-off that makes this model a favorite for large roofs. The telescoping handle lets the user quickly change the shaft’s length to match the required distance and pitch. Adjusting the telescoping handle is especially easy, and I found it very easy to manipulate. If you’ll be raking multiple roofs and will need to change lengths quickly, this is a great roof rake.

Although it’s a few feet shorter than some rakes, I was able to reach every spot needed on my roof. For a unique roof that requires some serious reach, there are much longer rakes out there, but they use segments rather than telescoping designs.

Get the True Temper roof rake at Amazon, The Home Depot, or Tractor Supply Co.

To properly clear a flat roof, it’s necessary to use a roof rake that doesn’t have a completely straight shaft. The shaft needs to be angled so that it can bend over the side and still sit flat on the roof. Designed with that in mind, this rake features a simple angled connector piece that fits onto the fiberglass shaft and extension bars. This gives users the ideal reach for a flat roof, like that of a truck trailer, RV, or shed.

Each extension bar is about 4 feet long, allowing the roof rake to extend to a maximum length of 15.5 feet. Also, keep in mind that each extension bar needs to be manually connected to the shaft. This is less convenient than other models that house the extensions inside the core of the shaft, allowing users to slide them out as desired. This roof rake weighs just over 7 pounds and features built-in wheels to keep the rake head from damaging the shingles.

I primarily tested this roof rake on my camper. It’s a flat roof with few obstacles aside from the two roof vents. The angled shaft made it very easy to clear the entire roof—it only took a few minutes. I think this model would also be great for semi-trailers, cargo trailers, and even flat roofs on garages and houses.

The problem with flat roofs is that they don’t allow snow to slough off naturally. This creates serious problem areas that can damage the roof and test the structural limits of a house or trailer. While straight-shaft models allow users to rake reasonably well, they still have trouble grabbing and pulling the base layers of snow. With this rake, I could easily clear the full snow load. It’s durable, sturdy, and easy to grip as well. I’m a big fan of this model and the quality of this brand’s products in general.

Get the Avalanche! Big Rig roof rake at Amazon.

Dealing with stand-alone pole extension segments can be a hassle at times. Not only is it a pain to have to lug them around while raking the roof, but it also takes time and planning to add and remove segments for different areas of the roof. It’s true that segmented designs work well for users who have a specific length need and consistent roof pitches—it’s not a bad system by any means, but all-in-one telescoping handles are much more convenient.

When you need to make adjustments on the fly and don’t want a pile of poles in the garage or truck, a telescoping handle design is an ideal option. This telescoping roof rake from Garant features a well-rounded design with a smooth-telescoping handle that extends to 16.5 feet long. It’s certainly not the longest out there, but it should be sufficient for most single-story homes.

I loved the handle and pole design of this model. It slides out in seconds, so length can be added or subtracted in a jiffy. I found the push-button design very user-friendly. Just make sure to hear a click to lock out the handle at the desired length. The coated primary pole also offers a great gripping platform for the second hand.

This model uses a standard poly blade with angled supports. The same system can be found on many other models. It’s sturdy and makes it easy to grab and pull snow. It requires more effort and strength than roller-and-slide systems, but I still found it easy to get the job done. For anyone with a normal single-story home, this roof rake is a winner. I love that it’s easy to store as a single-piece unit rather than having to deal with various poles and components.

Get the Garant roof rake at Ace Hardware.

Testing was done throughout a weeklong storm event. The first 3 days received heavy snowfall with a mix of dry powder and wet snow. The powder wasn’t problematic, but the wet snow was heavy, and it stuck to the rooftops. It also underwent a slight thaw and a subsequent freeze cycle that solidified the rooftop snow.

I waited for the storm to end and rotated through each roof rake, pulling sections of snow down. I judged each roof rake based on ease of assembly, grip, length, and ability to reach and grab snow without damaging the roof. Durability was also considered. Several of the brands have similar design features with slight variations in the way they are constructed. There was a reasonable overlap when it comes to performance and function with these look-alike models.

Make sure you’re up to date on the most important product factors before deciding on the best option to rake snow from your roof and prevent ice dams from forming. Some factors to consider in finding the right snow rake for roof cleaning include the type and quality of the materials, length, weight, and head design.

A roof rake for snow removal can be made of different materials, including fiberglass, polyethylene, aluminum, and wood.

Most roof rakes come with adjustable poles that can be lengthened from 14 to 22 feet for snow removal and shortened to less than 8 feet for storage. The longer the pole, the higher users will be able to reach on the roof, but longer poles can also be unwieldy and may require more physical strength to lift and maneuver.

Roof rakes range in weight between 4 and 14 pounds, with most of the weight in the rake head. The heavier the rake head, the more likely it is to sink deeply into the snow on the roof, allowing users to remove a good amount with each pull. Conversely, heavy roof rakes require more physical strength to lift and position.

Roof rake heads range in depth from a narrow 3 inches to a generous 10 inches, and widths run between 12 and 24 inches. The larger the rake head, the more snow the rake can remove in a single pull. If the snow is wet and heavy, it can require additional physical strength to pull it, which can lead to back and shoulder fatigue.

Curved heads and interchangeable attachments, such as a scoop, blade, or plastic slide, can greatly decrease fatigue if used properly. It’s also a good idea to consider looking for a roof rake with built-in wheels or bumpers to help protect the roof and shingles from damage. This is especially important when working with an aluminum rake head or if the rake has a blade attachment.

When starting to rake the snow off of a roof, it’s important to begin the process by removing the snow from the roof’s edge first. If users attempt to extend the roof rake as much as possible and simply pull down any snow caught within this range, there is a good chance that they will be unable to move the snow. Also, keep in mind that even if the snow does move, it isn’t a good idea to bring down that much snow at once because the weight of the snow could cause injury.

While the goal of snow removal on a driveway is to clear any snow or ice that could cause the car to slip, it’s not actually necessary to rake every inch of snow off the roof. Leaving one or two inches of snow isn’t going to cause ice dams to form, and this gap can help prevent the roof shingles from being damaged during raking.

Additionally, under no circumstances should users attempt to climb a ladder or walk on a sloped roof in winter conditions to remove snow. The entire purpose of a roof rake is for the user to remain on the ground, so don’t risk trying to rake the roof while perched on a ladder.

For more information on the best roof rake for snow removal at your home, take a look at some of the most commonly asked questions and the detailed answers below.

Unlike general-purpose rakes, snow rakes (also known as roof rakes) are specially designed for one purpose: to remove snow from single-story roofs without having to climb. This makes it much easier and safer to keep the roof clear of snow and prevent ice dams from forming in the gutter system.

The blade on a roof rake head can damage the shingles on a roof. This is especially true with aluminum rake heads, but this problem can be avoided if you invest in a roof rake with built-in wheels. These wheels elevate the rake slightly, suspending the rake head above the surface of the shingles instead of grating against them.

If you rake the roof the same day it snows, you can prevent ice build-up in the gutter system.

Whether or not you need a roof rake depends on how much snow naturally builds up on your roof. Some houses have steep roofs that don’t collect a lot of snow, while others may have a gentle slope. Metal roofs can be raked with a roof rake to keep them clear of snow, but in most cases, the snow will tend to slide off a metal roof without a problem. In fact, many homes with metal roofs need snow guards or snow fences to prevent piles of snow from sliding off and potentially hurting anyone standing nearby.

It’s a good idea to rake the roof on the same day it snows, just as it isn’t wise to wait long to shovel your driveway. Keep in mind that freshly fallen snow is much easier to move than snow that has been allowed to melt and refreeze under the sun.
