The Best Benchtop Planers, Tested and Reviewed

📅 2026-11-09  |  Read Source →

100+ Most Unique and Practical Gifts 🎁 for Homeowners and DIYers

By Glenda Taylor, Mark Wolfe, Additional research provided by Tom Scalisi

Published Nov 9, 2026 9:00 AM EST

We may earn revenue from the products available on this page and participate in affiliate programs. Learn More ›

Benchtop planers—box-shaped power tools that reduce board thickness while smoothing the surface—top many a woodcrafter’s wish list. They fine-tune the wood used to create cabinets, bookshelves, and more, replacing old-fashioned hand planes because they’re much faster and easier to use.

Like our readers, we wanted to know which of today’s popular models perform well in real workshop situations, so we tested them. We selected highly rated benchtop planers and started planing boards—hundreds of ’em! We also reached out to Jay Sanders, licensed contractor and owner of Castle Dream Construction, a home remodeling company based in Baltimore, Maryland, to get his insights on what to consider when shopping for one.

After testing, we liked the 15-amp DeWalt 13-Inch 3-Knife 2-Speed Thickness Planer the best. We chose it based on its built-in infeed and outfeed tables, fan-assisted chip ejection vacuums, and impressive speed.

Keep reading to find out how the following models earned a spot in this lineup of the best benchtop planers for DIYers and seasoned woodworkers.

Bottom line: Our tests reveal top benchtop planers for various needs, with DeWalt leading for overall performance and versatility.

Investing in the right benchtop planer is important for anyone who’s into woodworking. We put the top DIY and professional models through their paces and scrutinized each. The models that earned a spot in our lineup are among the best benchtop thickness planers available today, so one is likely to find a favored place in your workshop.

Our Ratings: Ease of Use 5/5; Performance 5/5; Board Capacity 5/5; Dust 4.7/5; Value 4.8/5

Those in the market for a high-capacity planer with plenty of features will want to consider the DeWalt 13-inch thickness planer. This benchtop planer can handle materials up to a 13-inch width capacity with a 6-inch length. This heavy planer weighs in at 92 pounds, and we found its weight was beneficial in keeping it in one place during operation. We put the planer on a reinforced work surface—this tool is too heavy for most portable-type bolt holes in the base.

We’re familiar with the high quality of many DeWalt tools, and this thickness planer is no exception. It boasts a powerful 15-amp motor and a three-blade cutterhead that spins at 10,000 revolutions per minute (rpm). It can be set to plane off as much as ⅛ inch of wood in a single pass. It comes standard with front and back stainless steel infeed and outfeed tables that help support the boards as they pass through the planer. A unique option on this benchtop planer is the ability to adjust the feed speed. A lever on the front let us select either 96 or 179 cuts per inch (cpi). At 95 cpi, the DeWalt planer pulled the boards through twice as quickly as when we selected 179 cpi, but the surface was slightly rougher. Interestingly, even at 96 cpi, we found the boards were smooth enough for most high-end woodworking. At 179 cpi, they felt as though they’d already been sanded. This is in large part because of the three straight blades in the DeWalt planer. Most of today’s planers have only two blades.

We planed dozens of boards with the DeWalt model: softwoods, including pine and cedar; and hardwoods, including ash, oak, and walnut. The DeWalt planed all of them smoothly, and we found little to no snipe on any of the boards. We attributed this benefit to an extension outfeed table that helps support the boards as they exit the machine. The benchtop planer has a fan-assisted chip-ejection port on the back of the machine that we connected to our dust-collection system. The chip-ejection fan is so powerful that when we walked behind the machine to grab the board as it passed through, the chips stung when they hit us. Impressive! And slightly painful. The downside is that the chip-ejection port is positioned just over the outfeed table, so the hose on our dust-collection system kept dipping down and interfering with the boards as they exited the planer. Our makeshift fix was to tie the hose up to keep it out of the way.

We believe this top-notch planer would be an asset in any DIYer or woodcrafter’s shop. It’s powerful, the feed speed is adjustable, and the ruler gauge is accurate. (We measured the planed boards with calipers to determine gauge accuracy.) The model comes with a “bump” on/off switch in the front that allowed us to shut the machine down simply by bumping the switch with our hand or arm. This is a safety feature in the event something goes wrong—such as a board getting caught sideways or an article of clothing getting drawn into the machine. Just a bump, and the DeWalt shuts down.

What our tester says: “I am a firm believer in ‘buy it once, use it forever,’ and the DeWalt DW735 feels like that kind of tool. Hardwood, softwood, fresh and sappy or old and dried boards, the finished faces always come out clean and smooth.”—Mark Wolfe, Product Reviews writer and tester

See our full review: DeWalt 13-Inch 3-Knife 2-Speed Thickness Planer

Get the DeWalt benchtop planer at Amazon, Lowe’s, or The Home Depot.

Our Ratings: Ease of Use 4.5/5; Performance 4/5; Board Capacity 4.5/5; Dust 4.5/5; Value 4.8/5

As a reasonably priced wood planer that can handle up to 12.25-inch-wide boards, the Craftsman benchtop thickness planer is worth a look. The manufacturer has a long and reputable history of making durable tools and quality machines. At just 61.8 pounds, the Craftsman was a bit easier to lift and position on the work surface, but we noted that this lighter weight resulted in the machine “walking” just a bit when we planed large hardwood boards. Fortunately, there’s a simple solution: Just bolt it down to a workbench. The benchtop planer has a 15-amp motor that produces cutter speeds up to 8,000 rpm. Thanks to its two-blade straight-knife cutter design, it provides 16,000 cuts per minute, which we found to be more than adequate for most small cabinetry and furniture-building projects.

This Craftsman planer accepts boards up to 6 inches long, and it comes with a safety bump on/off switch on the front and a precision gauge along the side for selecting the correct cutting height. The planer removes up to ⅛ inch. The Craftsman doesn’t offer a feed-speed adjustment, and we couldn’t find a feed-speed rating listed in the owner’s manual, but we used a stopwatch and came up with an approximate feed speed of 23 feet per minute. The planed boards were smooth enough that a light sanding would remove any residual grain roughness, but we did find a 2-inch snipe at the end of every board we planed. This is a common occurrence with planers; we didn’t deduct any points—just be aware that it occurs and plan to plane boards a couple of inches longer than the desired finished length. Those who won’t use a benchtop planer often and are a bit short on space might appreciate the fold-up infeed and outfeed tables that make storage a bit easier. We found this handy for setting the planer out of the way under a table.

Get the Craftsman benchtop planer at Amazon or Lowe’s.

Our Ratings: Ease of Use 4.7/5; Performance 4.7/5; Board Capacity 5/5; Dust 3/5; Value 4.5/5

Anyone looking for a portable planer that provides extra maximum thickness—for timber frame mailbox posts, say, or heavy furniture—will want to check out the 2012NB 12-inch planer from Makita. This machine has an above-average 6 3/32-inch maximum thickness capacity, which is slightly higher than the others we tested. The real draw of the Makita 2012NB comes in its stability and quiet operation. It’s remarkably stable for its relatively light weight of just 61.9 pounds. It didn’t walk or shift, even when we ran large hardwood boards through, making it one of the best portable thickness planers that do need to be bolted down. Plus, though hearing protection is still recommended, it’s noticeably quieter than the other models we tested—a sound output of 83 decibels, according to the manufacturer. The other planers don’t list the decibel factor but were, in our estimation, about a quarter louder than the Makita.

This planer has a 15-amp motor that produces 8,500 rpm on the cutterhead. It has two straight-knife cutters and a cutting depth of up to 1/8 inch. The Interna-Lok head clamp holds the board in place better than standard rollers, and we didn’t find any snipe on boards shorter than 4 feet long. As we planed longer boards, however, we began to see a faint snipe line on the last 2 inches of the board. By supporting the end of the board as it came out, we could eliminate the snipe line. Whether we removed 1/8 inch or 1/64 inch, the planed boards came out very smooth and would need only light sanding for most projects.

The feed speed is not adjustable, but the Makita makes quick work of pulling the boards through—the manufacturer lists the feed rate as 27.9 feet per minute, which seemed about right in our tests, but keep in mind that heavy boards typically move slightly slower than small lightweight boards. The planer comes with a bump power switch and a precision gauge, and although it doesn’t have a built-in dust-collection port, an attachment is sold separately.

Get the Makita benchtop planer at Amazon, The Home Depot, or Ace Tool.

Our Ratings: Ease of Use 4.5/5; Performance 4/5; Board Capacity 4.5/5; Dust 4/5; Value 4.2/5

Those looking for a robust planer for resurfacing rough or reclaimed lumber, or smoothing and thinning new lumber, may want to consider the Wen benchtop thickness planer. We found this model well suited to removing up to 1/8 inch of wood from the surface of boards up to 12.5 inches wide. In addition, it handles board heights of up to 6 inches. With 10,000 rpm, this planer offers plenty of power for smoothing rough wood. Its two straight blades make up to 20,000 cuts per minute. The height adjustment handle on this planer is slightly smaller than some—and it’s located at the top of the machine—but it still follows the same general rule of one turn equals 1/16 inch.

When we planed the first board on the Wen and then measured it for precision, we found it was 1/32 inch too high. That may not sound like a lot, but it’s substantial when it comes to creating uniform board height for a project. Fortunately, the gauge is adjustable, so we used a small screwdriver to adjust it 1/32 inch and then ran another board through. Perfect! Most planer gauges come preset, but occasionally one can be slightly off. Just adjust it and try again. We did notice some snipe at the last couple of inches, so we recommend planing boards at least 2 inches longer than needed. There’s no feed-speed adjustment, but according to the manufacturer, the planer moves boards as quickly as 26 feet per minute, depending on size.

At just 55.6 pounds, the Wen is slightly on the light side, and we needed to bolt it down to keep it from moving during operation. It comes with a conveniently located port that we attached to our dust-collection system. We did notice some small chips remained on the feed plate after planing. This isn’t a big issue, but the ejection fan didn’t seem to eject all of them through the port. If necessary, this can be remedied by just blowing the chips out before inserting a new board. Like many other quality planers, the Wen has a bump-type on/off switch for added safety.

Get the Wen benchtop planer at Amazon, Lowe’s, or The Home Depot.

Our Ratings: Ease of Use 4/5; Performance 4/5; Board Capacity 4.5/5; Dust 4/5; Value 4.3/5

Besides our Best Overall pick, the Ridgid 13-inch planer is the only other three-blade planer we tested, and it impressed us on many fronts. It features a maximum cut allowance of ⅛ inch and accepts boards up to 13 inches wide and 6⅛ inches high. In addition to its gauge, which is easy to read and comes with 1/64-inch increments, it also features a separate Ind-I-Cut gauge on the front of the planer that allowed us to see exactly how much wood we were removing in a pass. We found this feature valuable, especially because tiny 1/64-inch increments can be challenging to see. The Ind-I-Cut acted like a magnifying glass to let us see the amount of wood we were removing as we planned. The Ridgid planer features 9,000 rpm at the cutterhead, which translates into 27,000 cuts per minute, an enviable number of cuts in the world of planers.

We tested it on dozens of boards, both hardwood and softwood species, large and small, and it powered through all of them without bogging down. We did get a little snipe at the ends of the boards, but given how much power—and how smooth—the Ridgid performs, it was a minor downside. We found that supporting the ejected boards helped lessen, but didn’t fully eliminate, the snipe. As with the other planers we tested, we appreciated the large bump on/off switch for safety. At 73 pounds, the Ridgid remained firmly rooted while we planed board after board. It features a chip-ejector port that connects to a standard dust-collection system.

This planer is suitable for removing rough, weathered surface grain as well as fine-tuning hardwood for furniture and cabinetry projects. Its third blade and its Ind-I-Cut gauge pretty much puts it in a class by itself. Those who want an aggressive three-blade planer but don’t want to spend the extra bucks may wish to consider the Ridgid planer. We were delighted with its performance.

Get the Ridgid benchtop planer at The Home Depot.

When selecting the best benchtop planers, Sanders considers several factors, including cutterhead type, power, and whether the benchtop planer comes with a table that supports the boards. We looked at all of these factors during the selection process, plus we considered how well each planer rated with experienced users. We also considered the brand to an extent. After all, manufacturers such as DeWalt, Makita, and Craftsman have top-notch reputations in the power tool industry. We didn’t stick with just those three; we also included additional highly rated models from other manufacturers.

We unboxed and set up the benchtop planers according to manufacturer specifications. Then, we started planing new wood, reclaimed wood, softwood boards, and hardwood boards. We paid special attention to how smooth the planers left the board surfaces and whether the machines left snipes (unwanted cuts in the last few inches of the board).

We analyzed overall performance and ease of use and awarded points based on a rubric. The better a planer performed, the higher the points. After testing, we averaged the points to help determine the best category and intended use.

Benchtop planers, also known as “thickness planers” because they reduce the thickness of a board, have rotating blades inside that shave off a thin layer of wood. Those who are interested in owning one but don’t have a lot of experience with this powerful tool will want to keep the following factors and features in mind when shopping.

A benchtop planer’s motor needs enough power to get the job done. How much is enough depends on specific projects.

If users work primarily with softer woods such as pine, cedar, and fir—for building country-style furniture or birdhouses, for example—a motor in the 1- to 1½-horsepower (hp) range might suit them well. A thickness planer doesn’t need much juice to shave a layer off the top of these materials.

However, if users are into high-end joinery or cabinetry, they’ll probably work with species such as oak, maple, and even walnut. These hard materials will require more from a planer to shave them down, so they’ll want to look for a 2 hp tool.

Perhaps even more important than horsepower is a planer’s motor rating—choose one with a 15-amp motor. Planers draw a lot of electricity, and Sanders suggests getting a powerful one. “Look for at least a 15-amp motor with multiple speed options,” he advises. A 15-amp motor helps ensure the machine can handle the task without breaking down. Smaller-amperage motors don’t have the power necessary for planing wood.

Thickness planer blades (or knives) come in different styles. “The three main options are straight knife, helical, and spiral/helix,” Sanders says. “The straight knife is the most affordable but produces more tear-out. Helical and spiral cut cleaner but cost more upfront. Spiral cutter heads are generally regarded as producing the best finish.”

Blades vary on straight-knife cutter models. Some are reversible, so when they become dull, the user can turn them over and use the other side. Others have only one sharp edge, but they can be manually sharpened with a standard sharpening stone, just like a kitchen knife. Reversible knives are disposable, meant to be tossed out after both sides wear down.

All the planers we tested came with cutterheads designed to accept straight blades.

Cutting depth refers to how much material a wood planer can remove in one pass. Generally speaking, the more powerful the planer, the more material it can remove in one pass and the larger the depth of cut.

Planers that can shave off up to ⅛ inch at a time are good for removing the surface of rough or weathered wood. Removing that much depth at once, however, isn’t always optimal. Generally, it’s usually best for the board—and for the machine—to err on the side of caution and plane off thinner amounts. The more wood a planer removes in a pass, the harder it must work, which can lead to overheating if the user is planing a lot of boards.

Many woodworkers will find little need to remove more than 1/16 inch per pass, and for fine-tuning boards, they will remove even less—1/32 or 1/64 inch. Don’t pass up a planer just because it doesn’t remove ⅛ inch per pass. It’s easy enough to set the planer at 1/16 inch and then run it through twice.

A thickness planer’s allowance indicates the widest (and tallest) board that can be passed through the tool. Manufacturers label most planers with ranges between 12 inches and 13 inches (plenty for most woodworking hobbyists), but this metric doesn’t tell the whole story.

For example, a 12-inch planer can handle a board 12 inches in width capacity, but that does not say how thick (i.e., tall) a piece of wood the planer can handle. This metric is quite helpful, so look for a model that can handle materials as thick as 5 or 6 inches to get the most use out of a wood planer.

Snipe refers to an undesirable line, which can sometimes be very noticeable, left on the top end of a board after planing. It’s the result of the wood planer removing more wood at the end of the board. As the end of the board clears the first set of rollers, gravity tends to pull the board downward, which puts pressure on the other end. When this happens, the cutterhead blades may remove slightly more wood at the very end. A snipe looks like a thin dip, usually less than 1/16 inch, located along the last couple inches of the board.

“Longer infeed and outfeed tables help support boards and minimize snipe,” Sanders explains. He adds that “36-inch to 44-inch tables are good for a benchtop planer.” Just make sure they fold down for storage. Snipe may be unavoidable to some degree, but some benchtop thickness planers do better than others at minimizing its effect. A good way to avoid snipe appearing on a  finished product is to leave boards longer than necessary by an inch or so when planning and then cut the sniped ends off before using the board for your project.

Shaving the intended amount of wood off your workpieces can be tedious. Bringing a hardwood board from a 1-inch thickness to a ⅝-inch thickness, for example, could take five or six passes. The user could measure the board’s thickness before each pass and adjust the cutterhead using a tape measure or a combination square, but that’s time-consuming.

Many of today’s benchtop planers have a built-in gauge that looks like a ruler attached to the side of the intake slot. The gauge will indicate the proposed board thickness as the user raises or lowers the planer’s carriage (the top part of the tool that moves up and down) via a hand crank.

Planer gauges typically show measurements in 1/16-inch increments, but some show measurements in 1/64-inch increments. Some experienced woodworkers don’t use the gauge at all but rather determine cutting depth with the tool’s hand crank. On a planer, a complete revolution of its hand crank equals 1/16 inch. Half a crank equals 1/32 inch, and a quarter of a crank equals 1/64 inch.

A depth stop is a feature that keeps the user from shaving off more than the machine’s maximum cutting depth. For example, if the planer has a maximum cutting depth of ⅛ inch, the depth stop will prevent the user from inserting any board more than ⅛ inch thicker than the thickness chosen on the gauge (ruler). The board simply will not go into the slot. In this way, users needn’t worry about taking off more than the maximum cutting depth because the depth stop won’t allow it.

Thickness planers create a good deal of dust and wood chips. These machines rip off and spit out tiny pieces of wood, and the floor under a thickness planer will resemble a child’s sandbox after only a few boards.

Many of today’s benchtop planers have dust ports that attach to a shop vacuum or dust-collection system to minimize the mess. These collection systems also do a good job of removing the dust from the machine before it can settle in the cutterhead, helping to maintain its usable life and cutting speed.

Different speeds apply to a benchtop planer: cutterhead speed, cutting speed, and feed speed.

Alongside other popular power tools, such as band saws, table saws, and wood lathes, benchtop thickness planers are a boon in a woodworking shop. If this is your first planer, you will probably have questions about the tool’s best use and operation. Ahead are answers to some of the most common questions about benchtop planers. If you still want more information about your benchtop planer, refer to the user’s manual or contact the manufacturer.

Similar to hand planes, benchtop planers reduce the thickness of a board. Woodcrafters can use them to create consistent thicknesses on several boards for woodworking purposes or to uncover the beauty hidden underneath reclaimed wood.

With the benchtop planer turned off, place a board in the intake feed slot. Lower the roller carriage (the top part of the planer) using the hand crank until it just touches the board. This is the starting point. Remove the board, and then use the crank to lower the roller carriage to the desired cutting depth (typically between 1/64 and ⅛ inch). The woodworker can use the ruler gauge to determine the cutting depth or use the crank rule of thumb—one full revolution of the crank equals 1/16 inch. Once the cutting height is decided, turn the planer on and slowly feed the board into the intake slot. The planer will do the rest—users don’t have to push the board.

Planers reduce the thickness of a board by shaving a uniform amount of wood off its surface. Jointers make the board flat by shaving away only the high spots, not the entire surface, as a planer does.

Wood planers can be some of the heaviest tools in your shop. Benchtop planers weigh an average of between 60 and 100 pounds, so it may be wise to keep it on a benchtop rather than moving it around.

A high-fiber diet may clear away plastic in the gut

📅 2026-11-09  |  Read Source →

Every print subscription comes with full digital access

Mice that ate lots of fiber had far less particles accumulate in the body

A diet that includes high-fiber foods, like these vegetables, might come with a surprise benefit. Fiber could help carry nanoplastics out of the body, suggests a new study in mice.

Here’s a new reason to eat your beans and greens.

A high-fiber diet may help sweep plastic particles from the gut, researchers report October 15 in Nature Communications. In mice that ingested plastic, eating loads of fiber daily kept the particles from piling up in the intestines. The finding hints at fiber’s power to protect the body from environmental garbage, says Eddy Y. Zeng, an environmental scientist at South China University of Technology in Guangzhou.

We summarize the week's scientific breakthroughs every Thursday.

Researchers have long known that fiber can improve our health, in part by lowering cholesterol, keeping blood sugar levels in check and easing food through the gut. But now, Zeng says, his team’s results suggest an additional benefit.

We live in a world littered with plastic, much of it too small to see. These micro and nanoplastics, shed by disposable water bottles, fast food containers and other worldly junk, can make their way into our bodies. It’s not completely clear what such shards do to our health, but we have some hints. Scientists have linked plastic particles in people’s arteries to increased risk of heart attack and stroke. And mice that ingest tiny bits of plastic can show signs of organ injury.

Zeng’s team thought dietary fiber might provide some defense. Fiber forms a complex network of polymers that could trap ingested plastics like flies in a spider web, he says.

The researchers put mice on low-, medium-, or high-fiber diets, then fed them plastic particles. Over the next 24 hours, the medium- and high-fiber groups of mice cleared more particles from their bodies than their low-fiber counterparts, the team discovered. That’s important, Zeng says, because these particles can do damage. After a week of daily plastic feedings, his team saw gut tissue damage — but only in the low-fiber group. Experiments in test tubes suggest that fiber physically wraps around and sticks to plastic particles.

It’s too soon to say if dietary fiber does the same in humans. Zeng’s study was performed in mice and not tethered to realistic fiber numbers. Though mice in the high-fiber group ate far more fiber than what what’s recommended for people, he thinks the findings are worth pursuing in follow-up studies.

And even if fiber doesn’t end up purging plastic from people’s guts, Zeng says, it probably wouldn’t hurt for us to add a little more to our diets.

Questions or comments on this article? E-mail us at feedback@sciencenews.org | Reprints FAQ

Z. Yu et al. High fiber diet mitigates exposure risk of nanoplastics in male mice. Nature Communications. 2026. doi: 10.1038/s41467-026-78675-7

Albatross couple George and Geraldine reunite for the 10th straight year

📅 2026-11-09  |  Read Source →

Published Nov 9, 2026 9:00 AM EST

The Midway Atoll National Wildlife Refuge near Hawaii has welcomed a special couple: George and Geraldine, two short-tailed albatrosses (Phoebastria albatrus). The birds have officially found each other for nesting season on the Midway Atoll for the 10th straight year in a row.  George was first spotted on October 18, and the pair was reunited by October 22.

Every fall, the remote group of islands becomes a temporary home to the world’s largest albatross colony. Albatrosses are a group of large migratory seabird species, including the wandering albatross (Diomedea exulans), which has the largest wingspan of any current bird species. One Laysan albatross (Phoebastria immutabilis), on the other hand, claims the title of oldest known banded wild bird—the famous Wisdom, who is over 70 years old.

Short-tailed albatrosses (called makalena in Hawaiian) are almost completely white and feature a large pink bill. They can live up to 50 years, and as exemplified by George and Geraldine, are monogamous. They nest on islands and atolls, and the species is listed as endangered by both the United States government and the state of Hawaii. At the turn of the 20th century, feather hunting almost made the species go extinct.

“The vast majority of breeding for short-tailed albatross occurs on remote islands in the western Pacific, including Torishima Island, Japan, which is an active volcano south of Tokyo, and the Senkaku Islands near Taiwan,” according to the U.S. Fish and Wildlife Service (FWS). “Since 2000, biologists at the [Midway Atoll National Wildlife] refuge have used short-tailed albatross decoys and audio playback of the seabird’s calls to attract more of them.”

In fact, George and Geraldine are the only breeding pair of short-tailed albatross on the Midway Atoll, though other members of their species were spotted last season. They were banded as chicks on Torishima Island and first seen together on Midway Atoll National Wildlife Refuge in 2016. The cute pair brought their first chick into the world during the 2018-2019 breeding season.

“Millions of makalena once used their long wingspans to expertly glide on air currents across the northern Pacific Ocean, from Japan to Alaska and Hawaiʻi,” the FWS writes. “The slowly growing population is estimated at around 10,000 today.”

<a href="https://www.fiercehealthcare.com/ai-and-machine-learning/deerfield-taps-aws-fuel-healthcare-ai-ambitions" hreflang="en">Deerfield taps AWS to fuel healthcare AI ambitions</a>

📅 2026-11-09  |  Read Source →
Deerfield taps AWS to fuel healthcare AI ambitions" style="max-width:100%; height:auto; border-radius:8px;" loading="lazy">

Investment firm Deerfield Management is tapping into Amazon Web Services' AI and cloud technology to help AI-native healthcare and life sciences companies build and scale.

The collaboration brings together AWS’s generative AI and cloud services with the investment firm's team of AI engineers and the innovation ecosystem of the firm’s affiliate, Cure.

The firm’s Deerfield Intelligence and Deerfield Discovery and Development (3DC) teams will receive access to AI and cloud technology from AWS, as well as technical support, to scale proprietary healthcare AI products. Deerfield 3DC is the firm's internal research and development engine, while Deerfield Intelligence is the firm's data, technology and AI arm.

Select portfolio companies will get access to AWS' technical enablement and training on topics including large-scale model training, inference optimization and healthcare-specific managed services.

"This collaboration aims to drive the development of the next generation of AI-powered healthcare organizations,” said Dan Sheeran, vice president and general manager of Healthcare and Life Sciences at AWS, in a statement. "With support from AWS, interested Deerfield portfolio companies can be equipped with AI services specific to supporting healthcare and life sciences needs like Amazon Bio Discovery, as well as agentic services like Amazon Bedrock AgentCore to build AI agents and support organizations at a scale that wouldn’t have been feasible only a few years ago."

RELATED: Deerfield unveils $600M for emerging healthcare therapeutics and tech

Deerfield is working to build an ambitious AI-native platform in healthcare, according to Jim Flynn, managing partner at Deerfield. "Our Intelligence team is developing models that aim to transform how we source opportunities, evaluate science, and accelerate portfolio companies," he said.

The investment firm supports a portfolio of more than 200 private and public healthcare and life sciences investments. In May 2025, Deerfield unveiled the third installment of its healthcare innovations fund, earmarking more than $600 million for promising therapeutics and technologies.

Through the AWS partnership, Deerfield is taking steps to build AI infrastructure across its investment ecosystem. AWS will become a common technology layer that Deerfield can use internally and make available to portfolio companies, potentially helping startups move from AI development to deployment more quickly.

Ascertain, a Deerfield-backed healthcare technology company, is already using AWS infrastructure for its agentic AI platform, which automates workflows such as case management and prior authorization.

"We built Ascertain to give clinicians time back by automating the administrative burden in care, and we believe AWS’s purpose-built services for the healthcare industry will help us scale these capabilities," said Mark Michalski, M.D., chief information officer at Deerfield and CEO of Ascertain, in a statement. "We believe Deerfield’s collaboration with AWS and the AI and cloud services it makes available to Ascertain and other portfolio companies is part of a foundation with the potential to make AI-driven transformation possible at scale."

Deerfield’s headquarters at Cure, a healthcare innovation campus in New York City, sits at the center of one of the world’s densest healthcare ecosystems, home to leading academic medical centers, a growing biotech sector, and a deep network of clinicians and investors, according to the investment firm.

Playfool spotlights hidden digital labour in data centre cleaning game at the V&A

📅 2026-11-09  |  Read Source →

Photography and video by Nico Lejtreger

Visitors to the Victoria and Albert Museum in London vacuumed digital dust from an overheating data server in game installation The Server by art and design duo Playfool.

Handling a decommissioned data-centre vacuum formerly used in a data centre, players were tasked with vacuuming as much ‘dust’ as possible within 60 seconds from a replica data server.

The installation was designed to emulate the unseen physical labour tied to the technological infrastructure underpinning the AI boom, and to evoke the gamified optimisation strategies employed in Amazon fulfilment centres.

During their visit to one, the design duo “discovered the unique internal video games workers can play to gamify their tasks, displaying real-time performance rankings among peers”.

The Server game emerged as a means of surfacing the often overlooked physical labour that goes into maintaining the ever-expanding material infrastructure fuelled by the rapid growth of AI.

“We often overlook the fact that technologies are based on a material foundation, subject to the forces of an ever-changing environment, and thus requiring constant upkeep after being brought into existence”, Playfool’s Daniel Coppen and Saki Maruyama told Dezeen.

Their installation consists of a replica of technology brand Nvidia’s latest AI server and a vacuum module featuring a set of screens that visualise clouds of digital particulates and rank players’ performance metrics.

It was intended to show that our relationship to the digital world is less frictionless than we might think.

“Behind the frictionless, smooth interfaces on our screens is a messy entanglement with the material world”, the designers added.

Situated amid the priceless antiquities of the V&A, the installation draws a parallel between the hidden human labour that sustains the availability of both a museum’s collections and the digital infrastructure we are increasingly dependent upon.

In this way, the game “serves as a reminder that all creation comes with an infinite responsibility of maintenance”, the designers said.

“AI has been especially prone to this blind spot, due to both the black box nature of how it works and of the infrastructure behind it, from undersea fibre-optic cables to data centres”, Coppen and Maruyama explained.

With the US set to double its data centre building to keep pace with the boom in AI, and spending on data centre infrastructure projected to top $800 billion per year globally in 2026, the designers hope The Server will help interrogate the responsibilities of care generated by the contemporary AI boom.

Commissioned by the V&A Museum’s Emerging Designer scheme, Playfool intended for The Server installation to help create discussions around AI and digital caregiving such as cleaning and maintenance.

“We believe games are a powerful medium to provoke conversation about these complex topics, crystallising experiences and ideas through introducing new forms of agency”, Playfool said.

For more stories related to AI data centres, see this toy from Basrua that emits an “ear piercing hum” when squeezed, this 60-building data centre by Gensler and this East London data centre approved as part of Truman Brewery masterplan.

The Server exhibition took place from 12 to 18 September at The V&A. For more events in architecture and design, visit Dezeen Events Guide.

Google’s AI Features Sent One Site An Estimated 21% Of Its Clicks via @sejournal, @MattGSouthern

📅 2026-11-09  |  Read Source →

This webinar uses Conductor's research to help you prioritize marketing data and AI visibility measurement fixes.

This rundown shows how to test briefs, audits, or rank reporting with a four-layer system built on your own data.

Guide: AI Max Drove 68% Of Retail Invalid Clicks

One site opted out of Google’s AI features to estimate its clicks from AI Overviews and AI Mode, plus a possible lift on regular listings.

AI Overviews and AI Mode accounted for about 21% of one site’s Google search clicks, according to an estimate by SEO consultant Cyrus Shepard, who took the site out of Google’s AI features for four weeks. Adding a small lift in regular-listing clicks that he attributes to AI visibility, he puts the share tied to AI features at 23%.

Shepard, founder of Zyppy SEO, excluded Zyppy’s site using Search Console’s Search generative AI control on Sept. 1 and opted back in on Sept. 29, then published the results in his newsletter on Thursday.

He describes it as a single experiment on a single site and wrote that readers should treat the findings “more as a directional yardstick than Google gospel” and not assume the numbers apply to other sites.

In less than an hour, Shepard said Zyppy’s website disappeared from the AI features on Google. Prior to this, the website was receiving anywhere from 500 to 1,000 impressions per day through AI Overviews and AI Mode, which accounted for approximately 10% of all impressions of the site via Google searches.

The help center for Google explains that exclusions generally take several days, and that Google does not consider the exclusion as a ranking or inclusion signal for other parts of its Search function.

He showed an example search where the website remained at number 1 in standard results following the exclusion but didn’t appear in the AI features, not even as a link. Following the switch back after 28 days, AI impressions returned to the site nearly as fast.

Shepard estimated the number of AI clicks by comparing the amount of total clicks that were generated by the website during the 28-day period that it was opted out of Google’s AI features to the amount of total clicks that were generated during the next 28 days once the site was opted back in. He compared those results using the same searches and position rankings in each to limit the effects of position and search query changes.

Google counts clicks on an AI Overview or AI Mode link as a standard click within Search Console’s Performance report. Neither the help page for the generative AI performance report nor Google’s help documentation for the Performance report lists any way to separate the clicks made on links in Google’s AI features from all other web-based clicks, as I mentioned on Nov. 2.

His comparison of the two periods came to an estimated click-through rate of about 0.9% for Zyppy’s AI Overview and AI Mode impressions, and he considers 0.7% to 1.1% a reasonable range. Across Google Search as a whole, the site averages a click-through rate of around 0.4%.

With AI features making up about 10% of the site’s search impressions and drawing clicks at that higher rate, he estimates they produced about 21% of its search clicks. He says Zyppy has done a fair amount of work optimizing the site to win AI citations, which may be why so many of its clicks came from AI features.

Shepard says the site’s organic results received higher click-through rates (CTR) once it appeared in the AI Overview section of search engine results pages. He described this impact on CTR as small, with the effects greatest on those URLs receiving the most AI visibility.

He calls it a halo effect, an advertising term for when people who see a site listed several times on one results page are more likely to click at least one of them. He wrote that nobody knows for sure why, and that brand reinforcement is a likely explanation.

When he added the additional clicks on his regular organic listings, he estimated that 23% of all search clicks to the site were tied to AI features.

For now, Zyppy will keep the site opted in. In his view, staying in AI search and working toward AI citations seems like “the only reasonable action to take.”

After Google started letting some UK sites use the Search Console opt-out in June, I wrote that if a website opted out prior to viewing their AI visibility data, they might give up potential traffic that they could not yet measure. Shepard’s test gives an example of how much traffic is estimated to be at issue and also counts clicks on standard listings as well as those from AI links.

The generative AI performance report shows how many of your search impressions come from AI Overviews and AI Mode. On Zyppy’s site, those impressions were about 10% of the total, while Shepard’s estimate of their share of clicks came to about 21%. He says other sites could come in higher or lower.

The UK’s Competition and Markets Authority gives Google until Dec. 3 to provide publishers with impressions, clicks, and click-through rate for AI search features, according to a summary of its rules. The CMA’s summary lists the metrics as aggregated across features such as AI Overviews and AI Mode. The rules cover AI features shown to people in the UK, and Google has three more months after Dec. 3 to roll out page-level controls for AI search features.

Clicks and click-through rate for AI features are the numbers Shepard had to estimate.

Bitcoin Price Drops Below $74,000 as Nvidia Probe and Regulation Fears Spark $1 Billion in Liquidations

📅 2026-11-09  |  Read Source →

Bitcoin price traded near $74,700 on Monday morning after sliding to $73,477 overnight, its lowest since August, then bouncing back, Bitcoin Magazine Pro data shows.

Bitcoin price fell more than 2% to $73,477 in overnight trading, its lowest since August. Buyers stepped in soon after and pushed the price back above $75,000, but the bounce stalled and the bitcoin price settled near $74,700 into the New York open.

Crypto was the only major market open when two headlines landed. Nvidia faced an internal investigation into whether CEO Jensen Huang had been targeted by a foreign intelligence effort, and Secretary of State Marco Rubio said Iranian demands were outrageous as talks stalled.

Nvidia is the bellwether of the AI trade that has carried risk assets all year, so traders treated the news as a threat to the whole complex. More than $1 billion of leveraged crypto positions were liquidated over 24 hours and about $913 million of that came from longs, which turned a dip into a sharp flush.

The stock market delivered a calmer opening. U.S. index futures attempted a recovery as investors hunted for bargains, with Dow futures up 0.73%, S&P 500 futures up 0.40% and Nasdaq 100 futures up 0.21% at 5:10 a.m. ET. Nvidia shares were down 4.5% in premarket trading, Reuters said, and some chipmakers slipped after the Philadelphia Semiconductor Index posted its biggest weekly loss since March 2025.

That split mirrors Friday, when the bitcoin price reaction to a hot jobs report showed crypto sitting out an equity bounce. It also echoes the tech rout that wiped out leveraged longs last Thursday.

The other driver is Washington. Reuters tied the weekend drop to fears that a Democratic-controlled Congress will toughen oversight of the asset class, after last week’s midterms and the $6 billion Binance heist.

Sen. Elizabeth Warren, one of the loudest crypto critics in the Senate, is tipped to lead the Senate Banking Committee. TD Cowen has warned that the sweep makes a crypto-backed CLARITY Act nearly impossible, and House Democrats are already eyeing their own market structure bill with a bigger role for the SEC.

Institutional money is voting too. U.S. spot bitcoin ETFs shed $1.28 billion last week, their second straight week of outflows and the worst since late June. Those redemptions explain why the bitcoin price never joined the equity rebound.

October consumer price data on Tuesday is the next test. Economists expect headline inflation to rise 0.3% on the month, cooling from 0.6% in September, and futures imply roughly an 80% chance of a Fed hike at the Dec. 8 and 9 meeting after the strong October jobs report.

Options traders are bracing for more movement. One-week implied volatility for bitcoin has jumped to about 43% from about 33% on Thursday, and four of the six most active bitcoin contracts are puts with strikes between $70,000 and $72,000.

On the chart, bitcoin is wedged between support from its 200-day moving average near $73,150 and resistance from its 100-day average near $77,430, levels cited in weekend market analysis. A close under the lower line would put the August lows back in play, while a push toward $77,000 would need a calmer Nvidia story and softer inflation data.

At the time of writing, the bitcoin price is near $74,700.

CSS-Tricks, Version 20

📅 2026-11-09  |  Read Source →

I’ve been bashing on updating this site’s design for the last couple of weeks. It’s high time things get a fresh coat of paint, right? The last version of CSS-Tricks was pushed five years ago. That’s ancient in web (and dog) years.

It’s not like the site has simply sat all that time. We pushed lots of changes and shipped new features, particularly since 2024, but we never really got the paint brushes out of storage.

Now, before there’s a chorus of “things don’t look thaaaat different” I want to say I’m rarely a fan of complete re-builds and tend to lean into iterative releases. And the new v20 theme is what I’d call an iterative rollup that’s worthy of a version bump.

Let’s go over the most notable updates to see why.

First and foremost, some of the biggest changes are what you don’t see. As you might imagine, this site has carried over plenty of pieces across 19 years. I decided to excavate the theme and definitely found some ghosts to bust.

For example, the global compiled JavaScript file retained some scripts that just weren’t being used anymore. Things like handling embedded versions of the newsletter from before we began self-hosting the posts. There were relics that catered specifically to the Flexbox Guide for handling toggles back before we had proper HTML

. There was a jQuery lazy-loader thing that is well-covered by WordPress core now. We had some scripts for serving third-party ads that are no longer needed now that we’re self-hosting ads. Things like that. The overall JavaScript was nicely trimmed from 151.6 KB (55.3 KB gzipped) to 133.8 KB (48.1 KB).

I also decided to formally retire the CSS-Tricks Forum. We put it in “archive mode” back in, like, 2018. None of those discussions are particularly valuable for today as they were over 10 years ago, so I deactivated the bbPress plugin that powered it and cleared out the database… and wow, that carved a whopping 400 MB from the ol’ database, not to mention it allowed me to drop several custom templates from the theme.

There were more ghosts busted along the way and, to be sure, there are still some lurking around. But it’s way less scary poking around the theme directory now, and further cleanup should be a lot easier from here on out.

Yeah, yeah, raise your hand if you’re still running Gulp. We are 🙋‍♂️. That’s not a bad thing in and of itself, but keeping it in a fixed state for all this time is not the greatest. I think we’re sporting a more modern workflow than before, even if there’s still room to grow. A smattering of the related commits:

For years, I endured the same errors every time I fired up a new terminal. Those are gone and things run super cleanly, not to mention quickly. Very happy here.

Oof, this is everyone’s favorite job, right?! Aside from being able to spot and fix a few unescaped custom field calls here and there, I decided to ask Claude to help me with a holistic audit. I prompted it to review recent WordPress security release notes and identify areas where the theme could be hardened to comply with what WordPress is doing.

Whoa, you used AI? Yeah, I sure did. But for things like this where it could be my little gopher and allow me more time to do what I do best: front-end. I’m still able to make most of the patches myself based on the output recommendations, but it’s nice to have a gut-check for things where I’m clearly no expert, like security.

I still use and like Sass very much. I mean, yes, I would like to be straight-up vanilla CSS everywhere, but I can’t tear myself away from partials in particular.

The previous theme had a fairly nice “flat” structure — very few partial directories and most things all in the same top-level source. Totally cool. But an organization nut like me (you should see my house) has to have a place for everything to live. It ain’t perfect, but I’m happy with what I was able to put together without leveling the building in the process.

There are some compromises. Like, /header should really be in /layout. The ordering is really important, though, and some of my ideals were simply not possible. I’m hoping to eventually work in some sort of Cascade Layers structure.

I’m pleased as punch to see the overall size of CSS drop in the process. It went from 180 KB (31.7 KB) to 169 KB (24.6 KB). Not hugely significant, but not not significant either. I had to do a couple tricky things to cheat my way there, like serving all the form styles I’m about to talk about into a separate forms.css file and serving it just on pages where WooCommerce, Restrict Content Pro, and Gravity Forms are enqueued.

Are you starting to notice a trend? We’re still talking about fairly invisible work. CSS-Tricks has so many nooks and crannies that it’s nearly impossible to make things perfectly consistent across the board. But because of those nooks and crannies, so many fixed values, magic numbers, one-off styles, and older utilities were hiding about. I decided to tackle a good chunk of that while reorganizing the Sass.

For example, there are now semantic design tokens that abstract away one-off colors. So many grays, let me tell you. Now there’s a maintainable palette that’s referenced throughout. Same with fonts, font styles, and font sizes. Same with paddings and margins. It feels a lot better to make a change at the “system” level with confidence that the change is taking effect everywhere it needs to.

But this is also where some clearly visible changes happened! Most notably, link styles are consistent all over the place with a small handful of intended and maintained variations. Not only is it more pleasing visually, but it improves accessibility throughout because the inherited styles default to proper visual cues that do not rely solely on color or opacity changes.

Oh, and my favorite piece of this? Forms and tables have both been redone. That’s tough to do in a WordPress theme where these components don’t always come from one source. For example, forms could come from any one of the Gravity Forms, WooCommerce, or Restrict Content Pro plugins, and they all have their own stylesheets. Resetting those allowed me to bring a certain level of harmony in all those instances.

OK, something even more visual. This is the first time CSS-Tricks has had more than one color scheme! It certainly could have been done a long time ago, but I’d also argue that the design hasn’t really called for a light/dark toggle over the last 8 years or so. It’s a nice blend of both light and dark elements that marry the best of both worlds no matter your theme preference.

But I had to take a stab, right? The site has predominantly been dark despite the content being against a white background, so I treated things as though a “dark” mode already existed. The result is a creamy light theme that is largely just a change in background and component styles. And I used some of what I learned from the recent “toggle” debate to handle system, dark, and light themes with a single toggle.

What hasn’t changed is the main content container you see in things like articles. That was already white, even in a “dark” mode, and I’m not confident that simply toggling it between white and black is the best approach. Something on the radar for sure.

Oh, and it’s all wired up to a view transition that slides the new theme over the previous one.

I hope you think the site’s header and footer are cute. Yes, cute. That’s what I was going for when adding a colorful series of dots as the backdrop.

Sorta like a subtle candy texture, right? Or a colorful group of stars? I dunno, but I’m calling it “Jawbreaker” for lack of a better term and because it reminds me of the jawbreaker candy I loved as a kid. Naming still sucks.

It’s all part of the same SVG. I really, really (really) wanted to random()-ly position dots around, but that would have taken a lot of empty individual elements (e.g., ) in the markup. Not my favorite. So, I generate the elements in PHP:

I really, really wanted to random()-ize all the things, but inlining random coordinates on each was the way to go. Same deal with the inlined styles that set a fill, and sizing for each circle in the texture, and variables that set a --delay and --dur on the subtle animation that twinkles them in and out.

I’ll have to look at generating the SVG using or whatever later since that pattern repeats in the footer, too.

That’s carried through to the 404 page as well:

If your browser supports border-shape and random() (only Chrome, I think), then the homepage gives you a blobby-blob shape behind the grid of author avatars. Hover it and it gets, you know, even blobbier.

It takes an existing pattern I’m calling .gradient-card:

…and sets a gradient on the component’s :before pseudo:

That sits underneath the :after pseudo that’s translucent, allowing the :before‘s gradient to show through a smidge:

The differing border-radius values allow :before to bleed outside, giving the impression of a border around the entire deal. That’s the basic shape. The border-shape is where things get blobby:

Those variables? They’re mapped to shape()s. I grabbed the first one from Temani Afif’s blob generator:

It’s called “static” because that’s the default state. Hover or focus on the component and it swaps out those points out with a “hover” version of the shape:

Those variables? They’re mapped to random() values:

So, the hover state is different on each page load. And we animate between the two:

I’m pretty stoked on this one. The Almanac has grown, evolved, and just plain gotten great the past few years. But the primary page for it… not so great. It was definitely overshadowed by work on the pages inside it and never revisited beyond clickable cards to get to each feature subset.

Now the page is a real index of the CSS features that gets you where you need to go quickly. Someone somewhere sometime did some sort of CSS periodic table. I forget where I’d seen that but the general idea sat with me and I thought I could pull it off. There’s nothing “tricky” about it but I love what the layout adds to the overall site.

This is another area that sorely needed love. Our guides have always been a vertical stack of items. The book effect was definitely cool, but I started believing that leaning on it as the main visual hook was holding things back from being more functional. With something like 27 guides, we’re talking about a lot of scrolling with no clear idea of what you’re going to see as you go.

Again, nothing “tricky” but I like the overall usefulness of the new layout. There’s a featured guide, a widget of newly added and updated guides, and a categorized grid of everything below that.

The pages that show all of an author’s published articles got a little spruce-up, too.

It’s a little change, really. The right sidebar was moved to the left and the author image and bio moved in, allowing the articles to creep up to the top. I hope to do more here since the small layout tweak opens up more opportunities.

Also need to do the larger Authors index, but another time.

This one’s just for those of us working in the WordPress editor. I don’t need a complete visual editor to get things done, but it is a nice affordance to have some of the front-end styles sneak into the editing UI for a better idea of what you’re working with.

Using Lighthouse to measure things like performance, accessibility, and best practices is woefully fraught with caveats, but there’s no reason to chuck it completely. I find them more fun than fact and I don’t mind the recommendations for improving things.

So, after a few runs and tweaks, here’s how things shook out theme-to-theme.

I’m happy here. There are some things I just don’t have the control to fix when it comes to performance, so seeing v20 come in consistent with v19 is just fine by me. I’m happier with the boosts to accessibility and best practices. SEO? Meh. Don’t really care unless it’s hurting more important things like accessibility.

Again, very happy. There’s little I can do with time to first byte but everything else seems to clock in at least marginally better. The total page weight is a clear win and that’s probably what I care most about here.

Oh gosh, there’s a lot more but they’re mostly minor things that don’t bubble up to the front end. Sort of the same way browser changelogs have some generic line of “bug squashing” or whatever. In this case, it’s things like better post queries and breadcrumbs and function management and naming and… you get it.

There’s definitely so much more to do. And plenty of things I’m sure I missed or broke along the way. That’s just life and those will have to come in another iterative release less worthy of a version bump. You can let me know what I muffed up in the comments.

I hope you at least like where this is going!

Michael Carrick told Man Utd star was ‘playing for himself’ in Aston Villa draw

📅 2026-11-09  |  Read Source →

Troy Deeney took aim at Amad Diallo following Manchester United’s 4-4 draw with Aston Villa, claiming the winger ‘flattered to deceive’ and appeared to be ‘playing for himself’.

United twice fought back from two goals down to come away from Old Trafford with a point in Sunday’s thriller at Old Trafford, but the result saw Michael Carrick’s men enter the international break in the relegation zone.

The hosts were punished for their sluggishness early on as Alysson and Nicolas Jackson made it 2-0 inside only four minutes, only for Bruno Fernandes and Youri Tielemans to find the net and draw level.

The Red Devils were once again punished for a slow start, though, as John McGinn converted a 48th-minute penalty before Leon Goretzka capitalised on some calamitous defending from Leny Yoro to restore the visitors’ two-goal cushion.

Staring down the barrel of a fifth Premier League defeat, Carrick rang the changes in search of a route back into the contest in the second half – and it was two of United’s substitutes, Joshua Zirkzee and Patrick Dorgu, who struck to salvage a draw at the death.

Read exclusive analysis of the biggest stories in football with Metro's weekly football newsletter In The Mixer. Sign up now for free.

But with his side hovering just one point above rock-bottom Crystal Palace, Carrick will know the scrutiny and speculation surrounding his future is likely to rumble on ahead of the club’s crunch match away to bitter rivals Liverpool in a fortnight’s time.

‘I spoke about this on our last episode on Thursday, I feel like we’re doing the same things with Manchester United,’ ex-Watford and Birmingham striker Deeney said of United’s latest performance on talkSPORT’s Premier League All Access Podcast.

There had been calls for Carrick to field Shea Lacey in place of Amad following the youngster’s outstanding cameo display in United’s midweek Champions League victory over Roma.

However, the United head coach stuck to his guns and kept faith with Amad, who would go on to have a tough afternoon at the Theatre of Dreams.

‘Amad comes in and flatters to deceive,’ Deeney said of the Ivorian’s display.

‘A lot of running into nothing, he was… today it felt like a lot of them were playing for themselves and going, “Look, I’m not the problem!”

‘Amad had about nine shots in the first half that were never going to be on target.’

On United’s problems in the centre of the park, Deeney continued: ‘I saw a midfield that was empty and vacated today.

‘Villa went through that midfield at will, many times in that first 75 minutes.’

Unai Emery made five substitutions before United’s late fightback, with goalscorers McGinn, Alysson, Goretzka and Jackson all removed, as well as the lively Johan Manzambi.

Asked whether the Villa manager had made a mistake with his changes, Deeney replied: ‘Absolutely.

‘I also thought it was one of the few times where he’d probably be very disappointed and would want to go back when you look at taking off Johan Manzambi, who I thought was excellent.

‘His ability to not only roll the player and keep the ball, but buy fouls and take them ten, 15, 20 yards up the pitch was excellent.

‘I think the game got too easy for Villa at 4-2 and I thought Emery started to think a little bit too far ahead, “Okay, I can rest these players”, obviously going away to international break.

‘Nicolas Jackson at times looked like Kylian Mbappe against that Manchester United team. He was dropping team, running in behind, he was 15 yards away from everyone.

Facing the media shortly after the final whistle, Carrick jumped to Dalot’s defence and encouraged supporters to get behind the players, especially when they are playing out of position.

‘Diogo, he gives absolutely everything,’ Carrick said.

‘He’s played in different positions, he’s played left-back, he’s played really well at times. He gives a penalty away today, that’s, you know, he knows that.

‘Maybe he can do a little bit better, all right, that’s part of it, he’s made a mistake, but he cares an awful lot and he’s giving everything.’

On Dalot, the United boss went on: ‘I like what he brings to the team, I like what he brings to the group, I like what he brings to the whole environment and the culture that we’ve got.

‘Picking a team and who plays the next game, that’s a whole different decision, but it’s a whole different game that we’ve got to look forward to now.’

Nils Koppen deserves credit as West Ham ‘dodged huge summer transfer bullet’

📅 2026-11-09  |  Read Source →

There is a tendency to lump every player together in a failing team and declare them all unfit for purpose, as the West Ham fans’ much publicised chanting at Ewood Park might suggest. However, the boiling over of a couple of years of frustration and anger, aimed at the current occupants of the claret and blue shirts, need not be taken as voicing disapproval of the whole squad.

In fact, there are some positive signs – Ezra Mayers, as we wrote elsewhere this morning- gives us all cause for optimism.

I’d wager that another starter against Blackburn, Edvin Austbø is perhaps wondering about now what kind of hell he has signed up for as one of the Hammers’ youngest summer recruits.

However his is another fledgling career that looks to be hugely promising. Against Blackburn, the young winger looked prepared to take on and beat players in a way that his more seasoned colleague Manor Solomon appears to have forgotten.

Remember, West Ham were linked with Stoke City’s winger  Sorba Thomas in the summer – an ex Hammer who has in fact gone to Premier League Hull City and struggled enormously – so much so that footballleagueworld.co.uk claim ‘West Ham dodged huge £10.5 million transfer bullet’:

West Ham made a pretty shrewd decision by backing away from Sorba Thomas and investing in Edvin Austø

We may think things are bad right now, but with youngster Austbo West Ham look to have found a young talent with a high ceiling – compared with the -relatively speaking – wizened old pro’ Thomas who at 27 seems to have peaked, if Hull City’s experience is anything to go by. Good choice, Nils Koppen.

Very easy to hate on all things West Ham just now with the club sitting outside even the play off places. Austbø however continues to shine even on dark days at Ewood Park and needs to be nailed-on as a starter in place of Solomon who, by comparison, just looked plain ordinary.

From the old Bobby Moore Upper to the Billy Bonds' stand these days - I've been watching since '03 and a supporter since about 1970.. Favourite player - Dean Ashton: Curse you Shaun Wright-Phillips. I still watch YouTube repeats of the Cup Final of 2006 hoping in vain that Shaka Hislop grows another six inches and stops Steven Gerrard's injury time equaliser. Can tell I'm getting old knowing I watched both Mark Noble's debut and his last game at West Ham. Pulling on a Claret and Blue replica shirt still makes me feel the same butterflies as when I was seven years old. Magic.

The fans are the ones who are a credit to our club Weve proved our loyalty time and time again Waiting to see it from management owners and players

Credit for signing a player who has done what exactly in the second division so far? £20M on Engles, £5m on Solomon and £10m for Piroe had we gone up? I see nothing here to give him credit for. I was chatting to a guy Saturday who told me he thinks West Ham are the easiest team to play through in the league. He reckoned our midfield had zero defensive qualities and constantly left our back four exposed. The more I think about it, the more I see what he is saying. If we had no intention of playing Alvarez or Magassa, why didn’t we sign a midfielder who can defend or tackle because none of the current bunch can.

Promotion has to be the key aim this season, but it was always going to be a reset after the torrid few seasons and incompetent leadership at the club under Sullivan and his managers. I was ok with Nuno staying on, but hindsight always being clear 20/20 vision, I think it was Paco Jémez that was the factor in West Ham’s improvement towards the back end of last season. I hope Parker brings the feelgood factor back to the squad, last seen during the high times of Moyse. Stating the bleeding obvious, there’s still time to push for that automatic spot, but no room now for what we’ve been witnessing recently if it’s going to happen for us.

Luka Vuskovic has been a standout performer in a massively impressive Brighton side this season.

After shining in Germany on loan with Hamburg last season, Vuskovic joined the Seagulls in a permanent move from Tottenham, and the 19-year-old has been a sensation.

There has been plenty of speculation over who could replace Virgil van Dijk, who is into the final year of his current deal, and Vuskovic has played his way into that conversation.

📅 2026-11-09  |  Read Source →

Barcelona stay top of LaLiga after a 2-1 win at Atlético Madrid on Matchday 12. The result shapes the title race through the international break, with Real Madrid now their nearest challengers.

Barcelona came from a goal down to win the meeting of LaLiga’s top two at the Metropolitano. Giuliano Simeone gave Atlético Madrid the lead. Atlético had arrived with nine wins, one draw and one defeat from their first 11 league matches. Barcelona had scored 46 league goals in that same stretch.

The comeback changed the shape of the title race. Losing would have left Barcelona two points behind Atlético, the club’s match report noted. Real Madrid had drawn level at the top by winning at Valencia the day before. Going into Sunday, Barcelona had 30 points and Atlético sat second on 28.

Atlético started strongly. Inside the first seven minutes, Joan García saved when Julián Álvarez was through one-on-one. The goalkeeper also denied Lee Kang-in. Before kick-off, a minute’s silence was held for Jesús de Miguel. Play was briefly stopped again after Atlético’s opening goal for another tribute.

Barcelona drew level on 45 minutes. The equaliser followed a poor clearance by Marc Pubill, and Fermín’s finish beat Jan Oblak. For Fermín, that goal made Barcelona feel freer to play their own game and win.

Hansi Flick reshaped his side at half-time. Frenkie de Jong came on at right-back for Brian Fariñas. Barcelona pressed higher after the break. On 66 minutes, Lamine Yamal’s pass set up Fermín for the winner.

The result carries extra weight for Barcelona. Atlético beat them in the Champions League quarter-finals and in the Copa del Rey last season.

In the European tie, Atlético took a 2-0 first-leg win in Barcelona. A 2-1 Barça victory at the Metropolitano on 14 April 2026 did not prevent their exit. The Copa del Rey semi-final followed a similar pattern. Barcelona’s 3-0 second-leg win came after a 4-0 defeat in the first leg.

XRP Whales Are Quietly Loading Up as Millionaire Wallets Hit Highest Level of 2026; Here’s Why It Matters

📅 2026-11-09  |  Read Source →

XRP whales appear to be making a bold move while much of the crypto market struggles to regain its footing. Despite lingering price pressure, the number of wallets holding substantial amounts of the cryptocurrency continues to climb, pointing to a potentially significant shift in large-holder behavior. The development has caught the attention of market watchers, raising a bigger question: are deep-pocketed investors quietly positioning themselves for XRP’s next major move?

Data from blockchain analytics platform Santiment shows that the number of XRP wallets holding at least 1 million tokens has climbed to 2,147, marking the highest level recorded in 2026.

The trend has accelerated in recent days, with 28 new wallets joining the so-called millionaire club over the past 10 days alone, including 18 in the last three days. The steady increase suggests large XRP holders may be using the recent price dip to build positions, rather than retreating amid market uncertainty.

Santiment has also highlighted the $1.20-$1.25 range as a critical support zone for XRP. According to the analytics platform, the bullish outlook would remain intact if the token defends this price area while the number of wallets holding at least 1 million XRP stays above 2,140. Maintaining both conditions could suggest that large-holder confidence remains firm despite ongoing market volatility.

While XRP’s price action continues to attract attention, several developments beneath the surface could shape its longer-term outlook. Beyond whale accumulation, growing institutional exposure, expanding activity across the XRP Ledger (XRPL), and declining exchange reserves are emerging as important factors for bulls to watch. Here are three potential catalysts that could strengthen the case for a sustained XRP recovery.

One less obvious development supporting XRP’s bullish outlook is continued interest from institutional investors through exchange-traded funds. Despite recent price volatility, regulated investment products offer traditional investors another route to gain exposure to the cryptocurrency.

Blockworks’ Q3 2026 XRP report highlighted that global XRP exchange-traded products attracted roughly $355 million in net inflows during the quarter, representing a 40% increase from the previous quarter. Assets under management also climbed 65% to $3.3 billion.

Another potential catalyst is the growing activity around Ripple’s dollar-pegged stablecoin, RLUSD. Rather than competing directly with XRP, the stablecoin could help expand the range of financial activities taking place on the XRP Ledger, particularly payments, liquidity management, and institutional settlement.

A third development worth watching is the movement of XRP away from centralized exchanges. When investors withdraw tokens from trading platforms, it can reduce immediately available supply, potentially easing selling pressure if demand picks up.

As ZyCrypto covered earlier, Binance’s XRP reserves fell from approximately 2.69 billion to 2.47 billion between October 28 and October 30. Bybit also recorded a notable decline in its XRP reserves, with holdings falling from 306 million to 286 million tokens, representing an outflow of 20 million XRP.

The bullish case is simple: long-term holders appear to be absorbing available supply, while growing institutional interest, expanding XRPL adoption, and declining exchange reserves provide additional tailwinds for XRP.

A penalty from Bruno Fernandes (16) and a Youri Tielemans strike (44) levelled the scores before half-time, though.

Dramatically, though, Manchester United somehow stole a point with two injury-time strikes from Joshua Zirkzee and Patrick Dorgu.

We have to defend better in those positions. In the last 10 minutes of each half, we have to manage better.

“We have a lot of inexperienced players but a lot of talent, and that's why we score goals. It doesn't matter if United are low on confidence; you're always up against it. We lost two points. We threw it away. The last five minutes were just madness. It's going to sting. The manager is a winner; he'll be hurting the way we are. We go play international football but he'll dwell on it for two weeks.”

The thrilling draw means McGinn’s side are winless since their 2-1 triumph over Manchester City on October 24, leaving them in 10th place in the English Premier League. Meanwhile, their hosts are now the only side in the league to have won just one game this season, leaving them in 18th place and amongst the relegation spots as pressure mounts on head coach Michael Carrick.

However, United did set a little piece of history during the chaotic 4-4 draw with Unai Emery’s side, as they became the first team in English Premier League history to trail by two goals on two separate occasions within a single match and successfully fight back to level the score both times.

The Partenopei currently occupy 10th place in the Serie A after losing four of their opening 11 games, and looked set to fall to a fifth defeat when their unbeaten visitors took the lead through Davide Fratessi after 28 minutes.

Napoli took a share of the spoils though in strange circumstances when substitute Alfonso Pedraza smashed beyond his own goalkeeper while under pressure from McTominay just three minutes after being introduced as a half-time substitute. Already 14 points behind the league leaders, pressure is mounting on head coach Massimiliano Allegri, but remained defiant after the stalemate.

“Let us remember we are not out of Serie A, the Coppa Italia or the Champions League yet," said Allegri. “There are difficult moments over the course of a campaign. I believe we need to deal with them in a spirit of calm. When we are in the middle of the storm, we manage to get the boat to shore somehow.

Strops, scruff, and chaos

📅 2026-11-09  |  Read Source →

I watched Man Utd v Aston Villa last night. When Villa conceded late on to let a two goal lead slip for the second time in the game, Unai Emery threw a strop and went down the tunnel. It’s not the first time he’s eschewed a handshake with the opposition manager, and his post-game interview where he insisted he thought the game was over was very funny because it was such obvious bullshit.

This is someone who has managed over 1000 games in his career. I’m pretty sure he knows when the final whistle blows and when it doesn’t. Can you imagine if Mikel Arteta did that? You wouldn’t be able to move for all the opinion pieces about what a sore loser he is and all the rest. But perhaps Emery gets a pass because he is ‘the best manager in the league’. Anyway, perhaps it provides a stay of execution for Michael Carrick at Man Utd, which can only be a good thing.

Meanwhile, it looks as if Pierre Sage is gonna get fired by Crystal Palace and replaced by Thomas Frank. That seems a sensible appointment to me. Enough time has passed since his spell at Sp*rs for the toxins to wear off, and he’s clearly a good manager. I’ve never been convinced by Sage, I just think he looks too scruffy. As if someone got a 10 year old to draw man and brought it to life.

I quite enjoyed this quote from Mikel Arteta in his post-game press conference after Saturday’s 1-0 win over Hull:

Sometimes we try to score the perfect goal against low blocks, but we need to be much more chaotic than that. If we open the scoring much earlier also, it changes the game.

I love it as a concept, adding some chaos to our control makes a lot of sense, but it also kinda seems at odds with what the manager asks of his players. Maybe he just means more shots from distance, maybe riskier pass when we’re trying to break the opposition down, or maybe there’s something he’s got cooking for over the Interlull and when we return we see something we never expected. He did kick the shit out of a dugout chair on Saturday, that’s a new enough development to be fair.

I wonder was he referring to the Kai Havertz chance in the first half, when he could have shot on his right foot but tried to cut back/lay it off. If you shoot, maybe you don’t score but as we saw with the winning goal from Martin Odegaard, it might end up in the path of another Arsenal player who can finish it off. Perhaps too there’s a bit of a through line between being more chaotic and what Noni Madueke is doing at the moment. He can be a bit scattergun, but as Nick Wright points out on Sky Sports, his numbers are improving. I don’t think Arteta will ever embrace full chaos, all the time, but if he can add a little more to the mix, it could certainly help.

Right, I have to leave it there for this morning. A bit shorter than normal, I know. We will have an Arsecast Extra for you a bit later on though. We’ve already put out the call for questions on BlueSky @gunnerblog.bsky.social and @arseblog.com, so fire away using the hashtag #arsecastextra – or if you’re an Arseblog Member on Patreon, leave your question in the #arsecast-extra-questions channel on our Discord server.

The pod should be out around around lunchtime. Until then, have a good one.
