Stellar Eyes XLM Tokenomics Overhaul to Give Rewards to Holders and Users, Chief Product Officer Reveals

📅 2026-11-09  |  Read Source →

Stellar is preparing a tokenomics overhaul as its DeFi activity accelerates, aiming to introduce a user rewards mechanism funded through its transaction fees.

This week, Stellar Network’s Chief Product Officer revealed a major development that could be huge for the protocol and XLM. Speaking at the Meridian 2026 conference on Thursday, Tomer Weller said the blockchain company is preparing to overhaul XLM’s tokenomics structure.

While addressing the audience at the Meridian event, Weller said Stellar is getting ready to reorganize XLM’s tokenomics. The tokenomics overhaul proposal aims to give value to XLM holders and reduce token supply.

At the event, the executive said that by quarter 1 2027, Stellar will unveil an important governance transformation related to tokenomics and fully implement it across the network.

He acknowledged that Stellar has had a fixed supply with no inflation or staking rewards since 2019. He therefore said the company is now exploring ways to roll out effective value-capture mechanisms to help generate real profit for XLM holders and users.

According to the executive, the upcoming overhaul plans to use Stellar’s existing network transaction fees to fund user rewards. The initiative is looking at ways to reward user on-chain activity and balances, a move that aims to encourage customers to hold and use XLM.

The upgrade is still under deliberation, and governance voting will begin soon, with implementation targeted for Q1 2027 if approved.

According to the executive, the upcoming overhaul initiative will rewrite XLM tokenomics and redesign the supply-and-demand dynamics of the tokens. The tokenomics reform focuses on sharing existing transaction fees with holders and users instead of minting new tokens. This marks a shift from ‘how to issue more tokens’ to ‘how to decrease supply and create a connection between network revenue and token value’.

As disclosed above, Stellar is getting ready for a significant tokenomics upgrade, a major governance decision that could redefine its token economics.

This proposal matters because it aims to decrease token dilution, strengthen XLM’s scarcity narrative, and give value to token holders.

By reducing inflation through governance, this program focuses on ecosystem growth. If the proposal passes and its implementation is executed, it is set to become a significant bullish long-term catalyst for XLM, driving market demand and network adoption.

pearce+ co-builds guerrilla shading pavilion from salvaged timber and canvas sails in camden

📅 2026-11-09  |  Read Source →

architecture practice pearce+ completes the guerrilla shading pavilion for the camden inspire festival, an open, mobile cooling structure.

EE17 edited by christina vergopoulou November 9, 2026 3 min

Commissioned by Camden Town Unlimited and Euston Town BIDs for the Camden Inspire festival, architecture practice PEARCE+ collaborated with advocacy group Shade The UK and local volunteers to co-build the Guerrilla Shading pavilion. Sited at Camden Collective in London, the structure responds to escalating urban temperatures and the urban heat island effect, functioning as reusable, mobile shade infrastructure designed for continuous reassembly rather than single-event use.

The primary framework comprises five prefabricated panels sized for transport in a standard transit van. Engineered with a tapering profile to counter wind loads transferred by cantilevered tensile canopies, the structure utilizes structural timber salvaged from previous PEARCE+ installations. The lower register of the frame is intentionally left open, enabling visitors to manually weave an enclosure using reclaimed balloon fabric supplied by Cameron Hot Air Balloons and Inflate Studio. Tensioned naturally dyed canvas sails, developed in collaboration with artist Auda Sakho, project outward from the core frame to expand the shaded footprint over the surrounding garden. Complementing the physical pavilion, Camden Inspire and PEARCE+ Studio published Guerrilla Shading Our City, an open-source guide detailing low-cost shading tactics, ranging from DIY external shutters and tensile sails to urban tree planting and municipal planning permissions.

Guerrilla Shading pavilion is a co-built urban cooling structure designed by PEARCE+the structural skeleton is formed by five prefabricated modular timber panelsthe installation functions as reusable, demountable infrastructure

name: Guerrilla Shading Pavilion for Camden Inspire Festival architect: PEARCE+ | @pearce_plus

design team: Owen Hughes Pearce, Ken Pearce, Sam Bray, Charlotte Martin, Hannah Innes, Tim Lyddon

structural engineering: Root Workshop & Momentum Structural Engineers

material donations: Cameron Balloons and Inflate Studio

event: Camden Inspire Festival | @camdeninspire

thanks to: Georgie, Eve and the Camden Unlimited team, and everyone who participated in the public workshops

Nearby Dwarf Galaxies Show Surprising Lack Of Oxygen, Says Paper

📅 2026-11-09  |  Read Source →

Vital to almost all life as we know it, oxygen has long been valued as a tracer of the chemical evolution of the of the cosmos. As such, it provides a practical means of tracing how the heavy elements of iron, carbon, nickel, magnesium and sulfur --- the real building blocks of habitable planets like our own --- are distributed throughout the galaxy and beyond.

In a paper published in The Astrophysical Journal, researchers using Hubble studied 37 nearby dwarf galaxies â small galaxies containing a few thousand to hundreds of millions of stars â and found that much of the oxygen they expected to see is missing, says NASA.

For the first time, we have measured both how much oxygen produced by stars in low-mass galaxies remains in those galaxies, and how much oxygen has been lost from those galaxies throughout their lifetimes, Grace Telford, the paperâs second author and an assistant professor at the University of Utahâs Dept. of Physics & Astronomy, told me via email.

The findings show that dwarf galaxies can hold onto only a small fraction of the oxygen made by stars, says NASA.

The team used Hubble Space Telescope data; data from NASAâs retired Spitzer Space Telescope as well as the ground based Very Large Array and Australian Telescope Compact Array, NASA notes.

Most of the oxygen does come from core-collapse supernovae, which are the explosive deaths of massive stars, says Telford. Yet a very small fraction is also produced in other types of supernovae caused by the remnants of low-mass stars becoming unstable or merging, she says.

As the cosmosâ third most abundant element, oxygen is relatively easy to detect and measure, and its production is well understood â making it a reliable indicator of how chemical elements are distributed in a galaxy, says NASA.

We found that typically 15 percent of the oxygen produced by stars in these low-mass galaxies is still found inside those galaxies today: mostly in their gas, but small fractions are also in the stars and dust, says Telford. The explanation is that energetic radiation from massive stars and their deaths as supernovae drive oxygen-rich gas out of the galaxies over time, she says.

This means that the missing oxygen is likely in the gaseous halos surrounding the galaxies. That is, the Circumgalactic Medium (CGM). This missing oxygen has also likely traveled even farther into the intergalactic medium.

The Galaxies Losing Oxygen via Winds (GLOW) project, jointly funded by NASA and the National Science Foundation, is a large-scale effort to understand how gas and heavy elements move between low-mass galaxies, their CGM, and the intergalactic medium.

To do this requires combining a large set of new and archival observations of low-mass galaxies from both ground-based and space telescopes, says Telford.

The idea is that most of the oxygen found outside these dwarf galaxies has been driven there by energy from massive stars and supernovae explosions.

Similar measurements of oxygen (and other heavy elements) lost from galaxies have been made for massive galaxies like the Milky Way, says Telford. But low-mass galaxies are expected to lose oxygen more easily due to their smaller gravity and our paper demonstrates that this is indeed the case, she says.

In the Milky Way, there is about one oxygen atom for every 2,000 hydrogen atoms, says Telford. In low-mass galaxies like those in the GLOW project, that value is more like one oxygen atom for every 10,000-20,000 hydrogen atoms, she says.

The GLOW project found that low-mass galaxies lose more oxygen than current galaxy formation simulations predict, implying that we donât yet have a complete understanding of how massive stars interact with their host galaxies, says Telford.

Over the last three and a half decades, award-winning science journalist Bruce Dorminey --- a former Forbes senior science contributor, former Hong Kong Bureau Chief at Aviation Week and Space Technology magazine and former Paris-based technology correspondent for the Financial Times, has written for a multitude of high profile publications. They include Astronomy Magazine, Sky and Telescope, Scientific American, Science, Nature News, National Geographic, and Universe Today. He is the author of the 2001 book "Distant Wanderers: The Search for Planets beyond the Solar System." He was also the host of the "Cosmic Controversy" podcast which is still available via Podbean.

Adam Back Says Bitcoin Is Entering a Third Adoption Wave Led by Banks and Pension Funds

📅 2026-11-09  |  Read Source →

The CEO said Bitcoin is entering the third phase of global adoption, as interest from banks suggests BTC’s institutional demand is rising.

Bitcoin is about to enter its ‘third phase’ of global adoption, according to Adam Back, the CEO of Blockstream. In an interview this week (as tracked by Wu Blockchain), the legendary British cryptographer said BTC is preparing to enter this new growth phase. He termed it the ‘third phase’ of international adoption.

As per the CEO’s revelation, Bitcoin is entering a new phase of adoption. The executive indicated that the asset has had two stages of adoption. The first phase ran from 2011 to 2019. During this period, the decentralized currency attracted individuals to buy it and miners to mine it. During this time, BTC’s price surpassed $1 and later reached hundreds of dollars.

The second phase has been an institutional era from 2020 to the present. Institutional interest increased massively during this period, and BTC surged to a record $69,000 in November 2021, then climbed above $100,000 in August 2025. Institutional participation grew as ETFs validated Bitcoin’s legitimacy and made it easier for traditional investors to access.

In the podcast, Back said that the flagship cryptocurrency is on the verge of entering its ‘third wave’ of global adoption. According to the CEO, this period is unique and different from other phases. In this evolving era, traditional banks and other recognized investment institutions are preparing to further drive global Bitcoin adoption, the executive said.

As Back noted, the ‘third phase’ of institutional Bitcoin adoption is primed to be fueled by major investment banks and professional asset management channels, whose participation has yet to fully materialize.

The CEO revealed that some major investment banks have recommended a 2%-4% Bitcoin allocation in their model portfolios. Many mutual funds, pension funds, and insurance products now allow customers to gain indirect exposure to Bitcoin through their investment vehicles. In addition, Bitcoin treasury companies increasingly play this role.

Through this expanding third wave of adoption, Back believes that Bitcoin will be bought by the “world’s largest pools of capital.” He predicts that BTC will reach parity with Gold this cycle at a valuation of $1.5 million per coin.

Crypto’s $1B flash crash: What just happened and why it matters

📅 2026-11-09  |  Read Source →

Read the best crypto stories of the day in less than 5 minutes. Join to get it daily in your inbox.

Read the best crypto stories of the day in less than 5 minutes. Join to get it daily in your inbox.

A massive flash crash has shaken the crypto market.

Nothing reflects this better than the OFFICIAL TRUMP coin. From a technical standpoint, TRUMP just hit a new all-time low of $1.13, marking a 98.56% drop from its peak.

To put that into perspective, a $10,000 investment made in January 2025 would now be worth just $144. That’s a brutal drawdown, highlighting the scale of the ongoing sell-off.

However, there is more to the damage than just price. TRUMP’s collapse highlights an increase in macroeconomic uncertainty and a change in sentiment toward the U.S. crypto market.

Reinforcing this, Bitcoin’s Coinbase Premium Index continues to trade in the negative, indicating that there is less demand from U.S. investors. With both signals flashing caution, the upcoming macro week could prove critical.

As highlighted above, six key events are set to take center stage this week.

According to AMBCrypto, the inflation numbers for October could cause another crypto wipeout. After a sharp 9% jump in oil prices in September, which caused 0.3% monthly inflation, the entire crypto market dropped 9% in October.

Given oil prices rose a further 15% in October, inflation risks remain elevated. A hotter-than-expected reading could add fresh macro pressure, putting risk assets under further stress.

This brings the focus to the recent $1 billion crypto flash crash. With a major macro week ahead, rising inflation risks, and market sentiment deep in extreme fear, is this wipeout beyond just a deleveraging event?

The technical picture is starting to reflect wider market sentiment.

As mentioned above, the fall of Official TRUMP [TRUMP] demonstrates the increasing amount of FUD in the U.S. crypto market, which has been exacerbated by the Democrats winning the mid-term elections.

In this context, the recent crypto wipeout could be more than just a healthy deleveraging phase. Instead, it points to weakening investor confidence and a broader shift in market momentum.

On-chain data further supports this trend. As the chart below highlights, the Crypto Fear and Greed Index fell from 50 to 29 (indicating a change from neutral to fearful) in under a week. It is now only 4 away from indicating extreme fear.

With the risk of a crypto wipeout still elevated, a further drop into “extreme” fear remains on the table.

In short, capitulation risks remain high.

This puts November’s crypto rally in danger. Despite the recent carnage clearing out excessive leverage from the market, weak sentiment and increased macro uncertainty mean that there is potential for further downside, with another crypto sell-off still a possibility.

TRUMP’s 98.56% crash, negative Coinbase Premium, and rising inflation risks highlight weakening confidence in the U.S. crypto market.

The $1 billion wipeout may be more than deleveraging, as extreme fear and macro uncertainty keep further downside and capitulation risks elevated.

Which Foldable Phone Has the Best Battery Life? We Put Them to the Test

📅 2026-11-09  |  Read Source →

Our gifting experts are obsessively searching for holiday gifts that are unique, thoughtful and suitable for any budget.

Our expert, award-winning staff selects the products we cover and rigorously researches and tests our top picks. If you buy through our links, we may earn a commission.

From Apple's iPhone Duo to Samsung's and Motorola's foldables, we tested how today's most popular book-style and flip phones hold up under pressure.

For years, foldable phones haven’t exactly been praised for their battery life. However, that’s starting to change.

Because of their unique design, foldables have traditionally struggled to match the battery life of conventional slab phones. Their massive displays, space-consuming hinge mechanisms and limited room for heat dissipation all present challenges. But battery technology has improved in recent years, with some of the newest foldables benefiting from higher-energy-density silicon-carbon batteries and more power-efficient processors. Even slim phones like the Galaxy Z Fold 8 can last all day, showing that you don’t have to sacrifice practicality for a sleek, innovative design.

Still, foldable phones remain a niche category, even with a major player like Apple entering the market with the iPhone Duo. But they offer an appealing alternative for anyone looking to break away from the sameness of more traditional slab phones. And with battery life being a key consideration when choosing a new device, it’s important to know which phone can keep up with all your scrolling, streaming and snapping without constantly reaching for a charger.

We’ve dug into CNET’s extensive smartphone lab data to find out which foldable phone in 2026 delivers the best battery life. Because book-style and flip phones differ in design and screen size, we’re separating the results to provide a more accurate comparison.

Here’s how devices from Samsung, Apple, Google and Motorola stack up.

This year, CNET has reviewed and tested five book-style foldable phones: the Samsung Galaxy Z Fold 8, Z Fold 8 Ultra, Motorola Razr Fold, Google Pixel 11 Pro Fold and the Apple iPhone Duo. These devices are phone-tablet hybrids, with designs ranging from taller, narrower builds to squatter, passport-style designs. (We didn’t include Samsung’s Galaxy Z TriFold because of its unique design.)

Apple’s $1,999 iPhone Duo may be late to the foldable game, arriving seven years after Samsung’s first Galaxy Fold ushered in the modern era of foldable phones. Still, given Apple’s strong brand loyalty and mainstream appeal, the Duo has generated considerable buzz.

And there’s another reason Apple’s first foldable deserves attention: its battery. The iPhone Duo achieved the highest average score in CNET’s testing among book-style phones. It’s a testament to its dual-cell battery design, which is standard in foldables, and the efficiency of the A20 Pro chip powering the device.

To test a phone’s battery, CNET runs a 3-hour streaming video test and a 45-minute endurance test that includes watching videos, scrolling through social media, playing a game and joining a video call. Both tests take place on the foldable phone’s inner screen, starting with a full charge and while connected to Wi-Fi.

The iPhone Duo had the highest remaining charge after the 45-minute endurance test, finishing with 99%. It ended the 3-hour streaming test with 84%, placing it just below the Razr Fold, Galaxy Z Fold 8 and Z Fold 8 Ultra in that category. That average gives the Duo, which has a 4,883-mAh battery, the number-one place in our foldable battery tests.

Not far behind is the Razr Fold, Motorola’s first book-style foldable. This phone has a whopping 6,000-mAh silicon-carbon battery, which helped it earn one of the top spots in our battery testing.

Samsung’s Galaxy Z Fold 8, which is the company’s first palm-size foldable phone, just barely tops the more standard-size Z Fold 8 Ultra. Both phones are the thinnest in this ranking, which makes their results even more impressive. The Galaxy Z Fold 8 measures 4.5mm thick when unfolded and 9.7mm when folded, and has a 4,800-mAh silicon-carbon battery. The Z Fold 8 Ultra is 4.1mm thick when open and 8.9mm when closed, and it has a 5,000-mAh silicon-carbon battery.

Close behind the competition is Google’s Pixel 11 Pro Fold, which has a 4,806-mAh battery. Like the other phones, including the iPhone Duo, it performed relatively better in the endurance test than in the streaming test. That suggests these devices handle shorter bursts of use well but can take a more noticeable battery hit during prolonged activities like video streaming. Even so, they should easily power through a full day of mixed use, whether you’re watching movies, scrolling through social media or taking photos.

Overall, every book-style foldable we tested demonstrated strong battery results. Beyond the numbers, our hands-on experience suggests you can get through most days without constantly worrying about your phone’s battery level. Be sure to check out CNET’s in-depth reviews for more on the everyday experience using each of these phones.

Some people may prefer the more nostalgic design of a flip phone. Motorola revived its iconic Razr brand for the smartphone era, and now has a handful of devices at varying price points to choose from. Samsung’s Galaxy Z Flip 8 is another pocket-friendly choice.

These clamshell devices have less space for a battery than larger foldable and slab phones. Still, Motorola’s and Samsung’s battery specs are nothing to sniff at.

This year’s $800 Motorola Razr has a 4,800-mAh battery, the $1,100 Razr Plus has a 4,500-mAh battery and the $1,500 Razr Ultra has a 5,000-mAh battery. Meanwhile, the $1,200 Z Flip 8 has a 4,300-mAh battery. Every one of these phones uses a silicon-carbon battery to squeeze more power into a smaller design.

Impressively, the baseline Motorola Razr got the best average score in our battery testing of clamshell phones. It also had the highest result in CNET’s 3-hour streaming test among its peers and tied with the much pricier Razr Ultra in our battery endurance test. The Razr Ultra, Galaxy Z Flip 8 and Razr Plus are in close succession.

Interestingly, the flip phones aren’t that far behind their book-style counterparts in our testing. One key advantage is that their smaller cover and inner displays require less power, helping them keep pace with larger foldables.

The results are also a testament to how battery technology is progressing, with the mobile industry increasingly shifting toward high-density silicon-carbon batteries, which can pack more capacity into tighter dimensions. This means that regardless of whether you choose a book-style or clamshell foldable, you can be assured that your battery won’t leave you in the lurch as you go about your day.

From the newest foldables like the iPhone Duo and Pixel 11 Pro Fold to flip phones from Motorola and Samsung, you no longer have to sacrifice battery life for a more versatile mobile design. Today’s devices can comfortably get you through a full day of swiping, scrolling and snapping photos, even with larger screens to power.

In fact, when ranked among all the phones CNET tested this year, including non-folding options, the iPhone Duo sits among the top five performers, with the iPhone 18 Pro Max taking the top overall spot.

That’s especially important given that a major part of a foldable phone’s appeal is the ability to switch between two displays. You can quickly send a text on a smaller screen, then move to a larger one to watch a movie, for example. That versatility requires a battery capable of handling more intensive tasks like streaming and gaming, as well as extended activities like browsing and working across multiple displays.

And while the iPhone Duo and Motorola Razr topped CNET’s foldable battery tests, the other phones we evaluated weren’t far behind, highlighting just how competitive battery life has become across the leading brands. Ultimately, choosing the right foldable will come down to other factors, including camera performance, design and price.

Our testing data, combined with real-world experiences shared in our in-depth reviews, illustrates just how far foldable phones have evolved. They’re no longer just a novelty; they’ve become increasingly practical devices in their own right.

GPOD on the Road: A Historic Garden Built for Two Sisters

📅 2026-11-09  |  Read Source →

In June 2022, I attended the Garden Club of Evanston’s Garden Walk. I toured several beautiful gardens and learned a bit about their histories, but one I still think about today is the historic sunken garden at the Bichl and Schager houses in nearby Wilmette, Illinois.

The story goes back to 1923, when sisters Helen Bichl and Dorothy Schager had neighboring homes built on their family’s property. Their father, Wendelin Seng, had established a successful furniture-hardware manufacturing business, and architects George W. Maher and his son Philip were commissioned to design the two Italianate-style homes. The houses were built with nearly identical, reversed floor plans and a shared garden connecting them. The original estate also included tennis courts, a swimming pool, and even an underground tunnel between the houses! You can read more about the fascinating history of these homes here.

This is one of the two historic homes. Many of the same plants repeat throughout this side of the garden, including Russian sage (Salvia yangii, Zones 5–9) on the slope near the stairs, clipped boxwood hedges (Buxus cvs., Zones 4–9) lining the gravel paths, and roses (Rosa cvs., Zones 4–9) planted throughout the beds.

Here’s the second house. Both homes have beautiful stone terraces overlooking the garden. What a dream! This side has a greater mix of perennials and shrubs, but still repeats the formal boxwood hedges, roses, and gravel paths connecting the two homes.

A closer look at one of the planters next to the house. I thought the concrete urn really complemented the stonework.

Another view of the sunken garden. Photo 1 shows a staircase similar to the one in this photo, and both lead to the upper garden.

Woodland sage (Salvia nemorosa, Zones 4–8) and lady’s mantle (Alchemilla mollis, Zones 3–8) were a repeated combination throughout the gardens.

Looking back across the garden from near one of the staircases, you can see how densely planted the slopes are. Roses and flowering perennials fill the beds, and the clipped hedges maintain the garden’s formal structure. A work of art!

The upper garden. This angle faces the house in photo 1.

A shady spot to take a break from the June heat! I love how the astilbe (Astilbe cvs., Zones 4–8) foliage creeps beneath the bench here.

A gorgeous stone wall covered in creeping sedum (Sedum cvs., Zones 3–9) and a weathered birdbath. This is on the right side of the staircase in photo 4.

Another surprise in the upper garden was this waterfall tucked beneath the evergreens.

If you’re ever in the Evanston area, I highly recommend attending one of the Garden Club of Evanston’s garden walks. It’s a wonderful opportunity to explore beautiful private gardens and learn a little about their history. I’m certainly glad I had the chance to visit this one!

Track Attack: We Drive the 2028 Audi Nuvolari to the Limit

📅 2026-11-09  |  Read Source →

Unfamiliar surroundings can elicit feelings of uneasiness. We’re in Madrid; we’ve just rolled out of bed and arrived at Circuito de Madrid Jarama. A few minutes pass, and we’re belted into the passenger seat of the Audi’s newest halo car, the Audi Nuvolari, itself a relatively unexplored place. Site laps are sometimes easy, but not today. Our tour guide, Victor, has a crazy look in his eye. And maybe a screw loose. He attacks the braking zones, exits corners sideways, and shows zero restraint in his speed. Victor, we love you, but can you save a little tire for the rest of us?

We enjoyed our first experience with Audi’s first mid-engine car since the dearly departed R8. After just three laps driving around the former home of the Spanish Grand Prix, we love the Nuvolari. Having spent time with the Nuvolari’s donor plug-in hybrid platform, the Lamborghini Temerario, eases the tension.

The Audi’s cabin, a cocoon of microfiber and leather with aluminum door handles, seat controls, and vents, oozes fancy-dinner vibes more so than qualifying laps. It’s a welcoming environment. Strapped into the driver’s seat, the Nuvolari starts up in its pure electric mode. Turning the top-left knob on the busy steering wheel wakes the 789-hp twin-turbocharged 4.0-liter V-8; its flat-plane crankshaft is capable of spinning to a bonkers 10,000 rpm. The cabin fills with glorious noise, most of it real. Turning and holding that same knob activates Dynamic+ mode, summons more noise, and puts most of the systems in their most-ready state.

See all results for new 2028 Audi Nuvolari for sale near 20147

Exiting Jarama’s final corner and pointing down the straight, the Nuvolari accelerates as if it were shot off an aircraft carrier’s flight deck. As in the Temerario, the screaming V-8 has friends. A 147-hp motor connected to the crankshaft fills the lulls in the engine’s torque delivery. The front axle has two 147-hp motors that also provide torque vectoring. While the Temerario stores its energy in a 4-kWh battery, the Nuvolari (like the new Lamborghini Revuelto SV) utilizes a larger 7-kWh pack. The new pack supplies up to 254 horses, compared to the Temerario’s 187-hp limit. In all, the V-8 and electric motors work together to create a 987-hp party, or 80 more than the Temerario.

By the end of Jarama’s nearly half-mile front straight, we’ve almost reached an indicated 185 mph. The Nuvolari brakes as effortlessly as it accelerates, courtesy of Brembo CCM-R Plus carbon-ceramic rotors (16.5-inch up front and 16.1-inch at the rear) that bite hard. The pedal doesn’t feel as wooden as the Lambo’s, where more force yields more stopping power. In the Nuvolari, a spring change within the brake actuator feels like it prioritizes pedal travel to modulate the brakes rather than pressure. Nevertheless, the pedal feels great.

See all results for new 2028 Audi Nuvolari for sale near 20147

Bending the Nuvolari into Turn 2 reveals off-center steering feel that isn’t nearly as over-caffeinated as the Lamborghini’s. Here, it feels very Audi. Effort ramps up in a linear fashion but is never too heavy. It’s as direct as it needs to be, and there’s ample indication when the front tires have had enough.

A nearly 1000-hp supercar should be somewhat intimidating to drive, but Audi has tuned the Nuvolari to be a gentle beast. Audi’s Quattro Predictive Drive system uses a 6-D sensor (as in the Revuelto SV) to absorb information from the car, and, based on driving habits, in-house-developed algorithms predict what the car should do next. Tail out on corner exit? The car’s computer brain might send a little extra torque vectoring to the front axle to keep things in check. Crank a little too much steering input into a large braking event? You can feel the motors straighten out the car.

The Predictive Drive system also communicates with the dampers. They’re adaptive units with high- and low-speed electronic controls for the compression and rebound sides. The dampers allow Audi to use a softer spring than the Temerario to help maintain a consistent tire patch and keep the car stable during braking and the body flat when the Bridgestone Potenza Race tires are loaded up midcorner.

With Predictive Drive working in the background, the rest of our extremely limited time in the Nuvolari is spent trying to digest the experience. Don’t forget to tug the massive aluminum shift paddles before slamming into the 10,000-rpm limiter. Check. What’s after this corner? I can’t remember; Victor said something about braking. Speaking of braking, is the rear motor really braking the engine when there’s more horsepower than is available for traction to harvest extra energy for the front straight? There’s no indication it’s happening, but we’ll take their word for it.

Familiarizing yourself with your very own Nuvolari will cost north of $700,000. That’s before customizing one of the 499 Nuvolaris that will be built. You can specify any color you’d like or choose an unpainted carbon-fiber body for an unspecified cost, something we’re excited to see. We’re even more excited to see how Audi’s return to the supercar world trickles down into the RS brand. We’ll be waiting.

Premier League: 10 talking points from the weekend’s action

📅 2026-11-09  |  Read Source →

Delap’s talents resurface in style, Gómez delights Brighton with more fireworks, and hair-pulling confusion at Everton

It would have been very easy for Oliver Glasner to lose patience with Liam Delap. The forward is still only 23, yet the days when he was rattling in 12 in a Premier League season for Ipswich have come to seem a very long time ago (and his six games for Manchester City even longer). A season at Chelsea had left him bereft of confidence, an odd mix of diffidence and overaggression, devoid of composure in the box, arms flailing like a broken windmill. But the old gifts have not left him. Both goals on Saturday were the result of great muscular surges and if the first finish seemed essentially about hitting the ball as hard as possible on target, the second was a precise – and still powerful – finish in off the far post. After games such as this it’s possible to imagine him as England’s long-term replacement for Harry Kane. Jonathan Wilson

Match report: Nottingham Forest 3-1 Manchester City

Amid the chaotic blur of proceedings at Old Trafford, there was one outstanding performance. Alysson had only one goal to his name in his Premier League career at Aston Villa, but he scored one and helped set up another two in the first 48 minutes against Manchester United. The 20-year-old winger, who has represented Brazil only at under-16 level, was signed from Grêmio for an initial £8.7m last January. His only goal in 18 previous appearances was against Nottingham Forest in September – it took less than a minute to open the scoring at United and less than four for his saved shot to rebound out to Nicolas Jackson for Villa’s second. United’s right-back, Diogo Dalot, was too slow to close Alysson down for his opener and did not have any complaints when his trip on the Brazilian conceded a penalty at the start of the second half. Dalot, often the face of United’s struggles, was booed off when substituted in the second half – Alysson was applauded off by the away end. Billy Munday

Match report: Manchester United 4-4 Aston Villa

Diego Gómez is the latest example of a slow-burning Brighton player given the chance and space to bed in before then flourishing. His current status is as an overnight sensation, though a taste for the spectacular and an eye for goal have been obvious since two goals against Leeds in November 2025. Gómez had signed from Inter Miami that January. The inside-right position from which he drilled his left-foot sidewinder to open the scoring against Brentford brings the best from his shooting prowess with both feet. His October halfway line strike against Crystal Palace came with his right foot. As with a number of Brighton’s purchases, Gómez can play in other positions, variously defensive midfielder, attacking midfielder, even on the wing. Within Fabian Hürzeler’s stringent tactical setup, he is asked to work hard for the ball. Such high pressing created the opportunity Charalampos Kostoulas should have done better with in the moments after the opening goal. John Brewin

There is nothing wrong with Arsenal’s habit of winning games by a single goal: Mikel Arteta’s side did it 15 times on their way to the title last season and the latest against Hull was their fourth this season. Yet despite being six points clear of Manchester City heading into the international break, Arteta will have some concerns about the lack of goals so far. With only 15 from their first 10 matches, Arsenal are on course to break Manchester United’s record of 67 for the fewest by a team being crowned champions in 1993 – the Premier League’s first season. They have also been struggling to score in the first half of games, having gone six matches in all competitions without doing so last month, and Arteta has called on his players to be more ruthless. “Sometimes we try to score the perfect goal against low blocks, but we need to be much more chaotic than that. If we open the scoring much earlier also, it changes the game,” he said. Ed Aarons

All hail Brian Brobbey. It is fascinating that a player sometimes derided for his supposed lack of technical quality while at Ajax has developed into one of the Premier League’s most potent and difficult-to-play centre-forwards in little more than a year in England. His new contract, announced last Wednesday, is richly deserved. There is, as Régis Le Bris acknowledged after the win over Chelsea in which he bullied the visitors, no one quite like him. “Against a team that wants to press like that it’s so important to have a target man,” said the Sunderland manager, and Brobbey was that and then some. Much has been made of how Chelsea miss João Pedro but the Mackems are a different team with their flying Dutchman. Andy Brassell

It was a tale of two benches at Elland Road. The big question hanging over Leeds is whether they have the support for Dominic Calvert-Lewin to make a concerted push for Europe this season; a question that went some way to being answered when the substitutes Harry Wilson and Noah Okafor combined for the latter to score the winner against Tottenham. In contrast, Roberto De Zerbi’s attacking options let him down in a game that felt there for the taking. Dominic Solanke struggled after replacing Mathys Tel, Omar Marmoush looked limited in the central striker role and their brightest performer was the 18-year-old Lucá Williams-Barnett, who, you would suspect, is now not far from a deserved start. Having won three straight games before their defeat in West Yorkshire, Spurs suddenly go into the international break with more questions than answers over their attacking credentials once again. Aaron Bower

Kick off your evenings with the Guardian's take on the world of football

Andoni Iraola had one wish as he signed off for the international window with the 3-1 win at Crystal Palace – that he does not lose any players to injury before Liverpool are back in action against Manchester United at Anfield on Sunday week. The manager is scarred by the previous round of international fixtures: Dominik Szoboszlai, Cody Gakpo and Alexander Isak all picked up problems. But I have to understand the national teams. Also I’m sure Dom will want to go. I’m not sure if it’s going to be fine.” David Hytner

Bournemouth are really getting to grips with the Thursday-Sunday grind in their first season in Europe. Marco Rose has been working hard to ensure his team are in the right shape to start Premier League games quickly after a Europa League assignment three days earlier. After Thursday’s trip to face Sparta Prague, Bournemouth were quick out of the traps at Ipswich on Sunday, taking the lead through Evanilson after five minutes. “Last week [against Leeds] I said we start slow and this week we start quite quick and score an early goal – I am a magician,” said the head coach, after their eighth consecutive win in all competitions. The Brazilian did not need Christian Walton’s helping hand with this goal. BM

The laws on hair-pulling were changed for this season after the red cards that were issued to Michael Keane, Lisandro Martínez and Dan Ballard in 2025-26. An anti-AI indie game about keeping hand-drawn fish has become a surprise viral hit

📅 2026-11-09  |  Read Source →

And it's all thanks to its emotive trailer.

When you purchase through links on our site, we may earn an affiliate commission. Here’s how it works.

It's becoming harder for indie developers to stand out on Steam amid all the AI slop out there, but one little gem has just shown that it's still possible to cut through the noise. Drawquarium is a game about drawing and looking after fish, and it's picked up thousands of wishlists in days – much to its developer's surprise.

Key to the viral success was a novel trailer that breaks a key rule but seems to have really captured people's imaginations in the current climate. It provides a sign of hope for other developers too, if only by showing that there's a still a big appetite for quirky games that display a clear human touch (if you're just starting your own developer journey, see our guides to the best game development software and the best laptops for game development).

Kaleb Lechowski's Drawquarium is an upcoming sandbox game that involves drawing your own fish and then feeding and raising it, and gradually building a cosy aquarium. You can then use your aquarium as an animated desktop wallpaper, if you want, and there are some built-in tools designed to help players focus or unwind.

Kaleb says he was inspired to develop the game because he wanted to bring his children’s drawings to life. That original impetus appears to also have inspired the fictional story of the trailer above.

A lot of people might say the trailer breaks a core rule by not showing any actual gameplay until past the halfway mark in what is a three-minute video. That might not normally be the most sensible way to promote a game, and it wouldn't work for everything. But in this case, the well-executed and emotive nostalgia marketing really paid off.

A post on Twitter has received over 128,000 likes, another over 100,000, and the game picked up 70,000 new wishlists in just a week. A lot of people are saying the trailer made them cry.

"Your trailer made me sob. It was way too relatable," one person writes on Reddit. "I can’t wait to play this little game. Thank you for putting your creativity into the world. We all need things like this that make us wonder in awe at the magic of nature, beauty, and art."

Sign up to Creative Bloq's daily newsletter, which brings you the latest news and inspiration from the worlds of art, design and technology.

"Bro made a Cannes award winning short film to sell an indie game," someone writes on YouTube.

Kaleb has a background in 3D art for the film industry, and those storytelling skills clearly carry over to the trailer, which works as a self-contained story. But he was still surprised at how well it was received.

"What surprised me most was how strongly the message behind the trailer resonated with people," the developer comments on Reddit. "With AI becoming such a huge topic in gaming and art, it seems like people are really craving something made by humans, with genuine love and passion. I'm honestly overwhelmed and don't even know what to do with all these emotions."

You can download a Drawquarium demo on Steam. The full game is scheduled for release on 13 November.

Alas, the sudden attention has also brought with it the negative aspects of indie game success these days. Several unauthorised copies of the Drawquarium demo have been cropping up in the Google Play store, so make sure you check out the game via the official Steam page.

<a href="https://www.fiercehealthcare.com/sponsored/closing-last-mile-value-based-care-ai" hreflang="en">Closing the last mile of value-based care with AI</a>

📅 2026-11-09  |  Read Source →
Closing the last mile of value-based care with AI" style="max-width:100%; height:auto; border-radius:8px;" loading="lazy">

One of the most challenging parts of value-based care is its timing. Too often, diagnosis gaps or risks arrive late, after an appointment or follow-up, when there is less opportunity to improve care and results.

This is a persistent last-mile challenge in VBC: translating population-level information into action within everyday workflows. Reports, payer portals, and standalone analytics tools may identify important opportunities, but practices are often left on their own to connect those insights to the clinicians and teams responsible for addressing them.

AI can help close that distance. Instead of simply generating more insights, AI combined with clinical and payer data can surface actionable intelligence at the point in time when teams address care gaps, evaluate diagnoses, and monitor VBC performance.

Put VBC insights where clinicians can act on them

Identifying a potential diagnosis gap is only part of the job. A clinician still needs enough information to determine whether the diagnosis is supported by the patient’s record and, if so, document it appropriately. If finding that evidence requires searching through the chart or consulting a separate tool, a useful insight can create additional work before it leads to action.

The stakes are also rising. As scrutiny of risk-adjustment coding increases, practices need confidence that every documented diagnosis is supported by the record. An AI suggestion surfaced without clearly documented rationale can add risk, instead of the opposite.

This raises the standard for AI in diagnosis-gap workflows. Each suggested gap should come paired with clinical evidence that references relevant lab and imaging results, vital signs, and clinical notes, so that clinicians can quickly evaluate recommendations within the visit time.

athenahealth’s Clinically Inferred Diagnosis Gaps was built around this model. It uses generative AI to analyze existing chart information and surface potential gaps related to the CMS-HCC risk-adjustment model, along with supporting evidence, inside the clinician’s existing workflow.

Bring payer insights into the clinical workflow

With visibility across care settings, payers have a unique line of insight into patients’ overall care journeys. This more comprehensive view may reveal potential diagnosis gaps beyond what a clinician encounters during an individual visit. Bringing those payer-supplied gaps into the clinical workflow can give clinicians additional information to consider while the patient is in front of them.

New workflows connect payer insights with point-of-care clinical actionability. athenahealth’s moment of care diagnosis-gap workflow allows payers to flag a potential diagnosis gap prior to an upcoming appointment, allowing clinicians enough time to review during the encounter. If the clinician determines that a diagnosis is appropriate, they can document it through the existing workflow and can even close the loop by sharing information back to the payer about how the gap was addressed.

Find VBC opportunities across your patient population

For true value at scale, organizations need to be able to look beyond individual patient visits and at their broader populations. Quality and population health leaders need to answer practical questions across entire patient populations: Which patients still have an open care gap? How are clinicians performing on specific quality measures? Where should the team focus its attention next? Getting those answers can require configuring reports, combining results, or exporting data for further analysis.

AI tools offer powerful new ways to facilitate a macro view, plumbing data to surface trends, emerging needs, and risk profiles that can help direct strategies. Rather than requiring users to translate each question into a predefined report, Sage™, athenaOne’s AI assistant, gives them another way to investigate VBC performance and zero in on the patients, clinicians, or measures that need a closer look. For independent practices, that can make population-level information more accessible to the people responsible for turning it into action.

Reduce the work between VBC insight and action

The value-based care burden can be heavy for independent practices with thinner teams and fewer resources. Integrated AI-native platforms with smarter insights, payer data, population analytics, and performance reporting can help practices manage the coordinated efforts required to succeed in VBC.

When these stakeholders and systems can communicate, their independent value becomes exponentially greater. When information is spread across separate reports, payer portals, analytics tools, and clinical systems, practice teams may have to find and reconcile data before they can decide what needs attention.

Bringing more of that intelligence into the environment where clinicians and quality teams already work creates a more direct path: Find an opportunity → add relevant context → get the information to someone who can act → understand performance.

That is the larger opportunity for AI-enabled VBC: not simply generating more information, but making useful clinical, payer, and performance insights easier to put to work.

<a href="https://www.fiercehealthcare.com/sponsored/strengthening-healthcare-delivery-through-proactive-primary-care" hreflang="en">Strengthening Healthcare Delivery Through Proactive Primary Care</a>

📅 2026-11-09  |  Read Source →
Strengthening Healthcare Delivery Through Proactive Primary Care" style="max-width:100%; height:auto; border-radius:8px;" loading="lazy">

A stroke can happen without warning. In the months beforehand, however, there may have been a blood pressure reading that needed attention, a prescription that lapsed or a follow-up visit that never got scheduled. Primary care is usually where those signals surface first, and where there is the best chance to act before an emergency.

Much of that work happens between appointments. Care teams review charts ahead of visits, call patients after a hospital discharge and follow up on overdue screenings. Traditional fee-for-service pays for the visit itself but offers little support for the proactive work around it. Value-based care shifts the focus from the volume of services delivered to the quality, outcomes and total cost of care. By aligning financial incentives with prevention, it helps keep patients healthy and out of the hospital while strengthening the long-term financial sustainability of the practice.

For people with traditional Medicare, the Medicare Shared Savings Program (MSSP) enables primary care practices to be paid for that preventive work. MSSP is a permanent, voluntary accountable care program run within traditional Medicare by the Centers for Medicare & Medicaid Services (CMS). Physician groups, health systems and community health centers participate by joining an accountable care organization (ACO), which takes responsibility for the quality and total cost of care for its assigned patients.

Clinicians continue to bill Medicare for covered services under fee-for-service, and MSSP adds a layer of value-based savings on top. When proactive primary care lowers the total cost of patients' care against a historical benchmark, often by preventing avoidable hospitalizations and emergency visits, and the ACO meets quality standards, Medicare shares a portion of those savings with the ACO. Because savings stem directly from keeping people healthy, clinical goals and financial rewards finally pull in the same direction. Shared savings are not guaranteed and depend on multiple factors.

The program operates at significant scale. In performance year 2026, 511 ACOs are serving 12.6 million people with traditional Medicare.¹ In performance year 2024, 75% of participating ACOs earned shared savings, and the program produced roughly $2.5 billion in net savings for Medicare.²

Physician-led value-based care puts primary care physicians and practices at the center of care delivery and decision-making. Shared savings give primary care organizations a revenue stream tied to prevention, which they can reinvest in staff, outreach and technology.

An ACO enablement partner can provide the data, care model support and operational infrastructure needed to identify patient needs, coordinate care and manage the ACO's reporting to Medicare, while participating primary care organizations retain control over how they deliver care. Aledade is one such partner. In fact, practices with five or more years with Aledade deliver approximately three times higher savings than the national average for non-Aledade ACOs of similar tenure.3

With that support and the actionable insights and clinical guidance it brings, primary care teams can reach the right patient at the right time, and the results show up as an emergency visit avoided, a stroke prevented or a hospital stay that never happened. For patients, prevention can mean more birthdays, more holidays and more time with the people they love.

Aledade is a physician-led value-based care company and the leading value-based care participant in MSSP.4 Aledade works alongside primary care organizations to put proactive care into practice through true financial alignment. Within its ACOs, savings are generally allocated approximately 50% to Aledade and 50% to its practice partners, with individual practice distributions weighted by quality performance and other contractual terms.⁵ That means Aledade succeeds only when its primary care partners do.

For more than a decade, Aledade has partnered with thousands of primary care organizations through the ACOs it forms and manages, providing data, technology, care model support and operational infrastructure. Together, these capabilities help clinicians identify patient needs earlier, coordinate care and build a sustainable financial model that helps direct more revenue to primary care. When primary care is financially incentivized to keep patients healthy, warning signs can be caught earlier and life-threatening events can be prevented.

1 2026 Medicare Accountable Care Organization Initiatives Participation Highlights, CMS.

2 CMS Proposes Transformational Medicare Reforms to Expand Accountable Care, CMS, July 2026.

3 Aledade analysis of CMS Public Use File of MSSP annual results through 2024; for Aledade data incorporates private CMS data for 2025; weighted average of performance for all data 2015+, incorporates Aledade 2016+ cohorts.

4 Based on internal analysis of data from the 2024 CMS ACO Public Use File.

5 Based on Aledade's standard ACO contractual terms as of January 1, 2026.

<a href="https://www.fiercehealthcare.com/sponsored/fragmentation-integration-new-model-specialty-care" hreflang="en">From Fragmentation to Integration: A New Model for Specialty Care</a>

📅 2026-11-09  |  Read Source →
From Fragmentation to Integration: A New Model for Specialty Care" style="max-width:100%; height:auto; border-radius:8px;" loading="lazy">

Health systems are navigating growing demands with increasingly limited resources. As costs rise and teams are stretched, technology has become essential to helping people work smarter. But technology alone isn’t the answer. Many health systems and specialty care teams are discovering that more tools don’t always mean a more efficient process or better patient outcomes.

Approximately 25% of healthcare spending in the U.S. goes toward administrative expenses. At the same time, 60% of hospitals and health systems use more than 50 software solutions for operations. Yet more technology hasn’t meant less complexity. Care teams still navigate disconnected tools, siloed data, fragmented workflows, and manual handoffs. The opportunity isn’t to add more technology; it’s to make everything work better together.

Health systems want to better manage the specialty patient journey, but administrative burdens get in the way of more coordinated care. What they need is an integrated approach – one that improves patient experiences and outcomes by connecting workflows through technology and adding dedicated human expertise to serve as an extension of existing care teams.

When specialty care teams spend more time navigating processes and technology than focusing on patients, trust erodes, costs rise, and turnover climbs. It also limits teams’ ability to apply the clinical expertise and personalized attention that complex specialty care requires. This friction shows up in three core areas:

The rapid adoption of specialized software and standalone tools for tasks such as scheduling, patient intake, and remote monitoring fragments care delivery. Teams constantly toggle between platforms, manually piecing together a single patient’s record. This can contribute to cognitive overload among care teams, a factor in burnout, and it diverts valuable time and attention from clinical decision-making and personalized patient support that require human expertise.

Point solutions may solve individual challenges, but they often operate in isolation. When systems don’t fully connect, patient information becomes fragmented across applications, leaving specialty care teams without visibility into the full context of a patient’s broader care journey. This can increase the risk of redundant testing, duplicated work, and inaccurate reporting, while making it harder for clinicians to identify when patients may need additional interventions.

The care journey includes multiple critical transitions, from cross-departmental referrals to prescription routing to external coordination. Despite healthcare’s digital process, these transitions still rely too frequently on faxes, phone calls, and manual spreadsheets. The added administrative burden makes it harder for care teams to coordinate timely interventions and provide consistent, personalized support. Without an integrated model that connects the entire care journey, referrals or post-procedure care plans can fall through the cracks.

Complexity comes at a cost: time teams could be spending on patient care. According to AMA research, for every hour physicians spend with patients, they spend nearly two more on EHR and desk work.

For specialty care teams, disconnected technology and fragmented workflows can make it even harder to see the full picture of a patient’s care. Patients with multiple chronic conditions, for example, often see several specialists. When any one of them lacks complete visibility into the patient’s full treatment plan, specialty performance breaks down. The risk of prescribing therapies that shouldn't be combined rises, potentially compromising patient safety and delaying therapy.

Solving today’s specialty care challenges requires more than another point solution. It requires a connected operating model that brings workflows, information and expertise together, giving care teams the visibility and support they need to act with greater confidence and keep patients moving forward.

Shields Health Solutions’ integrated care model combines technology, data, and clinical expertise to reduce administrative burden, streamline care coordination, and provide specialty care teams with more time to focus on patients. Rather than relying on technology alone, Shields’ clinical pharmacists and liaisons work as an extension of specialty care teams. They translate insights into action, resolving barriers to therapy, coordinating care, and supporting patients throughout treatment.

That combination of human expertise and connected technology delivers measurable results. With average time to therapy in under 48 hours compared with the seven-to-10-day industry average, Shields’ integrated care model drives connected performance, alleviating operational strain on care teams while improving access to therapy, better patient outcomes, and long-term growth.

Opinion: Andoni Iraola's very own Ibrahima Konate has been found

📅 2026-11-09  |  Read Source →

Andoni Iraola's time as Liverpool manager has been a bit of a roller coaster so far.

The Spaniard took over at the start of July and, although the Reds did fairly well in their pre-season games, that same level of performance hasn't really followed them through to the actual season.

So far, the Reds have lost three games across all competitions and have failed to fill their fans with any sort of confidence that things can be turned around.

And, with the transfer window now just around the corner, fans have started to question what sort of moves Liverpool could make to improve their fortunes.

Indeed, Liverpool may have already spent around £600 million on new players over the last couple of seasons but, with the level of player they're being linked with, it feels like a lot more than that is going to be spent.

Liverpool have re-ignited their interest in Castello Lukeba

Ibrahima Konate's departure during the 2026-27 summer transfer window was one that many fans expected.

For the last couple of years, talks between the Frenchman and Liverpool about a new contract had taken place but, each time either party was asked about negotiations, nobody could give a definitive answer about whether an agreement was going to be reached.

This led fans to realise that Konate's time was up, especially when it was announced that Jeremy Jacquet would be joining Liverpool for £55 million.

From that point on, fans have come to grips with the loss of the Frenchman and have tried to source a new player to plug the gap.

That's where RB Leipzig centre-back Castello Lukeba comes in.

As per a fresh update from FootballFanCast, Liverpool are now the most likely team to sign Lukeba.

"Football FanCast sources understand that Liverpool are currently the most interested Premier League club in signing RB Leipzig centre-back Castello Lukeba," they wrote.

"The 23-year-old has attracted attention from several leading English sides, with Arsenal having monitored his development for an extended period and Manchester United also expressing interest.

"However, Liverpool are understood to have emerged as the strongest suitors, and sources understand the Frenchman's representatives have informed all three suitors of his desire to move to the Premier League."

Castello Lukeba could become Andoni Iraola's version of Ibrahima Konate

Looking back at Konate's time with Liverpool, there were definitely some highs and lows.

The Frenchman won the Premier League twice and the Champions League once but, towards the end of his time at Anfield, he was making some rather silly mistakes.

Whether he was misplacing passes or mistiming challenges, some fans were happy to see him go.

But, when he was at his best, Konate was easily one of the Premier League's best centre-backs.

This is exactly who Lukeba could become. Not only is he another French centre-back from RB Leipzig but he's also a similar age to when Konate left Leipzig for L4 back in 2021.

Much like Konate, Lukeba also has a release clause within his Leizpig contract and, although his is worth a bit more than Konate's was, it feels like 23-year-old has a lot of potential to fulfil.

Of course, these are just rumours at the moment but, of all the centre-backs linked with Liverpool ahead of the January transfer window, Lukeba is one of the deals that makes the most sense.

📅 2026-11-09  |  Read Source →

Lamar Odom is earning some major cash with his foray into OnlyFans.

A representative for the former NBA player told People on Sunday, November 8, that Odom, 47, has made over $50,000 in just eight days on the content creation platform.

Odom said in a statement to the outlet, “The response to my OnlyFans has been incredible. I’m connecting with fans in a whole new way, building something big, and now I’m looking to bring other athletes along for the ride.”

“There’s a real opportunity here and we’re just getting started,” Odom added.

Odom launched his OnlyFans account in October, writing in his bio, “You’ve seen me on the court. You’ve seen me on TV. Now get to know the REAL me. A little more personal. A little more playful. And a side of Lamar you haven’t seen before.”

The former Los Angeles Lakers power forward also told TMZ that he expected judgment for signing up to OnlyFans, which is popular among adult content creators.

“People hear OnlyFans and automatically think one thing, but I’m not here to tell you what you will or won’t see. I’ve lived a pretty wild life,” he said.

TMZ reported that Odom’s girlfriend, L.A. real estate agent Rachel Bradley, has also signed up for OnlyFans and the couple may share joint content in the future.

Odom was married to reality television star Khloé Kardashian, 42, from 2009 to 2016.

During a recent episode of Hulu’s The Kardashians, Kardashian and mom Kris Jenner recalled how they caught Odom having an orgy during the height of his substance abuse issues. (The former basketball player was found unconscious and went into a coma in 2015.)

“We’re going to extract him from the situation because he’s being held hostage by these hookers,” Jenner said.

“Khloé’s security burst into this room,” she added before Kardashian said, “And all I saw [was] they were literally having a circle jerk. There were 20 people in the room.”

“This is solidifying how I will never date a freak again,” Kardashian said on camera. ”I needed to put this on my manifest list [for a man]. Maybe I should add no circle jerks and no orgies. Also maybe no hardcore drugs.”

Report: Liverpool ready to sign €100m Virgil van Dijk replacement

📅 2026-11-09  |  Read Source →

Virgil van Dijk has entered the final year of his current contract at Liverpool.

The Reds' captain has been a defensive colossus for almost a decade in Merseyside, but his time at Anfield could be coming to an end.

It wouldn't come as a surprise to see the Reds extend their captain, but planning has begun in case a deal can't be reached.

Liverpool's recruitment team will have drawn up an extensive list of potential replacements, but one target is causing plenty of discussion ahead of what could be a €100 million move.

That would be a world-record fee for a defender, something which would be fitting to replace Van Dijk.

Bremer earns Juventus 2-2 draw at Fiorentina but leaves them six points off Champions League places

📅 2026-11-09  |  Read Source →

Gleison Bremer's equaliser earned Juventus a point at the Stadio Artemio Franchi after they twice fell behind against Fiorentina. Spalletti's side now go into the international break outside the Champions League places, with their next home game brought forward by a day.

The point leaves Fiorentina 15th in the table on 9 points.

The hosts led after four minutes. Mateo Pellegrino scored, set up by Pedro Goncalves. Nick Woltemade levelled in the 32nd minute from a Randal Kolo Muani assist, and it was 1-1 at half-time.

Fiorentina went ahead again in the 54th minute. Guglielmo Vicario made the first save, but the Juventus defenders were too slow to clear. Alieu Njie forced in the rebound. Bremer then reached a Douglas Luiz corner in the six-yard box before David de Gea could get to it.

Juventus completed 384 accurate passes to Fiorentina’s 273. Fiorentina still attempted 22 shots to Juventus’s 18, and both sides had 6 on target. The expected goals figures, 2.46 for Juventus and 2.14 for Fiorentina, are consistent with a fairly even contest despite the visitors’ control of the ball.

The closing stages could have gone either way. Former Juventus defender Radu Drăgușin headed in from a corner in the 82nd minute. The goal was ruled out because he pushed Bremer with both hands. Near the end, Vicario saved twice in quick succession to protect the point. Juventus also claimed a penalty when the ball struck João Mário’s arm in the Fiorentina area, but none was given.

Referee Andrea Colombo booked three Juventus players. Juan Cabal was cautioned in the 13th minute and Bremer in the 22nd. Kolo Muani was booked for dissent in the 73rd. Christ Inao Oulaï was Fiorentina’s only booking.

Juventus were without the injured Kenan Yildiz, Andrea Cambiaso, Nico González, Manuel Locatelli, Khéphren Thuram and Kamil Grabara. Weston McKennie and Zeki Çelik returned to the squad. Spalletti made three changes in the 57th minute, sending on Zhegrova for Woltemade, Alajbegovic for Boga and Celik for Cabal. Ekhator replaced McKennie in the 78th minute and Gatti came on for Lucumí in the 89th.

At his press conference at the Franchi, Spalletti said he was not happy because his team does not play at a steady enough level to aim for anything important. He did praise Kolo Muani, describing him as the player everyone wanted and one he can use as a centre-forward or out wide. Shown a replay of the João Mário incident, he dodged the question and asked, smiling, for a different one.

The draw ended a run of two wins, against AZ Alkmaar and Napoli. Woltemade and Pellegrino both received a rating of 8.2 from the data provider.

Next up for Juventus is Venezia, who sit 19th on 6 points. When fixture times were first set, that game was pencilled in for Monday 23 November at 20:45 Italian time.

Late Buchanan and Carlos Romero goals give Villarreal 2-0 win over Getafe

📅 2026-11-09  |  Read Source →

Villarreal ended a two-game losing run on Sunday, 8 November, with a home win over Getafe in matchday 12 of LaLiga. Both goals came in the final 10 minutes at the Estadio de la Cerámica. Tajon Buchanan scored first and Carlos Romero added the second in stoppage time.

Iñigo Pérez’s side came into the game after a 0-1 league defeat to Espanyol and a 0-6 loss to Paris Saint-Germain. They controlled almost all of the afternoon, with 73% of the ball and 566 passes to Getafe’s 220.

The first half finished goalless. Getafe goalkeeper David Soria made 5 saves and was rated 7.27, the best mark among Pepe Bordalás’s players. Villarreal finished with 22 shots to 9. They had 7 on target, and Getafe managed just 1. Their expected goals figure of 2.38, against 0.68 for the visitors, reflected how one-sided the attacking play was.

Getafe picked up the first two bookings. Mario Martín was cautioned in the 32nd minute and Nemanja Gudelj in the 62nd, both for fouls. Getafe committed 13 fouls in total, and Villarreal committed 6. Santi Comesaña and Renato Veiga were then booked for the home side in the 67th and 76th minutes.

The breakthrough came in the 80th minute. Nicolas Pépé set up Buchanan, who had 4 shots across the afternoon. In the first minute of stoppage time, Ayoze Pérez supplied the assist for Carlos Romero to make the game safe. Ayoze created 3 chances in his 27 minutes on the pitch.

Carlos Romero capped his 90 minutes with the goal and was rated 7.84, the highest of any player. Logan Costa was close behind on 7.8, and Buchanan received 7.69.

The result repeats Villarreal’s win by the same scoreline over Getafe at La Cerámica on 6 December 2025. Getafe had won the most recent meeting 2-1 at home in February. Across the last six league encounters between the clubs, Villarreal now have three wins, Getafe one, and two have been drawn.

Inside the world of Captain Chaos at Man United: Michael Carrick will not walk away but here's what the players are saying as they fail to respond to his direction, writes CHRIS WHEELER

📅 2026-11-09  |  Read Source →

Published: 02:00 EST, 9 November 2026 | Updated: 08:52 EST, 9 November 2026

While the Manchester United fans who had gone ahead with a booze boycott of Old Trafford watched in horror as their team teetered on the brink of total collapse and head coach Michael Carrick clung to his job, you couldn't help but think of Lloyd Bridges as Steve McCroskey, the frazzled air traffic control boss in Airplane! 'Looks like I picked the wrong week to quit drinking!'

God knows United have forced their supporters to hit the bottle this season, not least in the past six days when they have handed a two-goal advantage to Roma and Aston Villa (twice) and still managed to salvage a win and a draw.

But this cannot carry on. As cavalier as United like to be, this is not the way to play football.

Coming out swinging every game like a boxer on his last legs is great for entertainment, it's great for the Sky Sports cameras beaming it live and the Amazon Prime documentary makers filming behind the scenes, but it is not evidence of solid preparation on the training pitch.

Nowhere in the UEFA coaching manuals will you find a chapter on routinely conceding the first two goals being a sensible route to success. For all his bluster after a bonkers 4-4 draw with Aston Villa, Carrick knows it and his players know it.

'After we concede goals we show character, but if we do this before we can win much more games,' said Matheus Cunha. 'I don't know why, everyone tries to get answers for that. We want to be the best and show that we can come back. We need to show the mentality to do this from the start.'

As Manchester United head coach, Michael Carrick is doing a convincing impersonation of Captain Chaos

Coming out swinging every game like a boxer on his last legs is great for entertainment, but it is not evidence of solid preparation on the training pitch

It isn't just in the last week either. United were a goal down against Atletico Madrid inside the first minute and Como in the second. They were two down to Villa after three minutes and 40 seconds, the earliest that has happened to United in the Premier League era.

This is not a coincidence. It is not bad luck. This is in the mind-set of these Manchester United players, and the game management of Carrick – and that is what should worry Old Trafford bosses the most.

As if being in the relegation zone for the next two weeks after a start of one win from 10 games – the worst since Sir Alex Ferguson replaced Ron Atkinson 40 years ago – is not bad enough, United don't seem to be playing to a plan. They are not responding to direction. They are reverting to instinct and moments of skill when everything else fails.

Carrick made reference to it when he spoke before this game, admitting that the players are better when they don't think too much.

'Momentum within games, day-to-day, week-to-week, game-to-game, it's huge,' he said at Carrington on Friday. 'I think it's important with your feelings and your emotions to roll with it. Then your memory can click in and you realise, 'Oh yeah, this is what it feels like, this is how it should be', and you start seeing different things.

'It's all got to come together, but feeling, confidence, and that emotion and sometimes not even thinking. You're at your best when you don't think too much and you just trust and play on instinct, and I think we did a lot of that the other night (against Roma).'

The irony is that Carrick was so measured, so composed as a player. Now, as coach, he is doing a convincing impersonation of Captain Chaos. United's games are reminiscent of the high-scoring madness of Erik ten Hag's last full season in charge, devoid of stability or structure.

That is what Carrick's employers need to consider when they sit down to discuss whether he can restore the sanity of his first four months in charge which guided the club towards third place and Champions League qualification.

Now they are third from bottom and will stay in the relegation zone for the duration of the international break. For three minutes on Sunday, Manchester United – the aristocrats of English football – were actually in last place after Sunderland took the lead against Chelsea and Crystal Palace were still goalless with Liverpool.

United don't seem to be playing to a plan or responding to direction - they are reverting to instinct and moments of skill when everything else fails

Carrick's employers need to consider  whether he can restore the sanity of his first four months in charge

That being in 18th place – one below Coventry – is somehow seen as blessed relief from the unrelenting humiliation of this season, says everything about the state of the club.

At the end of a week that marked 40 years since Ferguson's arrival and was celebrated by a dinner with many of his most famous players, it has been quite the fall from grace.

Which brings us back to the fans. They are a loyal bunch and once again serenaded Carrick off the pitch at full-time having spent much of the previous two hours slaughtering the owners, chief executive Omar Berrada and director of football Jason Wilcox.

Protest groups had urged them to boycott buying alcohol at the stadium to hit the Glazer family and minority owner Sir Jim Ratcliffe in the pocket. 'Your funeral not ours – sell up now,' read one new banner. 'We don't want a new stadium, we want new owners,' said another.

But they know their football at United. Was it support or just sympathy with a head coach who has done his best in an impossible situation?

Carrick says he won't walk away and they never do. On Remembrance Sunday, he stood there on the touchline with a poppy on his arm and hope in his heart that United could get themselves out of another self-inflicted crisis.

They did. Again. But how much longer can United and Carrick keep winging it, and believe that calm will emerge from the chaos.

Ur-Nammu

📅 2026-11-09  |  Read Source →

Where Readers Share Great Links. Join Free

Ur-Nammu (c. 2112–2094 BCE) founded the Third Dynasty of Ur and ushered in the Ur III period, often called the Sumerian Renaissance. He is remembered for the Code of Ur-Nammu, the oldest extant law code, and for commissioning the Great Ziggurat of Ur while promoting building projects, irrigation, and cultural revival.
