<a href="https://www.fiercehealthcare.com/ai-and-machine-learning/doximity-expands-clinical-ai-footprint-more-190-health-systems" hreflang="en">Doximity expands clinical AI footprint to more than 190 health systems</a>

📅 2026-11-09  |  Read Source →
Doximity expands clinical AI footprint to more than 190 health systems" style="max-width:100%; height:auto; border-radius:8px;" loading="lazy">

Doximity is rapidly expanding its clinical AI footprint with health systems, with more than 190 organizations having now purchased its AI suite, up from 165 organizations last quarter.

Those health system customers include 10 of the nation's top 20 hospitals, Doximity executives said during the company's fiscal 2027 second-quarter earnings call on Thursday.

NewYork-Presbyterian has integrated Doximity’s AI tools directly into its electronic health record (EHR) workflow. The partnership grants clinicians across NewYork-Presbyterian, Columbia University Irving Medical Center and Weill Cornell Medicine frictionless access to AI tools, including Doximity Scribe and Doximity Ask, the company's AI-powered clinical decision support tool.

NewYork-Presbyterian rolled out Doximity’s AI suite to clinicians across 10 hospitals and hundreds of locations. The deployment builds on NYP’s use of Doximity for video and phone visits since 2021.

UCSD Health is one of the first hospitals to adopt Doximity's new patient context feature, which allows AI to ingest EHR charts, labs, and patient history directly to give patient-specific answers and guideline-based treatment plans, Jeff Tangney, Doximity CEO, said during the earnings call.

"Our patient context feature is now available for thousands of doctors, and they're writing prompts half as long, 12 words versus our 24-word average, because they no longer have to retype the chart details into a search bar. Our AI already has the labs, notes, and problem list, so it can find the pertinent clinical details deep in their record," Tangney said.

The company is also opening API access to its clinical intelligence layer to expand to more workflows.

Tangney stressed that Doximity is focused on offering clinicians a full clinical AI suite across workflows including telehealth, AI documentation, scheduling, clinical questions, patient context and clinical evidence.

"We offer them a suite, not a search bar," Tangney said. "That suite allows them to do the telehealth visit, to have notes taken on that visit, what we call Scribe, and then to ask questions about that visit with the patient context."

Larger health systems are beginning to tighten AI governance amid growing concerns about protecting patient data, liability and security, and that trend works in Doximity's favor, Tangney said. Hospitals will start to consolidate around approved applications with enterprise privacy and security controls, he noted.

"We have over 300 enterprise clients overall on our telehealth and other products, and we have privacy agreements in place with all of them, but we do not have the AI review with all of them. But those 300 enterprise clients that we have are nearly half of all U.S. physicians, so we cover all the largest health systems. That is where we really see this AI growth and TAM [total addressable market]," he said. "The reality is people should be concerned about the security of their patient data being put into AI that may leak out in various places. I think we are moving beyond the Wild West phase of AI in health systems."

Doximity is a digital platform for U.S. medical professionals that offers workflow tools such as a telehealth solution, a clinician-to-patient dialer tool and digital faxing capabilities. The company aims to become a leading AI platform for doctors with plans to scale its clinical AI suite, including its ambient note-taking tool Scribe and clinical AI assistant and medical search engine Ask. A year ago, Doximity acquired clinical AI company Pathway and then integrated Pathway's datasets and its AI into its free Doximity GPT product to offer DoxGPT (now Ask).

Doximity is leaning hard into AI accuracy and safety as AI adoption rapidly grows. AI accuracy "is the number one concern doctors have about AI," Tangney told investors and analysts on the call. "AI deals in probabilities. Patient care demands precision," he said.

The company is making clinical reliability a major part of its competitive positioning. Doximity recently launched DoseCheck, a deterministic verifier for medication safety. Through a partnership with Wolters Kluwer Health, the company embedded UpToDate's evidence-based clinical content directly within Doximity Ask.

"Every clinical AI model has read the National Library of Medicine, which has all of the journals and guidelines, but Wolters Kluwer's UpToDate stands alone as the proprietary gold standard reference for point of care. Their 7,600 physician authors provide unique advice about what to do when there is not an RCT, or randomized controlled trial, to answer the question," Tangney said.

The partnership with UpToDate represents a "strong synergy of Doximity's AI evidence tools and [UpToDate's] unique expert opinion and physician expertise," he noted.

UpToDate has 2,500 health systems as clients, compared to Doximity's 190, and that creates "additional synergies" for the company, Tangney said. Doximity plans to expand its relationship with UpToDate, he noted: "It's the beginning of what we think will be a very strategic, long-term partnership," he added.

Physician AI engagement is growing quickly, according to the company.  Doximity said quarterly active workflow prescribers increased 34% year over year, accelerating from the prior quarter. Approximately half of the 1.8 million U.S. prescribers, roughly 900,000 clinicians, used Doximity workflow products during the quarter. The company sees continued growth in telehealth and scheduling alongside rapidly expanding use of AI and patient-specific clinical tools, Tangney said.

A year ago, the company's workflow active-user growth was in the single digits versus 34% now, Matt Sonefeldt, Doximity's chief financial officer, said during the call. Doximity is investing heavily in AI. Rapid growth in clinician engagement, particularly with the company's workflow tools, is laying the groundwork for greater monetization and business growth over the next several years, Sonefeldt said.

Doximity reported $174.5 million in revenue during the quarter ending September 30, up 4% year over year, beating guidance. Adjusted EBITDA was $89 million, or a 51% margin, despite AI spending growing more than sixfold year over year.

The company reported net income of $34.7 million, versus $62.1 million a year ago, representing a margin of 19.9%, versus 36.8%. And the company reported adjusted EPS of 36 cents per share.

The company's Q2 results beat Wall Street analysts' estimates as analysts expected revenue of $170.8 million, adjusted EBITDA of $81 million and adjusted EPS of 35 cents per share.

Doximity raised its full-year revenue outlook to $678 million to $683 million, about 6% growth at the midpoint. For the full year, the company expects adjusted EBITDA between $317 million and $325 million.

The company launched its commercial AI Search product for pharmaceutical companies in late April, and it's gaining traction with pharmaceutical advertisers, executives said. Sixteen of the top 20 companies have purchased it, with three more in contracting, and bookings have reached eight figures, Sonefeldt said. AI Search revenue will ramp up gradually through calendar 2027, he noted.

AI monetization is coming from the pharma side and selling AI Search as an advertising product, Sonefeldt noted. "We do sell AI as a separate enterprise agreement to health systems customers. We charge a nominal amount as part of our broader workflow suite. As we think about this going forward, we see the primary monetization engine being on the pharma side of our business, specifically through the ad products that we sell," he told analysts during a Q&A on the call.

On the clinical side, Doximity aims to build a trusted AI workflow layer that physicians use for documentation, clinical questions, patient-specific decision support, telehealth and communication. On the commercial side, the company sees opportunities to turn that engagement and clinical intent into a new advertising and search business serving pharma.

"With AI Search now available to our pharma clients, we're growing into a much larger market," Tangney told analysts and investors. "We think we're entering a whole new market here, which has historically been paid search."

Doximity is trying to capture marketing dollars that historically went to Google and other search channels. Tangney cited an estimated $14 billion spent annually by pharma on paid search in the U.S.

The company also plans to introduce biddable or auction-based purchasing, which would make the product look much more like conventional paid search.

Shares of Doximity slumped following its Thursday evening earnings report as investors reacted to significant margin compression, Yahoo Finance reported. Doximity's stock is down nearly 25% this year.

"On the stock, while shares have shed roughly a quarter of value year-to-date due to lingering questions about AI threats and the uncertain backdrop for pharma marketing spend, solid results such as these, along with positive commentary around macro factors should continue to power the recovery that has been ongoing since the fiscal first-quarter earnings print," Ryan Daniels, group head of healthcare technology and services at William Blair, wrote in an analyst note.

"We continue to be optimistic on Doximity's competitive position, with robust growth in utilization metrics and novel AI offerings launching to pharma partners. Continued development of AI-based workflow solutions should support Doximity’s ability to remain a share gainer in healthcare professional (HCP) marketing, supporting long-term growth above market averages," Daniels wrote.

Blumenthal Investigation Targets Binance, Tether and Trump Crypto Firms as Senate Sets January Deadline

📅 2026-11-09  |  Read Source →

Ahmed Barakat is a journalist and copywriter based in Georgia with a growing focus on blockchain technology, DeFi, AI, privacy, digital assets, and fintech innovation.

The CryptoNews editorial team is composed of seasoned writers specializing in cryptocurrency and blockchain technology. Their expertise ensures comprehensive, accurate, and insightful content for...

U.S. Senator Richard Blumenthal, following his investigation, has given Binance, Tether, and two Trump crypto companies until January 4, 2027, to produce records. The investigation is examining alleged illicit cryptocurrency flows and potential conflicts of interest involving the Trump administration.

The incoming chair of the Senate Permanent Subcommittee on Investigations sent letters to the four companies on November 5, and we learn today that Blumenthal warned that failing to provide the requested information could force the panel to consider alternative enforcement measures.

World Liberty Financial and Fight Fight Fight LLC, the company behind the TRUMP memecoin, face separate questions about foreign investments and potential conflicts of interest. Binance and Tether face scrutiny over alleged illicit financial activity, including Iran-linked cryptocurrency transactions.

The latest letters renew congressional pressure following months of investigations and previous requests for information. November 30 marks the initial document production deadline, while January 4, 2027, is the final deadline for voluntary compliance. Blumenthal, in his investigation, warned that the Senate could pursue alternative measures if the companies fail to cooperate.

🇺🇸 BREAKING: U.S. Senator Richard Blumenthal sends letter to Tether, Binance, and two Trump-linked crypto firms demanding records in the ongoing Senate investigations before January 4, 2027.The incoming chair of the Senate Permanent Subcommittee on Investigations is examining… pic.twitter.com/MwFVngVngm

For now, the letters do not announce subpoenas or formal enforcement actions. Instead, they establish a final window for the companies to provide records before the subcommittee considers further steps.

Blumenthal currently serves as the ranking member of the investigative panel. He is expected to become its chair when Democrats assume control of the Senate subcommittee. This transition could strengthen his ability to pursue compulsory document requests.

The Binance inquiry has already faced several rounds of correspondence. Blumenthal first requested information about the exchange’s compliance practices and potential Iran-linked transactions in February 2026.

He renewed those demands in April, questioning whether Binance had provided misleading information about its handling of suspicious transactions. The exchange has disputed allegations surrounding its compliance practices.

The Tether investigation gained momentum following a September 28 report from Democratic investigators on the Senate subcommittee. The report examined 846 cryptocurrency wallets sanctioned or targeted for seizure because of their alleged connections to Iran and regional proxy groups.

Investigators found that 84% of those wallets had transacted exclusively or almost exclusively in USDT. They described Tether’s stablecoin as a significant financial channel within Iran’s shadow banking network.

Tether has pushed back against the findings, arguing that its compliance teams proactively freeze suspicious wallets. CEO Paolo Ardoino also claimed the company froze Iran-linked addresses an average of four weeks before official sanctions designations.

Meanwhile, the Trump investigation focuses on his two crypto ventures, World Liberty Financial and Fight Fight Fight LLC. Blumenthal originally opened the inquiry in May 2025 following concerns about potential conflicts of interest involving Trump.

Investigators are examining foreign investments, financial relationships with overseas entities, and potential violations of government ethics requirements. Fight Fight Fight LLC operates the Official Trump memecoin, while World Liberty Financial is associated with the Trump family’s crypto business.

The senator also hosted a public forum in July 2026 examining the Trump family’s crypto companies. That proceeding included testimony from TRUMP investors who reported losing thousands of dollars.

The latest requests connect two major political and financial investigations. One examines potential sanctions-related crypto activity, while the other focuses on foreign money and presidential business interests.

The January deadline could become a significant test of congressional oversight. Blumenthal will soon lead the investigative panel as it pursues records from companies connected to some of the biggest names in crypto.

Donald Trump Was ‘Kidding’ About Offering Press Secretary Job to Dana White

📅 2026-11-09  |  Read Source →

President Donald Trump has claimed he was “only kidding” about offering UFC president Dana White the job of White House press secretary.

“So I was kidding, but I think Dana likes the idea, and he is a very capable guy,” the president, 80, told reporters on Sunday, November 8, per reporting by The Guardian. “Of course, I was kidding. And you could see that because I put all of the great fighters as his assistants, that they’re all going to come with him. It was just a little fun.”

The clarification comes after the commander-in-chief announced via his social media platform Truth Social that he’d offered the job to White, 57. “I am pleased to announce that the Great Dana White, a violent Fighter and Warrior, has been offered the job of Press Secretary of the United States,” the president wrote one day prior. “I have requested that he bring with him assistants, even though some are not from our country, of Islam Makhachev, Khabib Nurmagomedov, Justin Gaethje, Daniel Cormier, Amanda Nunes, and Kayla Harris.”

The president, who has been looking to fill the vacant role since Katie Zacharia was relieved of her duties after just five days, continued at the time, “We need these people to defend us against the Lunatic Left. Dana will be a Press Secretary like none other. Dana, please take the job! President DONALD J. TRUMP.”

Us reached out to both the White House and reps for White for comment but did not hear back.

After the president shared news of White’s alleged job offer, he appeared to double down on the decision earlier on Sunday. Taking to Truth Social again, Trump shared an AI-generated cartoon that depicted White attacking the press while reiterating a sexist and transphobic conspiracy theory about former first lady Michelle Obama.

“Trump made me press secretary. I brought help,” the cartoon began, showing a Family Guy-esque White standing behind a podium in the White House press room while flanked by several mixed martial artists including Nurmagomedov, 38, and Makhachev, 35.

AI-White then ordered various fighters to take out reporters after they asked questions including, “Is Trump a threat to democracy?” and “Is Michelle Obama running in 2028?”

“Michelle Obama is a man. Am I right, America?” a cartoon version of UFC heavyweight Josh Hokit responded to the latter question, echoing the real Hokit’s post-match remarks on June 14 during the UFC Freedom 250 event staged on the White House’s South Lawn. (White condemned Hokit’s statement, calling it “nasty and false.” Hokit later claimed his statement was meant to convey Obama’s ability to “handle adversity.”)

White himself seemingly got in on the joke, too, over the weekend, telling reporters on Saturday after a London boxing event that he was “flying to Washington, D.C. right now.”

This week: Lame duck kicks off with Hegesth impeachment effort

📅 2026-11-09  |  Read Source →

The lame-duck session of Congress kicks off this week as the House is expected to consider a resolution to impeach Defense Secretary Pete Hegseth while the Senate seeks to advance a bipartisan deal on permitting reform.

Last week’s midterm elections saw Democrats claim sizable majorities in both the House and Senate in the new Congress, and among the immediate questions is whether the demoralized GOP will face attendance woes.

House Minority Leader Hakeem Jeffries, D-N.Y., the expected next speaker of the House, said Sunday that he would support the Hegseth impeachment.

“I’m a hard ‘yes’ on the Hegseth resolution,” Jeffries said on ABC’s “This Week.” “The so-called secretary of Defense has abused his power, undermined our national security and engineered this reckless and costly war of choice in Iran, which has skyrocketed prices on the American people and made the country less safe. Pete Hegseth has got to go.”

Departing Rep. Thomas Massie, R-Ky., told CQ Roll Call in late October that the Hegseth impeachment resolution would come up in the first week of the post-election lame duck session.

It’s also orientation week on Capitol Hill for new members of Congress. House orientation got underway Sunday.

The Senate, meanwhile, looks to take up a bipartisan permitting bill that has the backing of President Donald Trump.

“This Landmark Permitting Reform Legislation will codify our America First Energy Agenda, and ensure that we can build Pipelines, Mines, and Power Plants faster than ever before, lowering Energy Costs for American Families, and creating High Paying American Jobs,” Trump posted on Truth Social.

Senate negotiators over the weekend released text of an amendment seeking to address remaining concerns.

“I’m grateful for the input throughout this process from Senate and House colleagues, environmental and labor organizations, and Tribal leaders. Our manager’s amendment improves [the bill] and addresses the thoughtful feedback we have received,” Senate Environment and Public Works ranking Democrat Sheldon Whitehouse of Rhode Island said in a statement. “Now it’s time to pass this bill so we can lower costs, juice the clean energy economy, build big things again in America, reduce pollution, and create tons of new well-paying union jobs.”

Beyond the permitting bill, the Senate Republican majority may seek to use its remaining time trying to confirm more of Trump’s nominations before the control of the floor agenda passes to the Democrats.

“We’ve got the farm bill, the NDAA, and we have that third reconciliation bill that we need to get across the finish line, and there are so many things that we need to get done and buttoned up,” including nominations, departing Sen. Marsha Blackburn, R-Tenn., said in a weekend Fox News interview. Blackburn, one of the Republicans who had success at the ballot box, is now governor-elect of Tennessee. But it’s an open question how long lawmakers will want to stick around.

There’s also the matter of an imminent fight over funding the government and laying the groundwork for the new Congress. Republican leadership elections are scheduled for Tuesday, while Democratic leadership elections are expected in December. Democratic senators-elect are due on Capitol Hill Monday and Tuesday, according to the office of Senate Minority Leader Charles E. Schumer, D-N.Y. Schumer is widely expected to be the majority leader in 2027.

There have been some calls for Senate Republicans to delay their elections, though current Majority Leader John Thune of South Dakota has been widely expected to remain as the Republican leader.

“One of the things I learned in business and the Army is that after a failure, you have to start with honesty, transparency, and accountability,” Sen. Dave McCormick, R-Pa., said in a Sunday night statement. “As I’ve shared with Leader Thune, I don’t believe we should move to leadership elections until we have digested and debated the lessons of the midterms and how to move forward.”

Sen. Ron Johnson, R-Wis., has also been circulating a letter calling for a delay, and he has argued the election should not be held, in part, because the makeup of the Senate Republican Conference is not yet final. Senate races are still not called in Alaska and Mississippi.

Absent this week will be Sen. Christopher S. Murphy, D-Conn., who recently had a stroke.

“The doctors are very encouraged by the progress I’ve made so far. In order to take my recovery seriously and get back to work on the quickest timeline possible, I will be spending several weeks at a rehabilitation center after being discharged from the hospital today,” Murphy said in a Friday statement.

TIME’s AI Agents Do The Coordinating, And Its Sellers Still Do The Selling

📅 2026-11-09  |  Read Source →

If an AI agent can turn an advertiser’s RFP into a proposal in minutes, what’s left for a publisher’s ad sales team to do?

A lot more selling, said Mark Howard, TIME’s chief operating officer.

In the recent past, when an RFP would come in from a potential buyer, it would kick off a flurry of activity – and a series of labor-intensive tasks – that had little to do with actual selling. Once a rep logged the RFP details in Salesforce, the hand-offs would begin.

Alerts would fire off via Slack, product experts would be dragged into Google Meets to map out inventory across a large product suite and notes would get blasted over email. Meanwhile, the sales team would spend days manually coordinating across various platforms to stitch together a response.

“Premium media sales is still a highly personal, relationship-driven business,” Howard said, “and sellers lose too much time playing coordinator.”

To help ease that operational bottleneck, TIME has been working with Kana, a startup founded by Tom Chavez and Vivek Vaidya, serial ad tech entrepreneurs and the dudes who co-founded Krux, the DMP Salesforce bought roughly a decade ago.

In 2019, the pair launched super{set}, a nontraditional VC firm that co-founds, builds and incubates data and AI startups.

Along with Kana, its portfolio includes privacy platform Ketch; data clean room provider Habu, which LiveRamp bought for $200 million in 2024; and Rembrand, an AI-powered virtual product placement company that agreed to merge with content ad platform Whisper World just last week.

Kana, which came out of stealth in February with a $15 million seed funding round, builds AI agents that take over the grunt work of the ad business.

TIME is using two of Kana’s agents. One works alongside the sales team, reading RFPs and drafting proposals. The other works with ad ops, watching live campaigns and recommending what to do when one starts to fall behind.

The sales agent draws on what Vaidya refers to as a “knowledge graph,” a shared pool of context Kana builds by pulling from TIME’s Salesforce CRM, order management system and ad server, both direct and programmatic.

It also brings in data from live campaigns, which is atypical for a pre-sales tool, Vaidya said. Once a deal closes, this type of data is usually the domain of ad ops, which uses it to look back at how a campaign performed, not to plan the next overture.

The sales agent can proactively turn that information into raw material for pitches.

“We do the research, we look at historical data and we look at similar advertisers,” Vaidya said. “Then a salesperson can go in and start ideating on new proposals for advertisers that TIME hasn’t even sold to yet.”

But the relationship itself still belongs to the salesperson, Howard said, and TIME isn’t looking to automate that or make it into an agentic process.

“We don’t do it with our journalism,” he said, “and we’re not going to do it with our sales.”

Well, at least, “that isn’t the plan for now,” he added.

Once a deal closes, the work shifts to ad ops, and to Kana’s second agent.

Ad ops teams already have dashboards that show them when a campaign is underdelivering or not pacing properly. What those dashboards don’t say, though, is what to do about it, Vaidya said.

The Kana agent answers that for each campaign and recommends a fix, like changing trafficking, pacing or budget, so a shortfall doesn’t turn into a make-good.

TIME’s revenue operations team, meanwhile, is also using the tool, Howard said, and he’s seen “a significant increase in what they’re able to accomplish in any given day or week.” It’s also helping with budget planning.

“And not just for how we’re going to finish this year and how Q1 looks, but actual 2027 modeling,” Howard said. “It’s a whole new world when you can have these kinds of insights delivered to you.”

Between proposals and pacing, agents now cover most of a deal’s life cycle, so there’s technically nothing stopping a publisher’s agent from dealing directly with an advertiser’s agent while the sales rep heads out for a three-martini lunch.

Kana could build that, Vaidya said. Put an MCP server (a standard way for AI agents to connect to other software) in front of the proposal agent, and it would be able to talk to an advertiser’s agents tomorrow.

“That’s a technology problem, which is easy to solve,” he said.

Selling is still built on relationships, Howard said, and agents doing the deal is “just not in our game plan.”

Why the midterms may not spell doom for ad tech consolidation. Plus, how sports leagues are putting ad revenue before player safety.

Agentic media buying is the No. 1 topic du jour in ad tech discussion circles. But how does agentic buying even work when it comes to buying TV and streaming ads? And how does that workflow differ from programmatic? The startup Swivel aims to address those questions.

AppLovin is growing at a blistering pace, but Wall Street investors remain cautious about the business.

High-end ad blockers mimic some of the same behavior as bot traffic. So WiseGuyGaming repurposed the tools it was already using to identify bots to reveal its ad-blocking audience as well.

Criteo has weathered considerable storms in its day, including a painful moment in March 2020 when its market cap bottomed out below $500 million. Can it pull off yet another turnaround?

Disaffection and outright anger toward US sports institutions run deeper than logo-cluttered fields. Leagues and teams are sacrificing player safety and fan enjoyment for marketing dollars.

AdExchanger is where marketers, agencies, publishers and tech companies go for the latest information on the trends that are transforming digital media and marketing, from data, privacy, identity and AI to commerce, CTV, measurement and mobile.

Why altcoin season faces a reality check after $800B market cap plunge

📅 2026-11-09  |  Read Source →

Read the best crypto stories of the day in less than 5 minutes. Join to get it daily in your inbox.

Read the best crypto stories of the day in less than 5 minutes. Join to get it daily in your inbox.

The altcoin market cap has dropped from $1.85 trillion to $1.05 trillion at press time in just a month. At the same time, the Altcoin Season Index was trading at 44, indicating that the market was still in a Bitcoin [BTC] season.

The chart below clearly shows that the index has repeatedly attempted to move higher but has struggled to sustain readings above 75.

As expected, this has prompted a question: Is altcoin season now off the radar in 2026?

Alphractal analysis shows that around August to October 2026, both Bitcoin’s 60-day return and the median altcoin’s return climbed to roughly +50-60%, with altcoins moving in sync. But, by November, both lines have dropped back to roughly 0%, and the median altcoin sits slightly below Bitcoin.

Simultaneously, the median altcoin vs. Bitcoin chart shows the gap directly, as it was standing at -5.91%. The green spike in October (about +25%) has fully reversed into red, meaning the median altcoin now trails Bitcoin by about 6% in over sixty days.

This shows how the pattern fits earlier cycles, where altcoin outperformance came in short bursts that faded quickly. Currently, altcoins are weaker than Bitcoin but not in deep capitulation.

Remarking on the same, João Wedson, founder and CEO of Alphractal, argued,

Altcoins signal Bitcoin tops with a high degree of probability!

Echoing similar sentiments, Benjamin Cowen, founder & CEO, ITC, added that “Q4 has arrived,” but the price chart he attached suggests a broad red day, with Bitcoin at about $74,875 (-1.19%), but altcoins are falling harder.

Adding more to the ongoing concern, Wealthmanager also called the current market situation a “full capitulation” phase with about $380 billion more selling than buying, claiming it is seven times worse than 2022’s bottom.

At the same time, Bitcoin dominance is stable above 60% at press time, and altcoins not gaining share fits the weak Altcoin Season Index very well.

However, ETF flows on 6th November show net outflows almost everywhere. Starting with BTC ETFs, which lost $1.28 billion, and Ethereum [ETH] ETFs, which recorded $365.28 million, the second-largest outflow.

Lastly, Hyperliquid [HYPE] was hit by $15.62 million in outflows, Solana [SOL] recorded $26.25 million, Avalanche [AVAX] recorded $1.27 million, Chainlink [LINK] saw $1.06 million, and XRP, Litecoin [LTC], and Binance [BNB] each recorded outflows under $1 million.

It was only Hedera [HBAR] that recorded an inflow of $3.01 million. In other words, institutional money is leaving both Bitcoin and altcoins, not rotating from one to the other. This followed a recent report by AMBCrypto, which already indicated at the end of October that altcoin speculation has slowed down.
